#6969. When Does Spotify Wrapped Come Out? Find Out the 2026 Release Date and How to Make Sure You Don’t Miss Yours

It’s nearly Thanksgiving, so Spotify users are expecting their Spotify Wrapped any day now. The campaign—which includes a summary of your listening habits for the year—usually drops around the end of November or the beginning of December, so it can’t be long before it’s released.

So, when will Spotify Wrapped come out this year? How can you make sure that you don't miss yours? And when is the data collected?

Read on to find out everything you need to know about Spotify Wrapped.

The streaming service has yet to confirm an exact date for this year’s Spotify Wrapped, but based on previous years, we can make some educated guesses on when it might be.

Over the years, Wrapped has been released in late November or early December, so we expect it to follow the same pattern for 2026.

Below are all the release dates since Spotify Wrapped first dropped in 2016:

A majority of these dates fell on a Wednesday, so December 2 is looking very promising for 2026.

Spotify Wrapped is an annual campaign provided by the streaming site, giving readers a summary of their listening habits for the past year.

It usually includes five musicians, songs, and musical genres that the user has listened to over the year in a fun graphic that you can then share on social media.

Spotify Wrapped first dropped in 2016, as a rebrand of 2015’s “Year in Music.”

Speaking ahead of the 2024 drop, Spotify’s vice president of partnerships and marketing Marc Hazan said, “What started as a look back at the music and audio that defined the year has become a global cultural phenomenon in its own right, a moment driven by the passion of hundreds of millions of fans. We’re thrilled to debut 2024 Spotify Wrapped to more fans than ever before this year, celebrating the hundreds of millions of ways that they’ve streamed.”

You’ll want to make sure that you have the most updated version of the app so that you can get your Spotify Wrapped for 2026.

To ensure that you're using the newest version of the app, go to the app store on your phone and check for available updates.

With artists like Bad Bunny, Drake, and Taylor Swift predicted to top the 2026 lists due to major album releases and tours, you won’t want to miss your roundup.

Spotify has never confirmed the exact start and end dates for its listening data window for Spotify Wrapped. However, we do know that they start collecting data at the start of January and conclude sometime in November.

What is known is that Spotify begins collecting data at the start of January and concludes sometime in November.

Speaking to Mashable during a Wrapped press presentation in December 2024, the senior director of product Molly Holder explained, “We start at the beginning of January with all of your listening that begins right then at the beginning of the year, and it goes all the way through collecting data up until a few weeks before we launch the Wrapped product.”

This means Spotify likely stops collecting data for its annual Wrapped campaign in early to mid-November, so the music you listen to after Wrapped and before January 1 may not count.

#6966. Report: Cyber Criminals Move Toward More Targeted Phishing Attacks

Cyber criminals are moving away from mass phishing scams and focusing on more targeted attacks, increasingly using trusted websites and services to trick victims, according to a new report from KnowBe4. The security firm found that spear-phishing attacks increased 41% over the past year.

The company's Phishing Threat Trends Report, Vol. 8 found that overall phishing volume increased 29.8% year over year. Organizations received an average of one dangerous e-mail for every 733 messages, but the bigger concern is how attackers are changing their approach.

Rather than sending the same fraudulent message to thousands of people, criminals are spending more time researching their targets and creating convincing messages that appear to come from trusted coworkers, businesses, and technology platforms.

The findings are based on threat data from KnowBe4 Defend, the company's inbound e-mail security platform, which processes approximately 1.15 billion event signals daily.

KnowBe4 found that several traditional phishing methods declined significantly compared with 2025. Mobile-focused scams dropped 47.3%, advance-fee fraud fell 31.3%, and missed-message scams declined 11.3%.

Meanwhile, spear phishing increased 41.1%, pre-attack reconnaissance rose 33.7%, and company impersonation climbed 28%.

The shift suggests that attackers are finding more value in carefully crafted scams that exploit existing business relationships and workplace routines.

Business e-mail compromise (BEC) remains particularly common, accounting for 51.2% of phishing e-mails analyzed in the report. Human resources, finance departments, and executive leadership are among the most frequently targeted groups.

Attackers also appear to be timing their campaigns around normal business activity. KnowBe4 found that 90.4% of phishing messages arrive Monday through Friday, with Monday accounting for the largest share at 20.6%.

One of the report's more troubling findings involves the growing use of legitimate technology platforms to deliver malicious content.

KnowBe4 found that phishing attacks were twice as likely to originate from legitimate infrastructure in the third quarter of 2026 compared with the previous quarter.

That includes AI services, where attackers are taking advantage of trusted domains to host fake developer consoles, login prompts, and credential-stealing pages. The report also identified a 228% increase in AI-brand impersonation attacks between March and August.

Because these malicious pages can operate on legitimate domains with valid security certificates, users may not see the usual warning signs associated with suspicious websites.

"For years, security advice has focused on a predictable checklist: Check the sender address, hover over the link, and look for the padlock," said Jack Chapman, senior vice president of threat intelligence at KnowBe4.

"While still foundational, that guidance is becoming less useful by the day."

The findings echo a recent report about management software and AI e-mail assistants. Research from Fortra and Barracuda showed how criminals are using legitimate administrative tools and hidden AI instructions to manipulate users and compromise systems.

A new study from consulting firm KPMG found that while AI investment continues to grow, relatively few organizations consistently measure whether the technology's benefits justify its costs.

Network-provider trust concerns are impacting enterprise AI plans, with AI and data-driven projects the most affected initiatives among respondents reporting disruption, according to a report from telecommunications provider Arelion.

SMART Technologies has introduced SoundFlow, a plug-and-play broadcasting system that allows classroom audio content to flow directly from a SMART Board display to students' compatible hearing aids, earbuds, headphones, personal listening devices, or speakers.

CrowdStrike's latest Threat Hunting Report traces a multiyear shift from conventional intrusions toward attacks that exploit trusted identities, cloud services, AI systems, and software dependencies.

#6962. Study: Sign-In Problems Are Primary Drivers of Help Desk Tickets

Sign-in problems account for up to 53% of help desk tickets at individual enterprises, but network issues can take significantly longer to resolve, according to a new study from ControlUp.

Analyzing 1,069,110 help desk tickets, the IT operations company identified password resets, account lockouts, and multifactor authentication (MFA) issues as major drivers of support demand.

At one large enterprise, account-related tickets took a median of 11 minutes to resolve, compared with 50 hours for network problems. Meanwhile, 87% of network and phone tickets were reassigned between teams, compared with 20% of account tickets.

MFA enrollment and re-enrollment problems appeared at 40% of organizations, with one in five linking these issues to employees replacing or upgrading their phones. Virtual desktop problems appeared at 55% of organizations analyzed.

Traditional phone support remained a major channel for IT assistance.

Among organizations that recorded how support requests were submitted, 57% reported that phone calls accounted for approximately two-thirds or more of contacts. At one organization, that figure reached 99%.

Machine-generated tickets from monitoring, security, backup, and patching systems appeared at half of the organizations studied. At 15%, automated tickets represented between 25% and 50% of all requests.

The research covered enterprises across retail, healthcare, financial services, manufacturing, construction, energy, and IT services.

ControlUp said the findings highlight the need to evaluate recurring problems and their operational impact rather than relying solely on ticket volume and resolution speed.

"IT organizations have traditionally measured support performance by how quickly they close tickets, but that doesn't tell the whole story," said Simon Townsend, senior vice president of marketing and office of the CTO at ControlUp.

"A password reset that takes a few minutes and an infrastructure failure that disrupts an entire location shouldn't carry the same operational weight."

For more information, go to the ControlUp site.

In an active social engineering campaign, cyber attackers are impersonating IT help desks and using fake passkey setup requests to compromise employee identities and gain access to enterprise cloud data.

A new study from consulting firm KPMG found that while AI investment continues to grow, relatively few organizations consistently measure whether the technology's benefits justify its costs.

Cybercriminals are moving away from mass phishing scams and focusing on more targeted attacks, often using trusted websites and services to trick victims, according to a new report from KnowBe4.

AI is moving beyond assisting cybercriminals and beginning to operate within live attacks, according to a recent report from Check Point Research. The technology is making sophisticated capabilities faster and more accessible while creating new security risks for businesses deploying their own AI systems.

#6961. 30 Breakup Texts to Send Instead of Ghosting Someone

Figuring out how to tell someone you're not interested shouldn't be harder than the actual breakup.

You went on a date. They were perfectly nice, you had a decent time, and you even said you'd do it again. Except now they're texting you about Friday, and you're realizing you'd actually rather spend the evening doing literally anything else. So you consider taking the path of least resistance: simply never responding again. No awkward conversation, no hurt feelings (at least on your end), and no need to figure out how to tell someone you're just not that into them.

Unfortunately, there's a reason sending a breakup text feels harder than disappearing. “We ghost because we would rather avoid the interaction completely,” explains Kayden Kross, founder of Gasplighting and author of Deeper. And while ghosting might save you an uncomfortable interaction, it leaves the other person wondering whether you're busy, uninterested, or just never planning to text them back.

To be clear, we're talking about casual dating and early-stage connections here, not ending a years-long relationship with a two-sentence text. And if someone makes you feel unsafe or repeatedly disrespects your boundaries, you don't owe them an explanation.

But if the only thing stopping you from ending things is that you don't know what to say, we've got you. From the person who won't stop asking for a second date to the one who only texts after midnight, here are 30 breakup texts to send instead of ghosting.

Short answer: Yes, but context matters. If you've only been on a date or two, there's no need to schedule an in-person breakup conversation with someone you're still getting to know. “If the majority of the interaction has been by text or DM then ending it by text is within bounds,” says Kross.

But if you've been seeing someone for months, have developed real feelings, or are in an established relationship, a phone call or face-to-face conversation is generally more considerate. The more time and emotional investment involved, the more care the breakup deserves.

Of course, there's one exception to any breakup etiquette rule: your safety. If someone has made you feel unsafe, threatened you, or repeatedly ignored your boundaries, you don't need to meet up, get on a call, or even respond just to be polite.

The best breakup texts are short, honest, and leave very little room for interpretation. You don't need to write a five-paragraph essay about why you're not feeling it, but you do need to make it clear that you're ending things. “It’s more about being clear that we’re not interested in continuing so the other side isn’t left in a position of uncertainty about what’s going on,” Kross explains.

One easy approach? Acknowledge the time you've spent together, briefly explain that you don't see things moving forward, and wish them well. No need to list everything you didn't like about them or invent an excuse to soften the blow.

And whatever you do, don't leave the door open if you know it's closed. “To me, the most painful thing in relationships is false hope,” says Kross. Skip the “maybe someday” and “let's stay friends” unless you genuinely mean them. Being clear might feel uncomfortable now, but it's a lot kinder than keeping someone guessing.

Seriously. If someone has made you feel unsafe, harassed you, or continued contacting you after you've asked them to stop, you don't owe them another explanation. Protecting your peace and safety is more important than following breakup etiquette.

#6959. The Design Fiend’s Guide to Tangier

The Moroccan city, a complex, culturally kaleidoscopic crossroads between Europe and Africa, is home to a savvy community of creatives whose stylish impact continues to grow. Word is out.

Above: The pool terrace at Villa Mabrouka, a 12-room hotel that was once the home of Yves Saint Laurent and Pierre Bergé, with gardens designed by Madison Cox.

Writing about Tangier in the 1950s, Truman Capote observed that “the days slide by less noticed than foam in a waterfall.” Decades later, this city on Morocco’s coast, across the Strait of Gibraltar from Spain, is significantly changed from Capote’s time, when it was an exotic retreat for café society’s demimonde, people such as Barbara Hutton and Malcolm Forbes. In recent years Tangier’s mosaic of villages—from the medina’s warren of whitewashed lanes to the Ville Moderne’s blocks of Art Deco towers to the hillside villas and gardens on Jebel Kebir—has been reinvigorated, in part by an enterprising mix of Tanjawis (the locals) and expats who continue to infuse the city with a fresh creative spirit.

Yet there is much that remains the same: the dreamy light that seduced artists from Eugène Delacroix to Henri Matisse to Mariano Fortuny, the overgrown gardens of casbah courtyards, and the cooling breezes coming off the confluence of Atlantic and Mediterranean waters. And there’s still plenty of the languorous lifestyle that captivated Capote.

“Tangier is all about settling in,” says Charlotte Claydon, whose whitewashed Moroccan Art Deco house has been in her family for more than a century. Known as Dar Sinclair, the residence sits in a grove of papyrus and lilies between thick palms and weeping willows. It’s one of numerous historic villas and townhouses renovated by international designers and other creatives who live here that can be rented. There’s arguably no better way to ease into the rhythms of daily life in Tangier. Pin Affleck, who arranges travel and accommodations through the Tangier Bureau, says that “going into a private house, you get the sense of being inside Tangier.”

When Claydon and her sister Jacquetta Wheeler began renting out Dar Sinclair, they received messages from guests worried about filling up a week. “We spent hours coming up with lists of activities, knowing full well they might only get to one or two,” Claydon says, noting that most guests make their own discoveries and end up on unplanned diversions.

Among the sisters’ can’t-miss activities: tea at Café de Paris, Sunday service at historic St. Andrew’s Church, and a camel ride, especially with kids, at Achakar Beach. A typical morning in Tangier might begin with breakfast under a banyan tree at El Morocco Club. Around 11 a.m. the markets buzz to life, including the legendary fish market in the Grand Socco, the Fondouk Chejra (weavers’ market), the antiques mall on Rue de Hollande, and the Casabarata flea market, where a sharp eye is required to spot the gems amid the seedy furniture and cheap fabrics.

Lunch in town at Alma Kitchen or El Dorado is a must for crispy falafel and plates of lamb chops. Later, as the sun sinks into the sea, it’s hard to beat the views from the roof of Villa Mabrouka, the onetime home of Yves Saint Laurent and Pierre Bergé that Jasper Conran transformed into hotel with gardens by Tangier resident Madison Cox. After a martini, the hotel’s L’Orangerie restaurant is a smart spot for a dinner of thistle tagine and grilled sole; down the street, the atmospheric Hamadi serves standout harissa soup and sizzling skewers.

Claydon and Wheeler, for their part, also advise guests to reserve time for relaxing and being transported at home, one of the great pleasures of staying in a design-centric private residence. Other places available to rent include the red-and-white-striped riad owned by decorator Gavin Houghton and photographer Boz Gagovski (who took most of the photos in this story), as well as antiques dealer Gordon Watson’s large ochre house. Both weave together Moorish and European details, with objects from Tangier and around the world that reflect the city’s cosmopolitan history. As Hamish Bowles noted in his foreword to the book Inside Tangier, “The magic of this place was that everyone who had chosen to make a home here brought their own fantasy with them.”

That book was produced by my husband, designer Nicolò Castellini Baldissera, and our home here, a 1929 townhouse named Casa Tosca, is filled with a worldly mix of objects and furnishings that we designed. Outside, our terraced garden was overseen by Umberto Pasti, whose own Tangier residence is a collection of 1930s bungalows set within meandering gardens of datura trees, rambling roses, and alocasia. For us, so much of the pleasure of this place is being at home and having people over. Creating the setting with local craftspeople and market finds becomes a pastime in itself.

It’s the same for Marco Scarani and Jamie Creel, whose house, Dar Zero, has had an illustrious lineage of owners who have contributed to its rich layers. The home is in the casbah, with a bushy fig tree and bougainvillea spilling from its courtyard over the front wall. Inside, iron klismos chairs from when designers Yves Vidal and Charles Sevigny lived there sit on well-worn bejmat and zellige tiles. There are also pieces of their own creation, such as brass bird-foot candlesticks they make in Tangier and sell in their New York shop, Creel & Gow. “There is a lot of transmission from one generation to another,” Scarani says. “Friends here inherit objects and even homes. We live in houses that have the spirits of the people who came before.”

For designers Frank de Biasi and Gene Meyer, it was the relationship with artisans that prompted them a few years ago to open Habibi Burton, their boutique where they offer pillows, painted tables, and a rotation of decorative objects, all sourced locally. “Making things for the shop gives me more freedom than making things for clients,” says de Biasi. “Because we aren’t designing to a deadline, I can have four artisans contribute to a single piece.” He also recommends Las Chicas for fashion, Kasbah Collective for regional crafts, and the bookshop Les Insolites.

Down the street, artist and curator Hicham Bouzid oversees Kiosk, a cultural salon and shop that hosts talks and exhibitions bringing together creatives from Africa, the Arab world, and Europe. It has a selection of books and artist prints, as well as a restaurant and live music. Bouzid is one of the native Tanjawis who are animating the city’s cultural life. Another is Yto Barrada, perhaps Morocco’s best-known contemporary artist, who founded the popular Cinéma Rif, where screenings of contemporary and classic films share the program with talks and other cultural events.

Across town Clos Nadir, the house shared by designer Jean-Louis Deniot and William Holloway, exists in a different world, perched among pines and looking out across the strait. Focusing on spaces for entertaining, Deniot placed a 30-foot dining table on a roof, while his living room is outfitted with a large fireplace, plush carpets, and a welcoming mix of furniture, mostly made locally. “Tangier is a land of artistic opportunity, a place for aesthetes, where stylistic adventures, contemplation, and the freedom to create are central to daily life,” says the ELLE Decor A-List designer, who is opening an office here.

Tangier’s close-knit community of creatives comes alive during the summer, which is when Veere Greeney, another ELLE Decor A-List designer, hosts an annual theatrical performance in the gardens of his mountaintop villa, Gazebo. Producer Rob Ashford assembles actors from Broadway and the West End (last summer it was Dames at Sea, featuring Darren Criss and Julianne Hough), and the shows are open to the public, though tickets are limited. It’s a full sensory experience colored by the scent of jasmine and gardenias, one that captures the eccentric artistic allure of Tangier.

The energy and interest in the city continue to grow. Deniot is creating a hotel with theater producer Jordan Roth, the Four Seasons recently announced a new property on Malabata Bay, and King Mohammed VI is behind a luxurious renovation of the historic Hotel Continental.

And while there is excitement around these new projects, what appeals most about Tangier is something older. Over lunch on Grenney’s terrace, looking out at Spain, we discussed the city’s past lives. “What makes Tangier unique is its varied history and strategic position,” he observed. “The strait made it a city of diplomacy, openness, and inclusiveness. That is its bedrock.”

This story originally appeared in the November 2026 issue of ELLE Decor.

#6957. Enterprise AI Spending Outpaces Adoption Growth, Report Finds

Enterprise AI spending is growing faster than the number of organizations paying for it, according to a new Menlo Ventures report that points to a shift from buying access for employees to financing work performed by agents.

The venture capital firm's 2026 State of Enterprise AI report estimates deployment spending reached a $176.8 billion annualized run rate by September, up from $33 billion in 2025. The share of enterprises paying for AI rose from 55% to 65%.

A larger bill can reflect broader use inside an organization, but it does not independently establish that the additional work is useful or profitable.

Menlo's methodology describes revenue figures as annualized run rates. The $176.8 billion estimate therefore should not be read as $176.8 billion already spent during the first nine months of 2026.

The report also distinguishes unusually heavy spenders from the typical respondent. Average reported spending reached 4.7 times the prior year's level, while the median reached 2.2 times. That gap suggests a smaller group of large spenders drove the average substantially.

The practical implication is that an industry growth figure may be a poor benchmark for an individual organization's budget. Buyers need to understand their own workload growth and the results it produces.

"Agents are changing the economics of software," Menlo principal Derek Xiao said in the firm's announcement.

Menlo reports that agents account for 80% of tokens on OpenRouter, a service providing access to multiple models. That measures activity on one platform, rather than all enterprise AI use. Token consumption also measures processing volume, rather than completed business outcomes.

An agent that makes repeated attempts can consume more resources without delivering proportionately more value. For procurement teams, the relevant comparison is consequently the cost of completing acceptable work, including the review it requires.

Menlo estimates that Anthropic, OpenAI, and Google capture 69% of enterprise AI deployment spending. That measure is distinct from their shares of enterprise language-model API spending, a narrower category. The figures should not be used interchangeably.

The report draws on a Morning Consult survey of nearly 400 U.S. enterprise decision-makers responsible for more than $4 billion in AI spending. Market totals are modeled estimates informed by surveys, public filings, and third-party reports. Menlo discloses investments in Anthropic and OpenRouter.

A separate announcement from Information Services Group describes buyers putting more emphasis on the economics and reliability of production deployments. ISG says ongoing security and observability work remains necessary as organizations scale.

That provides a useful distinction between market demand and business value. Suppliers can generate growing revenue while customers still determine which deployments justify the cost.

For enterprise leaders, the new spending estimate strengthens the case for examining usage beyond license counts. It leaves the harder purchasing question with the customer: How much worthwhile work did the increased spending actually buy?

Menlo's State of Enterprise AI report is available here.

#6955. Are Zen Hotels Now Putting Us to Sleep?

Rich, layered maximalism is the latest form of escapism—historically inspired decor spiked with creative contemporary flair. Get ready to Instagram every nook.

Above: One of the cottages at Little Saint in Healdsburg, CA.

On the top floor of a Gilded Age McKim, Mead & White mansion in New York, now reincarnated as the Fifth Avenue Hotel, Martin Brudnizki recently put the finishing touches on a two-bedroom suite dubbed the Flâneur Penthouse. With his hallmark exuberance, the Elle Decor A-List designer arrayed the space with distinctive flourishes: a spirited Scalamandré wallcovering trimmed in grosgrain ribbon, bespoke furnishings mixed with French Art Deco pieces, India-inspired inlaid nightstands, Japanese-style lamps, and Murano glass ceiling lights. Each element connects to an imagined narrative that shaped Brudnizki’s vision for the hotel: “this cinematic idea of the home of a flâneur who has traveled the world, has seen and acquired a lot, and put it together here,” the designer says. “It’s a multicultural journey.”

Hotels in this vein tend to be -owner-operated properties looking to carve out a niche. One could trace a baroque family tree that links mold-breaking 1980s boutique hotels like Ian Schrager and Philippe Starck’s Royalton in New York to Jacques Garcia’s interiors for the Hôtel Costes and L’Hotel in Paris to Ilse Crawford’s Ett Hem in Stockholm to Kelly Wearstler’s Proper Hotel in Austin.

Fulk’s hospitality design, like Brudnizki’s, embraces spaces inspired by history and storytelling, with an emphasis on artistry, material richness, and character. Both men’s work is rooted in tradition, with idiosyncratic twists that feel fresh. And they have plenty of kindred spirits working in a diversity of styles, including such designers as Lorenzo Castillo, Kit Kemp, and Laura Gonzalez. While far from a coherent movement, the sumptuous, high-impact aesthetics practiced by these designers just might be today’s most zeitgeisty current.

Take Fulk’s Cottages at Little Saint in Healdsburg, California, each one an elevated boho mashup of influences. “Sixties and ’70s rock and roll, and a sort of Laurel Canyon meets Northern California spirit,” explains the designer. “The cottages feel utterly like nowhere else.” And that, of course, is the point.

For his part, Brudnizki doesn’t care much for maximalism as a label. In the case of his glamorously moody interiors for the Broadwick Soho in London—shimmering surfaces, lavishly upholstered furnishings, a riot of pattern—everything began with an idea he describes as “Studio 54 meets your grandmother’s Soho townhouse, a disco granny kind of thing.”

Then there are the roughly dozen memorable properties created by Kit and Tim Kemp under their Firmdale Hotels group. The most recent is the Warren Street Hotel in New York, with the forthcoming Bedford Place Hotel slated to open next year in London next to the British Museum. “It’s about personality, a sense of arrival, and fun,” says Kit Kemp, who outfits each of her hotels with a more-is-more mix of her ebullient textiles and wallpapers in splashy colors, animated by art and craft.

Kimberley Cohen, the creative director behind Maisons Pariente, says her family’s small portfolio of hôtels de charme are about creating a residential-feeling experience that is also “something you have never seen, that you would not do in your own home.” She has worked with Brudnizki on Le Grand Mazarin in Paris, with Charles Zana on two hotels in the South of France, and with Pierre Yovanovitch on a Méribel chalet. Her latest project, opening this winter, is a Courchevel retreat named Saint Roch, with interiors by Friedmann & Versace that channel the chic romanticism of traditional dachas.

Next up for Brudnizki, meanwhile, is the Knox Hotel, set to open soon in Dallas, new territory for him. As for what one should expect of the decor, he sums it up as “100 percent unapologetic glamour. It won’t be boring.”

This story originally appeared in the November 2026 issue of ELLE Decor.

He was previously the consulting features editor for Galerie and held top positions at Architectural Digest and Departures. #6953. Phishing Threats Totaled 7.3 Billion in Q3 2026

E-mail phishing threats reached 7.3 billion in Q3 2026, with September activity increasing 41% from August, according to Microsoft Threat Intelligence.

According to a report from Microsoft Threat Intelligence and the Microsoft Defender Security Research Team, phishing detections reached nearly 3 billion in September alone.

The findings also highlighted changes in attacker techniques, including business-service impersonation, credential theft, and social engineering through Microsoft Teams.

The Redmond-based firm recognized approximately 129 million malicious messages spoofing the sender domains of tracked business services during Q3.

DocuSign was the most frequently spoofed brand in September, accounting for 29% of the month's tracked spoofing activity. Adobe, SurveyMonkey, and Barracuda also experienced increases toward the end of the quarter.

The research found that some malicious messages were delivered despite failing e-mail authentication checks and being identified as threats.

In September, tenant- or user-level security overrides applied to 63% of these messages with a recorded security outcome — most involved tenant-level exceptions.

Microsoft warned that overly broad exceptions intended to support legitimate business communications can weaken protection against impersonation attacks.

The company emphasized that these findings do not establish whether recipients opened the messages or whether accounts were compromised.

Microsoft also recorded approximately 141,000 malicious Teams call attempts during Q3.

Attackers increasingly used ordinary personal names instead of explicitly impersonating IT support personnel. Such display names accounted for 46% of an observed sample in July, rising to 88% in September.

Meanwhile, QR code phishing declined 67% compared to Q2, totaling roughly 12.1 million attacks.

CAPTCHA-gated phishing activity increased toward the end of the quarter, reaching 3.1 million detected attacks in September. SVG attachments played a significant role in the increase.

Credential theft remained the primary objective of malicious attachment campaigns, with approximately 94% to 96% of payload-based attacks directing users to phishing sites or fraudulent sign-in pages.

Microsoft recommended reviewing Exchange Online Protection and Defender for Office 365 configurations, enabling Safe Links and Safe Attachments, and strengthening authentication protections.

The company also advised organizations to review security exceptions and verify the identities of external Teams contacts before responding to suspicious requests.

For more information, go to the Microsoft blog post.

A new study from consulting firm KPMG found that while AI investment continues to grow, relatively few organizations consistently measure whether the technology's benefits justify its costs.

Network-provider trust concerns are impacting enterprise AI plans, with AI and data-driven projects the most affected initiatives among respondents reporting disruption, according to a report from telecommunications provider Arelion.

SMART Technologies has introduced SoundFlow, a plug-and-play broadcasting system that allows classroom audio content to flow directly from a SMART Board display to students' compatible hearing aids, earbuds, headphones, personal listening devices, or speakers.

CrowdStrike's latest Threat Hunting Report traces a multiyear shift from conventional intrusions toward attacks that exploit trusted identities, cloud services, AI systems, and software dependencies.

#6952. What Is Unrequited Love? Experts Explain Why It Hurts So Much and How to Move On

Because knowing someone doesn't feel the same way doesn't magically make your feelings disappear.

There's nothing quite like having feelings for someone who doesn't feel the same way. Maybe it's a friend you've secretly been into for years, an ex you can't seem to get over, or someone who keeps giving you just enough attention to convince yourself there's still a chance. And while you know, logically, that you can't make someone fall in love with you, that doesn't stop you from overanalyzing every text, replaying every interaction, and wondering whether things might eventually change.

Welcome to the deeply frustrating world of unrequited love. “At its core, unrequited love is a love that’s not reciprocated from one person to another,” explains Leanna Stockard, LMFT, a marriage and family therapist with LifeStance Health. “One person either has stronger feelings for the other or there is no feeling at all from the other.”

And just because your feelings aren't mutual doesn't mean they're any less real or painful. In fact, clinical psychologist Monica Vermani, PsyD, says unrequited love can cause “tremendous emotional turmoil,” leaving you feeling anxious, stressed, or questioning your self-worth.

So, how can you tell if you're experiencing unrequited love versus a particularly intense crush? And how do you move on when part of you is still holding out hope? Here's what experts want you to know about recognizing unrequited love, understanding why it happens, and figuring out what to do next.

Essentially, unrequited love looks like an imbalance of effort, affection, and attention. Stockard says some signs your feelings might not be reciprocated include the other person consistently taking a long time to respond to your texts or calls, avoiding spending time with you, or, in some cases, not even knowing who you are.

Unrequited love often happens between friends, when one person starts developing romantic feelings while the other sees the relationship as strictly platonic. It can also happen when you fall for someone who's unavailable, whether that's someone already in a relationship, an ex, a person who lives far away, an authority figure, or even a celebrity.

If you're wondering whether your feelings are one-sided, here are some signs Vermani says to look out for:

Vermani says unrequited love can sometimes have more to do with our own expectations and unmet emotional needs than the person we're actually falling for.

“Unrequited love is often based on pursuing from a romantic partner what is lacking in our own lives,” she explains. “The reality of this quest for love as a means of filling in the gaps or deficits in our lives (and ourselves) is that it comes from a place of lack. When we set our sights on an ‘ideal’ partner who ticks all the boxes on our must-have list, we often create the ideal conditions for unrequited love.”

Think about it: When you're convinced someone is your perfect match, it's easy to start imagining what your life together could look like, even if the relationship itself doesn't actually exist. You might find yourself focusing on their best qualities, overlooking signs they're not interested, or holding onto the idea that everything would fall into place if they just felt the same way. And sometimes, that imagined relationship can feel easier to hold onto than dealing with the uncertainty and vulnerability of a real one.

“Many of us are brought up to believe that to be happy and fulfilled in life, we need someone—the one—to complete us,” Vermani says. “The main reason we suffer, though, is that we don’t accept people as they are, or accept situations as they are.”

Of course, it's also possible to develop strong feelings for someone without knowing them on a deeper level. You might know their favorite food, birthday, and practically every detail of their Instagram activity, but that doesn't necessarily mean you know what they'd be like as a romantic partner. And even if you're close friends, the intimacy you share isn't necessarily the same as a mutual romantic connection.

The short answer? Your feelings can absolutely be real, even if they're not reciprocated. But there's a difference between loving someone and being in a loving relationship with them. Sometimes, what feels like love can also be a combination of intense longing, attraction, and the idea of what a relationship with that person could look like. And if you don't know them on a deeper level, you might be falling for a version of them that doesn't entirely match reality.

“When love is mutual, there’s balance, give and take, shared intimacy, trust, mutual transparency (meaning a lack of secrecy), and commitment that grows over time,” explains Vermani.

Unrequited love is a pretty common experience, especially when you're still figuring out what traits you value in a potential partner. And having feelings for someone who doesn't feel the same way isn't inherently unhealthy. It becomes more concerning when those feelings start affecting your well-being, preventing you from pursuing other relationships, or making it difficult to respect the other person's boundaries.

“Being in a one-sided relationship may lead to further feelings of rejection and increased anxiety,” Stockard says. And while it's normal to have a hard time letting go, continually holding out hope for a relationship that isn't going to happen can make moving forward even harder. “Unrequited love can be dangerous when a person takes their love to a level such as stalking, manipulation, or other dangerous forms of obsession,” she adds.

That said, not every case of unrequited love turns into something unhealthy. It's entirely possible to care about someone, accept that they don't feel the same way, and eventually move forward without those feelings interfering with your life or theirs.

Just because someone doesn't return your feelings doesn't mean you can magically turn yours off. But if the situation is causing you anxiety or heartbreak, finding ways to refocus your energy can help. After all, continuing to pursue someone who's made it clear they're not interested is only going to make moving on harder.

Stockard says there are a few different approaches you can try, starting with giving yourself some space. “Determine if you need time away from the other person to gain clarity, and respect their needs as well,” she suggests. “As much as your feelings for them are valid, their feelings of not wanting to pursue something further with you are also valid.” That might mean resisting the urge to text them, making plans that don't revolve around them, or limiting contact until your feelings have lessened or gone away.

If you're still struggling, Stockard also recommends seeking therapy. “This process can help you feel confident that a partner who values you and wants you exists, and lessen the fixation on someone who does not,” she explains. Therapy can also help you better understand your relationship patterns and what you're looking for in a partner.

And while it might not feel like it right now, time and perspective can make a difference. “Often unrequited love is a fantasy—an intense, entirely one-sided romantic infatuation—rather than actual love. Over time, infatuations fade and neutralize,” Vermani says. “It’s important to focus on self-love, self-care, and self-fulfillment, because when we’re content with ourselves, we’re able to walk away from unrequited love that’s not in our highest and best interest. Once that happens, we’re able to recognize and find reciprocal love.”

You don't have to stop caring about someone overnight. But you can start making choices that leave room for a relationship where your feelings are actually returned.

#6945. The Slow Fade Might Actually Be Worse Than Ghosting

When someone's texts get shorter, plans become nonexistent, and you're left wondering what the hell happened.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

No one likes being rejected—unless, of course, it’s some kind of kink...in which case, more power to you. But that one-two punch to the gut is only made worse when the person you’re dating doesn’t even give you the common courtesy of honest communication.

And while I’m not going to sit here and advocate for ghosting either—in fact, quite the opposite! We’re all about the anti-ghosting movement—at least the silence provides an answer of sorts, unlike the mind fuck that is the slow fade (aka the fizzle). Unless you’re in a happy, stable relationship (heard of those?) and not dealing with the same complex dating landscape as the rest of us, you’ve likely endured this form of emotional warfare at least once. The texts come fewer and farther between, they’ve stopped initiating plans, and their responses are as stale as week-old bread. It’s a pattern of mixed communication that can lead to a lot of second-guessing.

And though we may hate to admit it to ourselves (a little delusion is fine, right? Don’t tell my therapist I said that), your situationship/casual fling/almost significant other is probably not “drowning in work” or whatever other half-assed excuse they gave you last. It’s more likely they’re just not that into you and don’t have the courage, respect, and ahem, balls to admit it.

And while you—and I, and literally any living, breathing human being—deserve a whole lot more than some emotionally incompetent clown who resorts to this form of immaturity, that doesn’t mean the slow fade isn’t hurtful and difficult to move on from. Without a concrete breakup or explanation as to what went wrong, there’s a lot of room for confusion.

“It’s much more brutal for the person on the receiving end because it leaves them with mixed signals. Rather than having information outright, they’re left to wonder what the other person is thinking, which can cause stress and anxiety and prevent them from moving on,” says licensed marriage and family therapist Emily Simonian, head of clinical learning for Thriveworks.

It’s no small epidemic either. Ghosting’s passive-aggressive cousin (as Hinge dubbed it) has become a familiar post-date communication issue for singles. According to the app’s 2023 LGBTQIA+ DATE Report, 90 percent of those surveyed said they were worried about someone “fizzling,” or slow-fading them, and it’s not just an issue in the LGBTQIA+ community. Another study by Superdrug Online Doctor found that 63 percent of participants admitted to slow-fading someone themselves. Cool cool cool.

“It’s become rampant in dating culture in response to the idea that ghosting is inappropriate or lacks compassion,” Simonian says. “However, the same individuals who might ghost due to lack of communication skills have just found a new way to avoid having difficult conversations while distancing themselves from the negative associations of ghosting.”

So while, yay, good, everyone’s finally getting the message that ghosting is not okay, the slow fade is no better. In fact, some (cough, cough me and the actual licensed therapist here) might even call it worse. It breeds confusion and, as Simonian puts it, “perpetuates the trend of avoiding difficult yet honest conversations in the dating space.” And having those difficult but honest conversations is a valuable skill you’ll need in order to make a relationship work when you’re ready. If you're the slow-fader, these negative communication habits won't exactly serve you in the long run. And they can be really destructive for the person on the receiving end, since being strung along and fed mixed messages can inspire distrust with future partners.

So what do we do to survive this hurtful dating pattern—other than the occasional, healthy cry and regular therapy? Well, for starters, if you’ve participated in the slow fade (or “fizzling,” as it’s also commonly referred to) in the past, it’s time to try a different—ahem, more mature—strategy when ending things with someone.

“The best approach is always compassionate honesty,” Simonian says. “Lead with something positive when you’re having these tough conversations, but be direct. It might be harder to let someone down directly, but it is the most compassionate way to end things.”

TL;DR: Be kind and be honest, because choosing anything but that approach is only going to make things more difficult for all parties involved. And while you can’t control another person’s actions—and as a result, you also can’t necessarily avoid the dreaded slow fade from happening to you—you can be accountable for not creating that confusion on your own end.

If your situationship is too chicken to have a real convo and is instead offering lame one-word replies, you can initiate a chat instead of just enduring (read: crying into a pint of Ben & Jerry’s) the energy shift.

“There isn’t really a way to avoid getting slow-faded,” Simonian adds. “However, you can take control of the situation and ask directly whether or not the person wants to continue dating to avoid being left in limbo.”

In other words, use the maturity they couldn’t muster to pull yourself out of the gray area. You deserve clarity, and they deserve to stew in their own discomfort for attempting to slow-fade you in the first place. Hey, a good ol’ fashioned dumping is timeless. Let’s bring that back and stop with this immature, indirect approach.

#6942. Wondering How Sasha Farber Feels About Getting Blocked First on ‘The Circle’? I Flew to Atlanta to Find Out (Exclusive)

The DWTS legend tells us what he would’ve done differently on The Circle—and why he’s scared to call his parents.

Getting blocked first was not the plan. Standing in Sasha Farber’s temporary home on November 18, hours before his blocking on Hulu’s The Circle is revealed to the public, the former Dancing With the Stars pro is still in a state of disbelief. “Never in a million years did I think I was going to come on and be off first,” he tells me from his couch, where—as he threatens several times—he plans on staying until production forces him out. “On Dancing With the Stars, I think the earliest I’ve ever been eliminated was week seven.”

During his 12-season run as a pro, he’d actually been eliminated twice during week six (once with Mary Lou Retton and again with Jenn Tran), but reminding him of this would only add insult to injury. Because not only did he get eliminated first—he got blocked by DWTS superfan Alyssa Khan. Thankfully, he doesn’t hold it against her...though I’m not sure the same could be said for her fellow influencer Justin Hinsley.

Does he have any regrets? “I didn’t get to chat with the fellas, the guys. I wish I got to have a chance to speak to the guys,” he tells me. “I only spoke to Hannah [Brown] and Alyssa, and Hannah I already worked with [on DWTS]. I just wish I had that chance to build a bond with the fellas.”

But as far as how he presented himself: “I’m like, what would I do different? I don’t think I would do anything [different]. I couldn’t do anything different.”

I ask him about the first thing he’s going to do once he gets his phone back—and surprisingly, it’s not check social media (or find out who went home during the latest episode of Dancing With the Stars). “Calling mom and dad,” he says. “Oh god, I’m so scared to tell them I got eliminated.”

He adds, “People were like, ‘Oh, it’s probably going to be really hard to get offline and be in a room.’ But, I mean, look at this place. I was having the time of my life. I feel like the kitchen turned into my dance floor. I was having so much fun.”

At this point, Sasha doesn’t know yet who’s real and who’s a catfish, but when I ask him if he has any suspicions, he immediately points to Lisa Vanderpump (who, as viewers know, is being played by Vanderpump Villa star Dom DeAngelis).

“I know Lisa very well, and she doesn’t say ‘bloody hell’ very often,” he explains. “That’s like me saying, ‘Hey, guys, welcome to the Circle. Let’s put another shrimp on the barbie.’ So I was like, okay, I know Lisa is a catfish. I’m just gonna put that in my back pocket and use it when I need to. But I didn’t have a chance to use it.”

The good news? At least we’ll see Sasha in the ballroom for the Dancing With the Stars finale next week.

As he jokes, “There was so much support for me, and now I’m gonna be there for the finals, like, ‘I’m back, guys.’ You should be happy for that. The whole team is gonna be there and they’ll be like, ‘Wait, I thought you were [on The Circle],’ but it really is, like, how this show goes. You can’t really anticipate anything, which is which makes it really exciting. I don’t think there’s any show like it.”

#6941. Kate Middleton and Prince William Begin Their First Overseas Tour in Four Years

The couple returns to India for the Earthshot Prize, which awards £1 million each to five environmental initiatives.

Kate Middleton and Prince William have arrived in India for a three-day official visit, their first joint overseas tour since 2022. Announced by Kensington Palace over the weekend, the November 16–18 trip is in honor of the sixth annual Earthshot Prize awards in Mumbai. It also marks a significant step in the Princess of Wales’s return to public duties following her 2024 cancer diagnosis.

The couple will attend the awards ceremony on Tuesday, November 17, when five environmental initiatives will each receive £1 million to expand their work. Founded by Prince William in 2020, the prize recognizes solutions across five areas, including restoring nature, reducing waste, and addressing climate change. Mumbai is the awards’ first Indian host city, following past editions in London, Boston, Singapore, Cape Town, and Rio de Janeiro.

The royal itinerary extends beyond the ceremony. Kensington Palace said the visit would highlight sustainable urban transportation, responsible manufacturing, youth-led environmental projects, and India’s creative industries. Today, the couple joined cricket great Sachin Tendulkar and local children for a game of street cricket in Ballard Estate, a historic neighborhood in South Mumbai. The Prince and Princess of Wales are also scheduled to attend the inaugural Children’s Earthshot Awards, recognizing environmental solutions developed by young people ages 9 to 17.

The trip is Catherine’s first overseas tour with William since she disclosed her cancer diagnosis in 2024, and her first appearance at an Earthshot ceremony since the Boston awards in 2022. It is also the couple’s second official visit to India, a decade after their April 2016 tour.

#6936. Why We Need to Stop Saying “I’m Sorry” When Someone Breaks Up

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

When my last relationship ended, I felt better than I had in months—maybe even years. If I’m being honest, I was probably happier after we split than I was when we first got together, and it made me feel like the heartless Bachelor villain of my own love life.

It’s not like I’d planned or even expected this post-breakup bliss. Like literally every other person who has ever been in a relationship, I wasn’t exactly looking forward to the end of mine. But when it finally happened, I was relieved—dare I say, excited? It wasn’t even like I had been actively miserable in the relationship; to this day, I’m not even sure whether it’s fair to say it exceeded standard toxicity levels. But I did know it had run its course, and I was grateful to have my future back, one I wouldn’t have to spend trying to convince myself a good-enough relationship was, in fact, good enough.

But to the rest of the world, this largely painless ending appeared to be a tragedy. Anyone I mentioned the split to, from friends and family to random strangers and acquaintances, immediately responded with some version of “I’m sorry.” In response, I’d find myself explaining that actually I was fine and the breakup was very much for the best. Unfortunately, this had a way of making me come off like a heartbroken caricature of a delusional woman desperately trying to convince everyone she’s fine when really I…actually was fine!

Being on the receiving end of all these condolences had me wondering if I actually was delusional—or maybe just dead inside. Had one too many heartbreaks, ghostings, and life-altering situationships rendered me fully incapable of mourning my own relationship the way everyone else seemed to be doing?

Exhausted from absorbing all this well-intentioned, if unsolicited, sympathy, I started trying to avoid mentioning the breakup altogether. Until one day, a few weeks after the split, when I casually broke the news to a friend at a party and she said, “Congratulations!”

I was so thrilled, so relieved to finally get a response from someone that actually matched my own feelings, I could’ve wept tears of actual joy. Maybe my lack of grief and I weren’t the problem! Maybe the problem I kept brushing up against with every unsolicited “sorry” had more to do with the patriarchal ideals our society still upholds but which I had recently rejected—the ones that dictate that being partnered, however happily or unhappily, is always superior to being single, especially if you’re a woman.

To be clear, I’m not saying that expressing sympathy for what may very well be an emotionally painful experience makes anyone rude or careless or insufficiently evolved. But the default “I’m sorry” most of us reach for when confronted with breakup news paints the dissolution of a relationship as a universal loss, reinforcing the dated idea that being partnered is paradise and being single is sad.

“There are a lot of societal, cultural, or even personal projections made when we automatically assume someone is devastated after a breakup,” explains therapist Lindsey Brock, founder of the Breakup Therapist. “It’s never helpful to assume what someone is thinking or how they are responding emotionally to any situation.”

And sometimes, the breakup is actually good news. “It’s natural for people to say ‘I’m sorry’ because they associate breakups with heartache and want to express sympathy,” says Shannon Lundgren, founder of the matchmaking service Shannon's Circle and author of the upcoming book The Love Funnel. “It’s not the most helpful response because it assumes the person is heartbroken, when they may feel relieved or happy about the ending.”

Perhaps more important than whatever heteropatriarchal norms we may or may not be reinforcing when we apologize to someone for their own breakup is the simple fact that “I’m sorry” might not be an appropriate response to a given situation. This default apology ignores the reality that, in many cases, a breakup might be totally neutral, or even something to celebrate.

“Walking away from a relationship may be the most courageous yet difficult thing someone does,” adds Brock. “Meeting them with an ‘I'm sorry’ may undermine that bravery, or even leave someone second-guessing whether they made the right decision.”

While the end of a relationship certainly may be cause for sympathy—I myself have had my share of emotionally annihilating splits in which condolences were very much in order—it’s not always appropriate or helpful to automatically assume that someone is struggling just because they’ve been through a breakup.

“Culturally, we are conditioned to assume that when a relationship ends, it’s a negative thing and that it must therefore be an unfortunate event,” says relationship coach Natalia Juarez, founder of Lovistics. “While expressing sympathy in some situations is appropriate, it’s essential to consider a variety of factors. Each breakup is unique and multi-faceted.” Rather than reaching for a canned response, “it’s far better to actively listen and be mindful,” says Juarez.

It's also worth remembering that even the person who initiated the breakup might be struggling. “People assume that if you did the breaking up, that you aren't unhappy about it, but that's not always true,” adds Susan Trombetti, matchmaker and CEO of Exclusive Matchmaking.

This, of course, requires a little more effort than offering the standard post-breakup condolences. Below, a few tips from the pros for approaching breakup news more mindfully.

Don’t assume you know how someone is feeling. An easy way to avoid doing this? Just ask! Instead of reaching for an “Oh, I’m so sorry,” Juarez recommends starting out with a simple “How are you feeling about it?”

Another option? “Do you want to talk about it, or talk about something else?” suggests Lundgren. It's a simple way to let someone decide whether they're in the mood to unpack the breakup or would rather focus on literally anything else.

“As a general rule, it’s most supportive to ask how someone is feeling about a big transition. This gives them room to honor their different feelings and experiences without the pressure to fit some social norm or expectation,” says Brock. “They may feel terrified yet hopeful, sad and relieved, guilty yet proud, ecstatic and free.”

It's also worth recognizing the courage it can take to end a relationship. “Everyone thinks breakups are awful, but that misses how much bravery it takes to make that decision,” notes Vanessa Errecarte, author of Valuable & Visible.

Sometimes the best thing you can give someone going through something significant is space to embrace what may be a wide range of conflicting emotions.

But know that that might look very different from situation to situation. Before expressing sympathy or offering advice, try asking something along the lines of “How can I support you?” says Brock. “Sometimes people need someone to celebrate with them, distract them from misery, give sound advice, plan an adventure, or join them in bashing their ex. When in doubt, just listen and never assume.”

Support doesn't always have to involve a conversation about the breakup, either. “Inviting someone out to lunch or dinner shows you care,” says Trombetti. “Just sitting with them is very comforting and shows caring and empathy.”

Asking someone what, if anything, they need instead of assuming makes it much easier to provide actually useful support to a friend who might need it. “Communicating openly is so much better than simply saying, ‘If you need me, I’m here,’” explains Juarez. “The fact is, many broken-hearted people don’t reach out because they don’t want to be a burden.”

A little self-reflection never hurts. When confronted with the news of someone else’s split, it may be “helpful to assess and challenge your own beliefs about marriage or long-term partnership,” says Brock. “While it’s totally okay to want a long-term partnership for yourself, others may not share that as a priority. As you support your friend through their breakup, stay mindful that being single isn’t inherently bad or wrong or just a blip on the radar en route to partnership.”

Instead of treating the end of a relationship as a setback, consider acknowledging the strength it can take to walk away from something that isn't working. “Help the person who's going through the breakup see the strength in making a hard decision,” says Errecarte.

#6934. Helen Mirren’s New “Split” Bixie Is the Most Sophisticated Short Style for Women Over 50: How to Recreate It

This cut is both versatile and sophisticated

Finding a short hairstyle that is both modern and timeless isn't easy, but Dame Helen Mirren has mastered it. Her softly swept bixie, complete with a side fringe, is a particularly elegant example.

The actor debuted her asymmetric cut, coined the “split” bixie, at the London premiere of her upcoming film, A Talent for Murder. Defined by its pixie-like proportions and bob-length, face-framing front, the bixie is gaining ground as one of this year's most popular haircuts.

"A bixie has all the benefits of a short style, but because the front is kept longer and softer, it has none of the severity of other short haircuts," Sasha Leslie, senior hairstylist at Nicola Clarke, previously told Harper’s Bazaar U.K.. The cut also adds versatility, as the longer front sections can be swept to the side, worn loose around the face, or styled back for a more polished finish.

For the occasion, Mirren opted for a split bixie cut, a more directional version of the classic style, with a deep side parting and a sweeping fringe that frames the face. Of course, age shouldn't dictate your haircut, but for those with mature hair looking for a sophisticated, easy-to-style length, the split bixie makes a compelling case.

If you're tempted to follow suit, consider a few details before your next salon appointment. As Leslie notes, the shorter length does require regular trims to maintain its shape. However, if you're too busy to schedule frequent appointments, she recommends keeping the bixie slightly longer, closer to a bob, so it grows out seamlessly. For thicker hair, she suggests asking your hairstylist to remove excess bulk while keeping length; thinner textures benefit from a more graduated silhouette to create the illusion of extra volume.

When it comes to styling, the split bixie's key feature is its side-swept part. It adds softness and movement to the shorter cut and is also a clever trick to achieve a natural contour. "Wearing a sleek side parting is a great way for one's hairstyle to contribute to a sharper, more contoured appearance, by adding more angles and height to the face," trend forecaster and hairstylist Tom Smith previously told Harper’s Bazaar U.K.. "Most people will suit a side parting more on one side than the other, so it's a good idea to try out a side parting on either side of your face to see which is more flattering for you."

Above all, Mirren's look reminds us that great hair has no age limit. With its flattering shape, soft movement, and effortless appeal, the bixie makes a strong case for embracing shorter lengths at any stage of life.

#6929. Asking for Rough Sex Is All About Being Specific

Below, sex experts explain how to bring it up, set boundaries, and explore rougher sex safely.

Maybe you're into the idea of having your wrists pinned above your head. Maybe a little hair-pulling or spanking sounds hot. Or maybe you've been thinking about asking your partner to be a little rougher in bed, but you're not entirely sure how to bring it up without making things awkward. Whatever your version of rough sex looks like, figuring out how to ask for it can feel surprisingly intimidating.

For one thing, what does “rougher” even mean? Are we talking a firmer grip during sex? A little spanking? Being told what to do? The possibilities are pretty much endless, and what sounds hot to one person might be a hard no for someone else.

“That's why ‘Do you like it rough?’ is just the start of a conversation. Ask what ‘rough’ actually means to the person you're with,” says Lee Anne Wine, a Toronto-based certified sex therapist and educator.

Of course, knowing what you want and actually saying it out loud are two different things. And while asking your partner to try something new can feel vulnerable, getting specific about your desires, boundaries, and expectations is what makes exploring rough sex possible without anyone having to guess what the other person is into.

Whether you're curious about BDSM or just want to experiment with a little more intensity, here's how to bring it up, figure out what you're both comfortable with, and get started.

Rough sex is exactly what it sounds like: consensual sex that involves a little more physical intensity than you might be used to. That could mean anything from firmer kissing and gripping to spanking, hair-pulling, or being more assertive with your partner. And while rough sex can overlap with BDSM, the two aren't necessarily interchangeable.

“Rough sex is more spontaneous intensity woven into regular sex,” explains Annette Benedetti, sex and intimacy coach and host of Talk Sex With Annette. BDSM, on the other hand, can involve more structured activities, negotiated power dynamics, and roles like dominant and submissive. You can enjoy one, both, or neither.

As for why people are into it? For some, it's about heightened physical sensations. For others, the appeal is more psychological, whether that's taking control, giving it up, or simply feeling desired in a different way. The important distinction is that everyone involved actively wants what's happening, and that consent can change at any time.

Asking your partner for rougher sex doesn't require a perfectly rehearsed speech. You just need to be honest about what you're curious about and willing to hear what your partner thinks, too.

As tempting as it might be to blurt out “Be rougher!” in the middle of sex, it's better to have the initial conversation when you're both fully clothed and not distracted by everything else going on.

“Bring it up with your clothes on, not in the heat of the moment, and lead with curiosity instead of a demand,” says Benedetti. “Something like, ‘I’ve been curious about trying something a little more intense; would you be open to talking about it?’”

The key is making it an invitation rather than implying your partner isn't doing enough for you in bed. You can even admit you're nervous. Saying something like “This feels a little awkward to bring up, but I've been thinking about trying something new with you” gives you a way into the conversation without putting pressure on either of you to agree to anything right away.

And if your partner needs time to think about it? That's fine, too. The point is to start talking, not get an immediate yes.

Here's the thing about asking someone to be rougher: You're going to have to explain what that actually means to you. Because “rough” could mean a firmer grip on your hips, being held down, a little spanking, or something else entirely.

Wine recommends starting with one specific example rather than trying to describe an entire fantasy at once. You might say, “I feel a little shy saying this, but I think I’d enjoy it if you held my hips a little more firmly when we kiss. How does that sound to you?”

From there, talk about what you each find appealing and what you're not interested in trying. Maybe you like the idea of being told what to do but have zero interest in pain. Maybe you're curious about spanking but not restraints. None of those preferences need to come as a package deal.

And remember, asking for something doesn't mean your partner has to be into it, just as their interest in something doesn't mean you have to try it. You're figuring out where your desires overlap, not negotiating someone into a yes.

Before you start experimenting, get clear about what's on the table, what's a definite no, and what you might be open to exploring later. That includes talking about intensity, where you do and don't want to be touched, and whether either of you has injuries or sensitivities to consider.

“If you are planning to try something new, such as spanking, impact play, biting, or scratching, make sure to discuss your intentions first with your partner,” says Candice Smith, sex expert and cofounder of The KinkKit.

It can help to make a simple yes/maybe/no list together so neither of you has to guess. You'll also want to agree on a safeword or signal before things get started. One option is the traffic-light system: “green” means you're comfortable continuing, “yellow” means pause or slow down and check in, and “red” means stop immediately.

Wine also recommends having a nonverbal signal, such as three taps, in case speaking becomes difficult. And regardless of what safeword you choose, regular words like “no” and “stop” always count. Consent isn't something you establish once and then forget about. Either person can change their mind at any point, even if you've already agreed to try something.

Once you've talked through what you're both interested in trying, you can start experimenting. But rougher doesn't automatically mean better, and there's no reason to jump straight into the most intense version of something you've never done before. A little trial and error is part of figuring out what works for you, as long as you're both communicating along the way.

If you're new to rough sex, you don't need to break out the handcuffs on night one. In fact, you don't need any equipment at all. Try adding a firmer grip around your partner's waist while kissing, experimenting with a more assertive tone during dirty talk, or asking them to guide your hands during sex.

Wine recommends starting with something you already enjoy and changing just one detail. That way, you can figure out what feels good without introducing a bunch of new sensations at once.

You can also experiment with intensity using a simple rating system. For example, start with gentle pressure and ask your partner to rate it on a scale of one to five, then adjust based on their feedback. Just remember that what feels like a three to you might feel like a five to someone else.

If spanking is on your list of things to try, start gently. Impact play involves consensual hitting or spanking for sensation, and knowing where and how to make contact matters.

“If you’ve both agreed to spanking, start with light, open-handed contact on the fleshy part of the buttocks. Keep impact away from the spine, lower back, and tailbone,” advises Wine.

Avoid assuming that harder is automatically hotter. Start with low intensity, check in frequently, and only increase it if your partner explicitly wants you to. And if either of you decides you're not into it after all, that's a perfectly good reason to stop.

Hair-pulling and restraints might sound relatively straightforward, but both can cause injuries if you're not careful.

For hair-pulling, Wine recommends agreeing on a gentle hold close to the scalp rather than yanking on the ends or jerking someone's head backward. If you're curious about bondage, start with soft cuffs that have a quick-release mechanism you've already tested.

Skip zip ties, cords, and improvised restraints that can tighten unexpectedly. Never leave someone restrained alone, and release them immediately if they experience pain, numbness, tingling, or changes in skin color.

And one particularly important safety note: Keep pressure off the neck and avoid anything that restricts breathing. As Wine puts it, “There’s no safe way to choke someone during sex.”

Rougher sex doesn't mean you need to deal with uncomfortable friction. In fact, adding some lube can make a big difference, particularly if you're experimenting with more vigorous penetration.

Keep some extra lube nearby, reapply as needed, and pay attention to how your body feels. If something starts to hurt or feel irritating, don't try to push through it. Just make sure you're using a lubricant that's compatible with any condoms or sex toys involved. Oil-based lubricants, for example, can damage latex condoms.

Even if you've already discussed your boundaries and agreed on a safeword, that doesn't mean you can stop communicating once things get going.

Wine suggests keeping check-ins simple with questions like “More, less, or same?” or “Do you want me to keep going?” That way, you're giving your partner an easy opportunity to tell you what feels good and what doesn't.

And don't rely on verbal responses alone. If someone suddenly goes quiet, freezes, pulls away, or seems disconnected, stop what you're doing and check in rather than assuming they're enjoying themselves.

“If something hurts, feels wrong, or goes past a boundary, stop the activity and release any restraints immediately,” Wine advises. “Ask what they need and give them room to answer. Nobody owes an explanation for stopping. Only resume if you both clearly want to.”

Just because the sex is over doesn't mean the experience is. Aftercare, aka taking care of each other physically and emotionally after an intense sexual experience, is an important part of exploring rough sex. And what that looks like will depend on the people involved.

“Someone might want water and a blanket, a hug, reassurance, or a few minutes alone,” says Wine. “Ask before assuming touch will help.”

For some people, aftercare might mean cuddling and talking about what they loved. For others, it could mean grabbing a snack, tending to any sore spots, or having a little space to themselves. There's no one right way to do it, and what feels comforting to one person might not be what another needs.

It's also worth checking in with each other the next day, especially if you're trying something new. Did you enjoy the intensity? Was there anything that surprised you? Is there something you'd want to do differently next time?

As Benedetti puts it, “There’s no award for intensity.” Rough sex isn't about pushing yourself or your partner to see how far you can go. It's about figuring out what you both enjoy, respecting each other's limits, and knowing you can always decide something isn't for you.
