#6925. There’s Science Behind Why You’re So Obsessed With Your Partner's Smell

Is it pheromones, chemistry, or something else entirely?

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

You know that thing where your partner's T-shirt somehow smells better after they've worn it? Or when you find yourself nuzzling into their neck during a hug because you genuinely can't get enough of how they smell? It's not necessarily their cologne, shampoo, or laundry detergent you're into, either. Sometimes, it's just them. And apparently, you're really into it.

If you've ever wondered why you're so attracted to your partner's natural smell, you've probably heard that pheromones are to blame. There's this idea that our bodies can somehow sniff out the people we're most compatible with, even before our brains catch up. But the science behind that theory isn't nearly as straightforward as you might think.

“Researchers haven't definitively identified a human sex pheromone that reliably produces sexual attraction in the way we sometimes see in other species,” explains Casey Tanner, a LELO sexpert and certified sex therapist.

That doesn't mean your partner's smell has nothing to do with your feelings for them, though. Our sense of smell is closely connected to memory and emotion, which helps explain why someone's scent can become so comforting (or downright irresistible) once we're attached to them. But does loving the way your partner smells actually mean you're compatible? And should you be concerned if you don't particularly care for their scent? Here's what the science says.

Pheromones are chemical signals released by one member of a species that can trigger specific behavioral or physical responses in another member of the same species. In the animal kingdom, they play a role in everything from attracting mates to marking territory. But when it comes to humans, things get a little less clear.

According to Tanner, while pheromones are well-documented in other animals, their role in human sexual attraction remains unproven. So, there's no solid scientific evidence that your body is sending out some irresistible chemical signal that makes certain people want to jump your bones.

And while pheromones and body odor often get lumped together, they're not actually the same thing. According to Tanner, your natural body odor is essentially your unique scent signature, shaped by everything from genetics and hormones to skin bacteria, diet, and your environment. Unlike pheromones, which are defined by the biological responses they trigger, ordinary body odor doesn't have to produce a specific response to influence how someone feels about you. So, while you might find someone's body odor incredibly attractive, that doesn't mean you're responding to pheromones.

Research suggests that body odor can influence attraction and who we're drawn to, but scientists are still figuring out exactly how much of a role it plays. And just because someone's natural scent appeals to you doesn't mean your bodies have identified each other as a perfect genetic match.

If you've ever found yourself lingering over your partner's pillow after they've left, there's a reason their scent can feel so comforting. And it has a lot to do with how closely our sense of smell is connected to memory and emotion.

“When we're falling for someone, their smell can become intertwined with experiences of pleasure, affection, comfort, and arousal,” Tanner explains. “Over time, that scent can become a kind of shortcut to feeling close to that person!”

Basically, your brain starts associating your partner's natural scent with all the things you love about being around them, from cuddling on the couch to having sex. And those associations can stick around even when you're physically apart.

“Once we’re attached to that person, their body odor essentially becomes a reinforcement or reward,” says psychologist Pamela Dalton, PhD, MPH. Their scent can bring back positive memories and feelings of intimacy, which helps explain why something as simple as a worn shirt can make you feel closer to someone you're missing.

As much as we'd love to tell you that being obsessed with your partner's natural scent means you've found your soulmate, science doesn't exactly back that up. Sure, there may be some biological factors at play, but liking the way someone smells isn't proof that you're genetically compatible, much less destined to spend your lives together.

Some research has explored whether people are more attracted to the body odor of potential partners whose immune-system genes differ from their own. The theory is that these differences could lead to greater immune diversity in biological offspring. But according to Tanner, the findings have been inconsistent, and there's no reliable evidence that humans choose romantic partners based on genetic compatibility.

And even if someone's scent makes you want to bury your face in their chest 24/7, that doesn't tell you much about whether you'll actually work as a couple.

“Loving your partner's scent can be a true reflection of attraction and attachment, but no, it’s not a reliable diagnostic tool for long-term compatibility,” Tanner says. “Chemistry is a great indicator of initial attraction, but compatibility is something we have to discover, negotiate, and create over time.”

In other words, enjoying someone's natural smell is a nice bonus, but it's not a substitute for shared values, trust, communication, or the other things that make a relationship work. And if you're not immediately drawn to someone's scent, that doesn't mean attraction can't develop as you get to know them.

If you've made it this far and realized you've never once felt the urge to steal your partner's T-shirt just to smell it, don't stress. Not everyone is particularly sensitive to scent, and being indifferent to your partner's natural smell doesn't mean you're any less attracted to them.

According to Tanner, people vary in how sensitive they are to smells, how much attention they pay to them, and how important scent is to their experience of attraction. Some people might be more drawn to their partner's appearance, voice, personality, or the emotional connection they share.

“If you’re indifferent to a person’s smell, it’s likely not going to be a game-changer because once you have the opportunity to get to know the person, you find [other] positives and compatibility between the two of you,” says Dalton.

And if you're otherwise happy with your relationship, there's no reason to start questioning it just because your partner's scent doesn't do anything special for you. As Tanner puts it, “If you’re into them, don’t go looking for a problem just because you haven’t noticed their scent!”

Okay, but what if you used to love your partner's natural scent and now you can't stand it? Before you start questioning your entire relationship, know that a change in how you perceive someone's smell doesn't automatically mean your feelings for them have changed.

According to Tanner, everything from hormonal fluctuations and medications to diet, illness, and changes in your sense of smell can affect how you experience your partner's scent. Pregnancy, for example, can make you more sensitive to certain smells. And sometimes, the explanation is as simple as your partner switching shampoos or laundry detergents.

Of course, emotions can play a role, too. Because scent is so closely connected to memory and feelings, your associations with someone's smell can change as your relationship evolves.

“There is evidence that when, for whatever reason, that relationship isn’t working, that body odor is no longer pleasant or preferred,” says Dalton. “All of a sudden, because of the association with negative characteristics, [the body odor] is changing. Your response is changing.”

Still, suddenly disliking your partner's scent isn't proof that something is wrong between you. As Tanner explains, a change in smell isn't a reliable indicator of relationship conflict, and there may be a physical explanation worth considering first.

If you notice a significant change in your partner's actual body odor, it's worth gently asking whether anything has changed with their health, medications, or routine. And if there's a particularly noticeable change in genital odor, Tanner recommends encouraging them to check in with a healthcare professional.

Bottom line: A change in how your partner smells to you isn't automatically a sign that something's wrong. How you feel about your partner's scent can change for all sorts of reasons, and it's just one small part of the bigger picture.

#6923. Bella Hadid Buys Rustic Colorado Retreat for $5.5 Million

The Victoria’s Secret angel recently started dating someone with strong family ties to the area.

The rugged allure of the Mountain West continues to attract celebrities. Tom Cruise and Oprah Winfrey both own properties in the ski mecca of Telluride. Kevin Costner glamorized multigenerational homesteading with his hit show Yellowstone and, in real life, oversees Aspen’s sprawling Dunbar Ranch. Now, model Bella Hadid—who recently opened the 2026 Victoria’s Secret runway show—just acquired an alpine retreat of her own in a remote town called Crested Butte.

Nestled deep in Colorado’s Rocky Mountains, a roughly three-hour drive from Aspen, Crested Butte has been known as the “Wildflower Capital of Colorado” since 1990, when the state legislature bestowed the honorary title on the Gunnison County town (population: 1,639).

Hadid has reportedly been living in the tiny village for “several months,” per The Wall Street Journal, which was the first to report the sale. The news also comes just days after the model went public on Instagram with new boyfriend Nathan Lacy, a construction manager who has ties to the region.

“I’ve always loved hockey living in NYC,” she wrote, alongside several snaps of her and Lacy at a Colorado Avalanche game. “And being a Colorado girl at heart, growing up here since i was young, I guess it was only a matter of time before I found my way to the @coloradoavalanche.”

Her new creekside compound hit the market over the summer for nearly $7 million with Channing Boucher of LIV Sotheby’s International Realty. Hadid reportedly got it at a discount, paying $5.5 million.

Built in 2001, the property sits on a 0.2-acre parcel with 200 feet of frontage on Coal Creek. Wrapped in weathered lumber, the three-bedroom, three-bathroom retreat was partially constructed out of two “original 100-year-old coal sheds,” per the listing. The rustic interiors, meanwhile, come with 30-foot beamed ceilings and hardwood floors reclaimed from a Virginia barn.

Rounding out the estate is a creek-front dining terrace plus a hot tub at the edge of the water.

This appears to be Hadid’s first real estate transaction since 2021, when she offloaded her New York City penthouse for $6.35 million.

Geoffrey Montes is an associate editor at ELLE Decor with a serious love for all things real estate and design. #6920. It’s the Season of the Statement Glove

Vivid colors, lush leathers, and fingerless styles: Gloves have never been bolder.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

If the late, great, legendary fashion editor Diana Vreeland were still alive, I believe she would incorporate a question like, “Why don’t you replace your ring stacks with statement gloves?” into her famous “Why don’t you” column, where she rhetorically mused on ridiculous and often opulent situations. A good glove is a winter staple, yes. But lately, fashion has been having a love affair with embellished, stylized gloves. We’ve now seen the opera glove in all of its forms. But what about gloves bedecked in crystal cuffs, neon hues, faux fur effects, and beyond? They’ve already arrived, on the runways and at retailers.

Consider Givenchy’s leather wonders, which come in ballooning silhouettes that envelop the hands and wrists. Or Prada’s spring/summer 2026 silky satin opera gloves in shades of pale yellow or olive green. With delicate ruching throughout, the formal opera glove gets an ironic, modern, slouched, and louche makeover. Elsewhere, Khaite has shown the power of an opera-length glove in python. Clearly, texture is an embellishment all its own in these kinds of extreme examples.

In some cases, the gloves become the jewelry. In others, jewelry further accents the statement glove, like for Mugler’s fall/winter 2026 show in which models wore kelly green and royal purple scrunched leather gloves up to their elbows, sometimes styled with a big chunky bracelet over top. Elsewhere, some gloves were mismatched: vivid yellow on one hand and brilliant blue on the other. The brand called it a new take on power dressing, which melded codes of the 1980s, 1940s, and 17th-century aristocratic court coats. “Ultimately, this show intends to acknowledge that self-empowerment is the most potent antidote of clarity to the oppressive forces and grey political climate that surrounds us at this very moment,” Mugler creative director Castro Freitas was quoted in the show notes, “and most of all, it’s a manifesto of the highest power that we should never forget to reclaim: FREEDOM.”

Most of all, the statement glove dresses up any neutral wool coat or adds an extra layer of over-the-top theatrics to a statement coat. When in doubt, you could also simply opt for a colorful glove, which the runways have loved in droves lately. Take, for example, Valentino, which has been pumping out short and statement-making silky gloves in shades of ’80s neon pink or dreamy cobalt blue.

Sometimes, all you need is a change of silhouette, too. At Chanel, the fingerless glove has long been a staple, and it’s now become a favorite of superfan clients during street style moments between shows. Elsewhere, the fingerless glove has forever been a celeb favorite, especially when it comes to style legends like Madonna.

“We’ve got every color under the rainbow of gloves and/or a sheer opera-length glove that is in either a matching color or an opposite color of whatever the look is,” Madonna’s stylist Rita Melssen told ELLE in August 2026.

One thing is clear: Statement gloves are the new power accessory, equal parts practical, theatrical, and impossible to ignore.

#6919. What’s Your Vacation Vibe?

Whether brand new or reimagined, these four hotels offer distinctive stays. The only question is, which style to choose?

To be a discerning traveler is to have, in equal measure, preference and knowledge of the endlessly evolving hotel landscape. But even the greatest of globetrotters need a guide to what’s new every once in a while. Below, find four recently opened (or reopened) hotels, organized by travel preference: Looking for something in the city? We’ve got you. Country? We have that too. A place to hot air balloon? No worries. The toughest part will just be figuring out where to go first.

Time and Place: Perched atop the Spanish Steps, this 133-year-old mainstay is Old World luxe translated with fresh verve following a lavish update with revamped public spaces by ELLE Decor A-List designer Jacques Garcia.

Design Vibes: Delving into the archives for the Palm Court Garden redo, Garcia collaborated with Italian firm Colleoni Roberto & C. on a green-and-white-striped upholstery and with a Roman blacksmith on vintage-style lanterns.

Hide Out In… One of the damask-covered suites or at the renovated Michelin-starred Imàgo restaurant.

Time and Place: Some will recognize this property as the former home of the Standard, sans human fish tank at the check-in desk. Actually, sans any check-in desk. There’s also no room service, but there is a rooftop park with a gym, a neon blue pool, and 360° views.

Design Vibes: Legendary hotelier Ian Schrager, with his “luxury for all” philosophy, teamed up with minimalist maestro John Pawson on the architecture. Rooms feature custom-made plush seating, pale woods, and custom lighting systems for shifting moods.

Hide Out In… Your own private cineplex. Each room has a projector and 66-inch screen. Or disappear on the dance floor of the Studio, a speakeasy-style nightclub clad in marble and velvet.

Time and Place: Occupying a colonial-style former school in Old Bennington dating from 1829, this intimate inn was brought to life by Rafe Churchill, co-founder of the Elle Decor A-List firm Hendricks Churchill, and Casey Sunderland, Churchill’s partner in the new hospitality firm Place in Mind.

Design Vibes: The hotel leans into its links to a family of writers and to nearby Bennington College with an inviting library stocked with books by alums. Each of the eight unique guestrooms is cozily layered, mixing rich jewel tones and florals with antiques and art that ranges from the Hudson River School to Keith Haring.

Hide Out In… The gardens. Landscape designer Grace Fuller Marroquin lent her wild, cinematic touch to the grounds, which feature an array of native plants as well as a fruit orchard.

Time and Place: Steps from slopes that hosted ski events for two Olympics (1956 and 2026), the hotel occupies a former palazzo from the 1920s.

Design Vibes: Eschewing the traditional Alpine look, designer Achille Salvagni tailored the guestrooms with bespoke references to the local landscape and culture, like plaster bas-reliefs of mountains, ceiling motifs based on traditional clothing embroidery, and hardware in the shape of native wildlife.

Hide Out In… A hot air balloon. The hotel arranges private tours in the off season for unparalleled mountain views. Or, more down to earth, the spa includes a cryo chamber and a plunge pool.

#6915. Hailey Bieber and Bella Hadid Make a Case For “Coyote Chai” Colored Fur Coats

Chocolate brown’s cooler cousin has officially entered the chat.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

Move over, chocolate brown: There's a new shade taking over the celebrity style scene, and it's honestly the perfect shade for transitioning from fall to winter. Enter what I’m coining Coyote Chai, the deliciously rich, multi-tonal brown fur coat trend that Bella Hadid and Hailey Bieber are making a serious case for right now.

If winter dressing had a moodboard, these two would be running it. And while their personal aesthetics might differ, they've seemingly landed on the same outerwear obsession: fluffy, statement-making coats in a gorgeous mix of earthy browns, caramel, taupe, and deep brown.

Bella stepped out in a fuzzy, sandy-brown jacket paired with dark-wash denim and heels, alongside a brown Coach purse. Meanwhile, Hailey took a similarly tonal approach in New York, wearing a cropped, textured fur-effect jacket with warm brown, honey, and gray undertones, styled with classic jeans and barely-there black heels.

While it feels like chocolate brown has been the star of the show, this shift into what I’m calling Coyote Chai feels like the rightful move as we head closer to winter, brightening the sartorial vibes as it gets gloomier outside. This shade of brown is the cooler, blended cousin of espresso and caramel shades. As a non-coffee drinker and chai latte warrior, this light brown shade blended with hints of darker spots is the perfect layering style coat to be bundled up in. And as Bieber and Hadid show, the coat makes the simplest of outfits all the more interesting.

Consider this your sign to grab a good coat for winter, and maybe make it in this fun Coyote Chai shade.

#6913. Warren Beatty and Annette Bening’s Former L.A. Mansion Lists for Nearly $15 Million

The Hollywood power couple custom-built the gated Beverly Hills house.

The longtime Beverly Hills home of Warren Beatty and Annette Bening will soon hit the market, ELLE Decor can exclusively reveal. The married couple custom-built the gated residence in 1992 and raised their four kids there. The stars are one of Hollywood’s most famous power couples: Beatty has received 14 Academy Award nominations, winning for Best Director in 1982, and three Golden Globe Awards, while Bening has received five Oscar nominations, including Best Actress for 1999’s American Beauty, for which she won the Golden Globe.

The A-list actors parted with their longtime property in 2012, selling it for $6.7 million, per Zillow. Its new asking price will be $14.995 million.

The nine-bedroom estate’s current owners—hotelier Jonathan Baker and his wife, Jenny—acquired the property directly from Beatty and Bening, then spearheaded a $5 million renovation that concluded in 2019. They modified the Mediterranean-style home to have a more contemporary feel, with minimal moldings, glossy surfaces, modern furnishings, and bold splashes of color.

However, they still paid homage to the estate’s movie-star pedigree, creating what they call the “Warren Beatty Theater,” a 17-seat screening room in what was once Beatty’s home office.

Other nods to the legendary actor—who starred in classics like Bonnie and Clyde, Dick Tracy, and Bulworth—included integrating his drop-down projection screen into the new living room. Additional perks include a wine room with a suspended-wire storage system, a wood-paneled dressing room with plenty of built-ins, and a network of terraces.

The home’s amenities continue on the manicured grounds, which feature an infinity-edge pool, a lighted tennis court, multiple al fresco entertaining areas, and a separate guest house.

The couple are parents to four children, among them Ella Beatty, who is following in her parents’ acting footsteps and recently played Lizzie Borden in the Netflix series Monster: The Lizzie Borden Story.

The Beverly Hills house will be listed for $14.995 million with Cory Weiss of Douglas Elliman and Jacob Greene of the Altman Brothers at Douglas Elliman.

#6911. Which Ultra-Processed Foods Are the Worst for You? A Dietitian Breaks It Down

From hot dogs to whole-grain breads, here are the ultra-processed foods worth limiting first.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Research over the years has shown exactly how ultra-processed foods (UPFs) negatively affect the body. What’s still being studied is how many of these specific foods, and how much of them, impact the rise in certain cancers and other diseases. Although the internet is rife with stats showing an uptick in certain cancers among younger adults, a recent study conducted among a massive 460,000 Americans debunked one of the many fear-mongering rumors that ultra-processed foods are associated with increased colorectal cancer risk specifically. But before you place your next fast food delivery order, Caitlin Hosmer, MS, RD, LDN, a registered dietitian and co-founder of MenoPantry alongside Mark Gilbert, nutritionist from The 1:1 Diet by Cambridge Weight Plan, explains which parts of the meal are potentially the most harmful, and what you should know about the study’s findings to understand which ultra-processed foods are actually the worst for you.

Most researchers use a system called NOVA, developed by a Brazilian epidemiologist, Carlos Augusto Monteiro, who coined the term “ultra-processed foods.” Hosmer explains that NOVA sorts foods into four groups based on how much they’ve been processed and not by their nutrients.

Gilbert argues that the system is questionable. “Even nutrition experts were unable to categorize foods reliably or consistently into one of [these] four categories, from unprocessed to ultra-processed. Other systems, including IFIC, Nutri-Score, and the proposed CHIPS system, exist but are similarly controversial,” says Gilbert.

To better understand what these foods are exactly, Hosmer says the simplest way to put it is that a processed food is still recognizably food that’s been preserved or prepared. An ultra-processed food has been taken apart. “These foods are designed to be cheap, shelf-stable, convenient, and very easy to overeat. The key caveat is that NOVA describes how a food is made, not how nutritious it is. So the Ultra-processed category can hold both a hot dog and a packaged whole-grain bread,” says Hosmer.

Gilbert further explains that the processes many of these foods undergo often reduce healthy, essential nutrients, remove fiber, and make the resulting food easier to consume, absorb, and digest, leading to untoward blood sugar levels, less satiety and, in the longer term, poorer metabolic health.

Gilbert notes that it’s difficult to deem any one type of UPF as “worst” simply because genetic and environmental risk factors to various diseases and ill health vary so vastly. “So, just like no single diet is ‘best’ for everyone, no single food is necessarily worst. For instance, if someone has a high heart and vascular disease risk, they might benefit from less saturated fat. If someone has a high risk of diabetes, they should avoid sugar and other refined carbohydrates that raise their blood sugar levels,” Gilbert explains.

However, Hosmer points out that when researchers break ultra-processed foods into subgroups, processed meat consistently comes out on top. The aforementioned study of more than 460,000 adults, conducted by the National Cancer Institute (NCI), found that ultra-processed food intake overall wasn’t linked to colorectal cancer—but higher consumption of meat from ultra-processed food sources was. Hosmer references a 2024 Harvard analysis in The British Medical Journal that found the same pattern for mortality.

“Across more than 30 years of follow-up, meat-, poultry-, and seafood-based ready-to-eat products showed the strongest and most consistent associations, followed by sugar-sweetened and artificially sweetened beverages, dairy-based desserts, and ultra-processed breakfast food. That category includes processed meats such as hot dogs, deli meats, bacon, sausages, and many frozen breaded meat products,” Hosmer summarizes.

Second to processed meat are sweetened drinks—both sugar-sweetened and artificially sweetened. “Sodas, sweet teas, energy drinks, and diet drinks give you sweetness and calories (or the expectation of calories) with no fiber, protein, or real food structure. They show up consistently across heart, metabolic, and mortality research,” Hosmer notes.

Sugar-laced and dairy-based desserts alongside sugary breakfast foods follow on the list. “Many sugary cereals and pastries are basically dessert dressed up as breakfast,” says Hosmer.

These foods won’t necessarily give you cancer or lead to other diseases; Hosmer explains that the more useful message is that “worst” means low in what the body needs and high in what it doesn’t. In the NCI study, higher intake of whole grains, fiber, dairy products, and calcium from ultra-processed food sources was linked to a lower incidence of colorectal cancer. “A packaged whole-grain bread and a package of bologna are both “ultra-processed,” but they are not the same choice,” she adds.

Many UPFs are often made to be irresistible, meaning you’ll likely eat more than the recommended serving size. Gilbert explains that this leads to large spikes in blood sugar and doesn’t reduce satiety as much as most whole foods do. UPFs can also increase overall calorie intake. “Increased calorie intake and excess blood sugar spikes cause weight gain, high blood pressure, inflammation, diabetes, unhealthy blood lipids, generally poor metabolic health, and a variety of other effects which increase a person’s odds of the most common serious health conditions and diseases, reducing health span and lifespan,” Gilbert warns.

To illustrate, Hosmer points to what she calls a small but elegant 2019 National Institutes of Health (NIH) trial led by Kevin Hall, which she says has the strongest causal evidence. Twenty adults lived on a metabolic ward and ate either an ultra-processed or a minimally processed diet for two weeks each. The diets were matched in calories, sugar, fat, fiber, and sodium, and people could eat as much as they wanted. On the ultra-processed diet, participants ate about 500 more calories a day, ate faster, and gained weight—about two pounds; on the minimally processed diet, they lost about the same amount. “That points to something about the food’s form, not just its nutrients: it’s soft, energy-dense, quick to eat, and slow to trigger fullness,” says Hosmer.

Considering these results, she says there are multiple mechanisms present that likely work together:

Turn the package over and read the ingredient list. That tells you far more than the front of the box. Some signs:

Context matters too. A whole-grain bread with one dough conditioner is a very different food from a toaster pastry, even though both may technically count as ultra-processed. Ask two questions: how processed is it, and what is it actually giving me? Fiber, protein, whole grains, and added nutrients count for a lot.

This depends greatly on the specific foods in question. “That’s what makes this new study that differentiates between the different kinds of processed foods particularly important,” says Hosmer. “The clearest evidence is for processed meat. In 2015, the World Health Organization’s International Agency for Research on Cancer (IARC) classified processed meat as a Group 1 carcinogen for colorectal cancer. That’s the category for strong evidence that something causes cancer, though it says nothing about how big the risk is,” Hosmer clarifies. More practically, she further explains that the International Agency for Research on Cancer (IARC) estimated that eating 50 grams of processed meat every day—about one hot dog or a few slices of deli meat regularly—is associated with roughly an 18 percent increase in baseline colorectal cancer risk. “That’s a relative increase: for an average person, it moves a lifetime risk from approximately four to five percent to roughly five to six percent,” says Hosmer.

For ultra-processed foods as a whole category, Hosmer says the picture is less clear. The new NCI study is one of the largest ever done on this question. When the team looked by food group, whole grains and dairy were linked to lower risk and meat to higher risk. The researchers note possible opposing effects of some ultra-processed foods on colorectal cancer etiology—mainly that the helpful and harmful foods cancel each other out in the total. More specifically, the total consumption may matter more than the specific foods or food groups.

According to Hosmer, there’s also an indirect pathway. “Diets high in ultra-processed foods are linked to obesity, and excess body fat is an established risk factor for many cancers, including breast cancer after menopause, endometrial cancer, and colorectal cancer,” she notes. Hosmer warns, though, that this study’s findings shouldn’t be read as ultra-processed foods are fine. “Read it as a reminder that it’s hard to make a single recommendation about such a large and varied category of food.” When the researchers broke ultra-processed foods down by type, the results lined up with what we already know: whole grains, fiber, and dairy were linked to lower colorectal cancer risk, and processed meat to higher risk. Hosmer’s challenge is to check the new data against well-established research. “Headlines alone can be misleading, especially on topics as emotionally charged as what we eat.”

Turn the package over and read the ingredient list. That tells you far more than the front of the box. Here’s what to look for:

Context matters too. A whole-grain bread with one dough conditioner is a very different food from a toaster pastry, even though both may technically count as ultra-processed. Hosmer says to ask yourself two questions: “How processed is it, and what is it actually giving me?” Fiber, protein, whole grains, and added nutrients count for a lot.

Hosmer explains that the risks associated with ultra-processed foods include two things: how much of someone’s diet comes from these foods, and how much room their body has to absorb the negative effects.

One caveat from the new study that Hosmer points out is that participants were mostly older white Americans, so results may not generalize to younger or more diverse populations. “That matters because colorectal cancer is rising in adults under 50, the group that has grown up recently, and with the most ultra-processed food available,” she cautions.

Gilbert adds that knowing your family’s health history and getting various tests can offer some indications of individual risks, but predicting these risks is by no means foolproof. “Ultimately, those who have prediabetes, diabetes, high blood pressure, cardiovascular disease or cancer, or are living with overweight or obesity are the most at risk because these conditions can often be avoided or improved with better nutrition,” says Gilbert.

Both Gilbert and Hosmer agree here. “Some foods can be intentionally processed to increase and improve their nutritional profile rather than reduce it,” Gilbert explains. Frozen vegetables, canned beans, plain yogurt, canned salmon, and bagged salad are strong examples. They are processed and still very good for you. Processing often makes healthy food more affordable and accessible. Gilbert’s 1:1 Diet is designed to align with this. “The products we produce are processed, but instead of reducing the essential nutrient content of the products, we actually increase it, adding vitamins, minerals, essential fats, and quality protein,” Gilbert notes, adding that extensive research has shown his diet improves blood sugar, blood pressure, inflammation, metabolic health, cardiovascular health, and reverses diabetes in about half of dieters.

Myth: This new study proves ultra-processed food is safe.

Hosmer says explicitly that it doesn’t. “It looked at only one disease, colorectal cancer, in a mostly older population, and participants reported their diets just once, back in the mid-1990s. The authors themselves point out that ultra-processed foods became far more common and varied over the following two decades, so the study likely underestimated how much people were actually eating,” she clarifies. “The fairer reading is that which ultra-processed foods you eat matters more than the category itself: a whole-grain bread and a hot dog may both be “ultra-processed,” but they don’t affect the body the same way.”

Myth: It’s only about nutrients, and processing doesn’t matter.

Kevin Hall’s controlled feeding trial suggests otherwise. People ate significantly more on the ultra-processed diet even when nutrients were matched, so a food’s structure and how fast you eat it matter too.

Myth: Organic, plant-based, or “clean label” means it isn’t ultra-processed.

Myth: You have to completely eliminate ultra-processed foods.

Perfection isn’t the goal. Hosmer points out that in the NCI study, replacing 10 percent of the total weight of ultra-processed foods with minimally processed foods was associated with a 2 percent lower incidence of colorectal cancer. “We don’t yet know exactly what that looks like on an individual’s plate. The study measured diets statistically, not swap by swap. But the direction is clear, and it fits decades of nutrition research: incremental shifts away from ultra-processed foods appear to matter, even when they’re modest. Start with categories that matter most: processed meats and sweetened drinks,” she concludes.

#6910. A Full Timeline of Ella Langley and Tucker Wetmore’s Rumored Romance, Because It’s Very Confusing

If you’re a country music fan (or tbh, even if you’re not), then you’re probably well aware that the love lives of country music’s rising stars are quite entangled…and have been for a minute now. The latest (reported) update? Ella Langley and Tucker Wetmore are rumored to be dating, but we really can’t talk about their maybe-relationship without discussing Tucker’s ex Bryana Ferringer...or Ella’s rumored ex Riley Green (who went on to maybe date Bryana).

Ella and Tucker may have sparked dating rumors late last year, but it wasn’t without some drama on the way. It’s a pretty confusing ride, so buckle up and let’s get into it. Starting with…

After months of dating rumors following the success of Ella and Riley’s song “You Look Like You Love Me,” Ella finally shuts down Riley dating rumors. “We’re not dating,” she tells Taste of Country, adding, “We’re just good friends.”

Riley also shuts down dating speculation during an appearance on The Viall Files podcast. “Ella’s probably too smart to date me anyway,” he jokes, later adding, “When we first started having the success we did with that song, I kinda told Ella, I was like, ‘There’s gonna be a lot of things said, but I mean, you don’t really have to play into it or not play into it.’”

Tucker posts a TikTok on August 20 with the caption “@ my ex,” sparking breakup rumors with his model girlfriend Bryana (who, btw, appeared in his music video for “Wind Up Missin’ You”).

Soon after, Bryana is spotted with Riley at his Red Rocks tour stop.

Ella and Tucker both attend the 2025 BMI Country Awards on November 18, though it’s unclear whether this is the first time they ever meet (or if they even interacted, despite standing right next to each other in this group photo):

A day later, after several reported sightings around Nashville, Riley brings Bryana as his date to the CMA Awards on November 19.

However! Riley later refers to Bryana as merely a “friend,” telling Backstage Country, “It’s gonna be tough to tiptoe out of having a date to an award show. Yeah, I mean, you go to award shows all the time, and I’m always by myself, or I’ve got, like, a security guy, or a buddy with me, so I took a friend with me to the award show.”

Around the same time, Ella sparks dating rumors with Farmer Wants a Wife season 3 star John Sansone (whose cousin, music exec Sophia Sansone, previously dated Riley). Rumors begin after John includes a picture of Ella’s dog, Crue, in a November 26 Instagram photo dump...and then, on the same day, Ella includes a video of herself shooting shotguns with John in a photo dump of her own.

John later confirms to Taste of Country in April 2026 that he and Ella “dated for a couple of months” after he slid into her DMs but are no longer together.

Oh, and side note: John’s ex from Farmer Wants a Wife, Samantha Riehl, posted a TikTok with Tucker back in April 2025 with the caption, “Blondes are the worst.”

And then right after, John posted a TikTok with Bryana at Stagecoach (which has since been deleted).

Pretty sure nothing ever happened between Samantha and Tucker (or between Bryana and John), but still, it feels worth mentioning!!

On December 5, Tucker releases “Proving Me Right,” which fans are preeeetty convinced is about his ex, Bryana.

A few weeks later, Ella and Tucker are photographed together in the British Virgin Islands at the Soggy Dollar Bar...but considering the photo also includes other country artists, rumors circulate that the trip was possibly a writing retreat.

Tucker and Ella reunite to sing a live acoustic duet of Tucker’s “Sunburn” at the Heart Strings for Hope charity event on March 10.

And while Tucker didn’t write “Sunburn” himself, a few of the lyrics kiiiinda seem to allude to his December getaway with Ella.

Tucker then appears on the March 17 episode of Kristin Cavallari’s Let’s Be Honest podcast, where he claims that he has “never been broken up with” and has always been the one to “call it quits.” When Kristin asks if he has “sworn off blondes,” Tucker says, “Yep. I have. For the foreseeable future. That’s right.”

And then, a few days after that on March 21, Tucker posts an Instagram photo of himself holding a fish, and Ella slides into his comments with, “Cute fish.” Flirty!

Tucker discusses his hit “Brunette” with Audacy, saying, “My mom’s always said, ‘You're going to mess around with blondes for a little while but I truly believe you're going to settle down with the brunette,’ and I was like, ‘Yeah, 100 percent.’”

Not long after, during an appearance on Taste of Country Nights, Tucker notes, “Pretty much every girlfriend I have ever had was blonde, and I think it’s time to change up a couple of things.”

On September 23 (just a day after Ella breaks Mariah Carey’s record for longest-running Billboard Hot 100 hit with “Choosin’ Texas”), TMZ breaks the news that Tucker and Ella are (reportedly) dating.

The outlet obtains photos of the two out with friends in Nashville on the night of September 22 following the Nashville Songwriter Awards, with sources telling TMZ that the two met “through the music industry and mutual friends.”

Multiple sources also confirm the rumored romance to People so…it seems legit to me!

While making an appearance at WAVE in Wichita, Kansas, a fan straight-up asks Tucker whether he and Ella “are a thing.”

Tucker’s two-word response? “Next question.” Okay!!

Ella and Tucker seemingly take another trip to the Virgin Islands…at least, according to a sneaky fan who spotted them entering a restaurant together.

Soon after, they attend the CMAs, where they walk the carpet separately but still can’t dodge questions about their relationship status. When asked by People if there is a certain brunette he’s rooting for, he jokingly answers, “Zach Top or Stephen Wilson Jr.” Mmmkay!

Ella is also caught grinning during Tucker’s performance, which...isn’t confirmation of anything but certainly telling.

#6908. An Up-Close Look at The Lord of the Rings: The Rings of Power’s Twisted Love Triangle

Morfydd Clark, Jamie Campbell Bower, and Charlie Vickers unpack that tree nest scene.

This story contains spoilers for The Lord of the Rings: The Rings of Power.

After sleeping alone, it takes some getting used to sharing a bed with someone. This is a challenging proposition in the third season of The Lord of the Rings: The Rings of Power for the recently reunited Galadriel (Morfydd Clark) and her husband, Celeborn (Jamie Campbell Bower), when they use a tree nest as a makeshift bedchamber, as the Dark Lord Sauron (Charlie Vickers) hunts them down. But Elves are not mere mortals, and after centuries apart, they slip back into slumbering beside each other as if not a day has passed. On set, however, things were much more awkward: Galadriel’s sword gets in Clark’s way, she struggles to roll over in chainmail, and the couple’s moss pillow is too small.

It is mid-September 2025 at the legendary Shepperton Studios just outside London, and there has been another request for more real moss to elevate Bower’s head. (He likens this grassy pillow to the mattresses in The Princess and the Pea when we talk a year later.) The mood up in the tree nest, as Clark and Bower rehearse a scene from the fifth episode, is much lighter than what plays out on screen. Under the watchful eyes of director Sanaa Hamri, the actors figure out the logistics of spooning in this confined space, mimicking sleep so the camera can see their faces, and allowing Clark to shift to her side, then quickly back away when Galadriel imagines Sauron lying beside her. Later in the day, Vickers will switch in. The shock sends Galadriel falling backward out of the tree and straight into danger. This is not your run-of-the-mill love triangle or burgeoning throuple.

To put it mildly, Galadriel has been going through it in the adaptation of J.R.R. Tolkien’s history of Middle-earth. After she rejected Sauron’s offer to be his queen (again) at the end of season 2, he has since taken much of Middle-earth, and his quest to create the One Ring—and thereby rule over all—continues. In the previous episode, he attempted to locate an elf being held prisoner in Gundabad who knew the secrets of forging the One Ring. It turns out the last Elven-smith was already dead, but Galadriel’s long-lost husband, Celeborn, was locked up in a neighboring cell. Celeborn likely knows those same secrets that Sauron needs, making him invaluable. During their escape, Arondir (Ismael Cruz Córdova) is slain, and Sauron comes face-to-face with Galadriel for the first time this season. Well, other than popping into her subconscious.

When Sauron appears in Galadriel’s dreams again in the fifth episode, “The Lady of Lórien,” the safety of the tree canopy is immediately shattered as Galadriel’s sleeping mind pictures Sauron lying inches away where she expects to see Celeborn. When I catch up with Clark on Zoom a year later in September 2026, her face lights up when I mention this scene. “I loved being up there [in the tree nest],” she says. “It was so cozy.” While the episode ends with Celeborn being captured by Sauron—separating the couple once again—there is a brief respite from the horrors of this war-torn land. “There’s a real unabashed lean into the gorgeousness of fantasy,” Clark says of this season, including the foray into swoon-worthy romance in the cocooned foliage.

While the Elves have only just reunited, Clark and Bower had already established the foundation of Galadriel and Celeborn’s relationship during a dance sequence in the season 3 premiere episode, revealing a far lighter Galadriel than we have seen for some time. This also gave the actors an opportunity to get to know each other. “There is a magic about him,” Clark says of Bower. “We did get to rehearse that together, which also took away a certain type of magic in that learning a dance with someone is pretty vulnerable. We got to know each other doing that, which was really nice. He was so lovely, and he is elf-like.”

As we learned in season 1, Celeborn left to fight in the war during the First Age and never came home—Middle-earth is now in its Second Age. Galadriel and Celeborn share battle stories, but it is Sauron informing Celeborn that they have previously met (Celeborn claims he has no memory of this) that throws more fire onto this already sizzling dynamic. But before Sauron storms in like a jealous ex, Celeborn and Galadriel manage some K-I-S-S-I-N-G in the tree. “It was a tight space,” Bower laughs over the phone earlier this month. “Towards the end of that scene, there’s that beautiful moment of physical reconnection, and our intimacy coordinator [Sophie Cooch] was so helpful in finding a beauty and almost like a dance and a grace within that physical reconnection into the falling down [together], and being able to lie side by side,” Bower says.

Having just played Stranger Things’ big bad Vecna, Bower (who is a huge fan of Tolkien’s work) said he was looking for a character who represented love, and Celeborn radiates light even in the darkest circumstances. In the episode, Celeborn senses Galadriel is holding back, but doesn’t press too hard about what. “She definitely feels like she’s lost a sparkle, and he’s a mirror to her,” says Clark. “She can’t ignore the parts of it that have changed.” Clark recalls discussing with Bower that there should be zero “feeling of ownership between them.” When I mention this to Bower, he references the line from Breakfast at Tiffany’s to sum up this dynamic: “We belong to nobody, and nobody belongs to us. We don’t even belong to each other.”

Tolkien’s marriage and experience in World War I provided inspiration for this union, but it was The Magnetic Fields song “Abigail, Belle of Kilronan” that Clark says she became obsessed with while filming. “It’s about someone going off to war, and it’s like, ‘And when I come home, if I come home, you’ll be a grown woman. And when I come home, if I come home, don’t be alone, Abigail, Belle of Kilronan,’” Clark says. “That’s almost been her biggest betrayal of him that she allowed herself to be lonely for so long, which I found really beautiful.”

One person Galadriel was close to before knowing his real identity was Sauron. Although she vocalizes that she doesn’t want Sauron present in this moment—not even uttering his name—the image of him lying next to her is a jolt back to reality, even if he is a figment of her imagination. “Even though in many ways her dreams are coming true with being reunited with Celeborn,” Clark says, “they can’t live in a simple vacuum of happiness. The world is still out there, and she’s part of the world.” Unfortunately for Galadriel, as she quickly backs away, she falls from the tree, alerting the nearby orcs to their presence.

The vision seems to be Galadriel’s own doing; Sauron is living rent-free in her mind instead of intentionally popping into her dreams. Vickers discussed this approach with Clark. “The way I play it was determined by what she [Clark] had in her mind—because it’s Galadriel’s experience, it’s not Sauron’s experience—and that was to be the scariest I could be in Galadriel’s mind,” Vickers says.

When I met Vickers on set, he was in costume, grabbing a juice from craft services before joining Clark up in the tree nest. Having spent a lot of time on a nearby soundstage, where Sauron’s bleak Barad-dûr lair is located, the Australian actor was happy to experience this change of scenery. “Barad-dûr this season is incredibly intense. It was 35 degrees [95°F] on the days we were filming there,” Vickers says over Zoom. “There was a lot of sweat. It was wonderful to go into the forest on a soundstage.” Still, there were some logistical issues for Vickers up in the nest, first finding the same eyeline to match Bower’s sleeping position and then trying not to stab Clark with Sauron’s pointy gloves. “Lying down in all that stuff is incredibly challenging,” Vickers says.

Of course, you don’t see this human behavior in the final cut. “It’s a very particular thing to film something fantasy in this way because the look is so important,” Clark says. “It does matter that their hair looks absolutely lovely in that light, and they move like they’re artists with their bodies.” This includes scenes that might require a stunt double, like the one I observed. “Your stunt double is always a huge part of creating the character, but I think more so than ever with an elf, because she’s got to be excellent,” Clark says. “We do as much as we can with me being as excellent as I can be.” For everything else, Clark’s stunt double Hannah Grant (who has a ballet and martial arts background) steps in.

While the version of Sauron in this scene is a figment of Galadriel’s imagination, the real Dark Lord is traveling to the Eryn Galen woodland. Celeborn sacrifices himself to stop his wife (who is lying unconscious nearby) from being discovered. What comes next will likely be incredibly unpleasant, but Bower enjoyed going head-to-head with Vickers as the twisted trio enters its next phase.

“It’s where sparks can fly,” Bower says. “You need to build a rapport, have a friendship, a comfortability, and a trust in one another. Charlie made that so easy from day one.” However, Sauron won’t be as accommodating. “Celeborn has something that Sauron really needs,” Vickers says. “It’s the key to his whole existence up to this point, so you have all the emotional interplay, but then beneath that you have this real psychological drive, which is, ‘I will do anything. I will not stop. I will do all I can to get this secret out of you.’” It seems unlikely that Celeborn, Galadriel, or Sauron will get any sleep anytime soon.

#6907. How Tiffany & Co. Made a Bird on a Rock Box

Can inspiration be infinite? Yes, if you know where to look.

Above: Tiffany & Co. Singing Bird on a Clock and Bird on a Rock brooch, 800-843-3269.

How does a yellow cockatoo flitting near an open window in Guadeloupe end up a jewelry icon? That bird knew what it was doing when it perched outside the home of Jean Schlumberger, one of Tiffany & Co’s best-known jewelry designers. Nature was always the apple of Schlumberger’s eye, and in his vision that bird would become a diamond-studded feathered friend nestled in lapis lazuli and pinned on Bunny Mellon’s beret (he would go on to make for her a full, whimsical bestiary. Theirs was a partnership of hundreds of jelly fish, camels, and seahorses, plus the beloved fruit and flowers inspired by Mellon’s own garden).

That was 1965. How to keep reaching new heights? A partnership between Tiffany & Co. and the Musée des Arts Décoratifs (MAD) in Paris offers inspiration: An archive of thousands of Schlumberger’s sketches, now being restored and digitized, reveal a mind constantly at work and a vision without boundaries. And so it continues, in spessartine and chrysoprase, in rubellites and mother-of-pearl, and turquoise. The next reincarnation of the bird, though, might be one of the most exciting horological spectacles of the year: a glimmering, singing automata atop a burst of sun, a singing gold and titanium bird on a clock. Though, we must note, it does not play music: instead, it tweets. Look, and listen.

This story originally appeared in the November 2026 issue of ELLE Decor.

Editor-in-Chief Stellene Volandes is a jewelry expert, and the author of Jeweler: Masters and Mavericks of Modern Design (Rizzoli).

#6898. Only Murders in the Building Season 6: Everything We Know So Far

Though Only Murders in the Building wrapped its fifth season in October 2025, the Hulu hit is hardly slowing down. The series is heading toward its next chapter with awards-season momentum after earning Golden Globe nominations for Best Television Series – Musical or Comedy, as well as best performance nods for stars Selena Gomez, Steve Martin, and Martin Short.

The final episode of season 5, which ended with the suspicious death of Cinda Canning, left fans on a major cliffhanger that begs for a deeper investigation. Here’s everything we know so far about the future of the show.

Yes! The news came shortly after the season 5 finale premiered. According to The Hollywood Reporter, the forthcoming season will officially switch locations and be filmed in London. The previous season’s finale ended with the trio listening to a new Cinda Canning podcast set across the pond. That, along with Canning’s mysterious death at the end of the episode, may hint at why the group could travel to the U.K.

Selena Gomez previously stated that she hopes Only Murders continues for many seasons to come. “I really hope that I can just keep evolving Mabel as I get older, because I want to do this for a long time,” she told USA Today in September 2025.

Steve Martin also felt optimistic about a sixth season. “We believe we’re doing another season,” he said. “We think so. But you have to live season by season. And even if it was canceled tomorrow, we would have to say, ‘We had a great, great run. We loved what we were doing. It was a great way to spend five, six years.’”

In May 2026, Gomez shared that filming had officially begun when she reposted a photo from the show’s Instagram account to her Instagram Story. The image, showing Gomez alongside some of the cast members together in London, was captioned, “Season 6 🇬🇧 Day 1!”

Just like previous seasons, season 6 is confirmed to have 10 episodes.

Only Murders in the Building season 6 is taking place in London, as well as a select few countryside locations in England. In an August 2026 interview with Vulture, showrunner Josh Hoffman teased, “one of the more signature episodes is called ‘The Country House,’ and that sits in a very specific vibe and look, and quite a collection of characters.” He added, “We got about an hour outside of London…and into some of the stately homes.”

Season 5 concluded with Cinda Canning (Tina Fey) dropping dead in front of the Arconia, the same night that her new U.K.-set podcast The Girl With the Curls aired. Given the proximity, Charles, Oliver, and Mabel set out to solve her murder. “It was technically a murder in the building because she did it with her last gasp,” Hoffman told Vulture. “But the question is, what was going on with her? That is what our trio is off to investigate.”

Specific plot points will remain under wraps until its premiere, but Hoffman revealed that Charles’s Brazzos television show is getting a reboot in the U.K. “Martin Freeman will go toe-to-toe at some point with Charles as to who’s the better Brazzos,” he shared.

The show will also be taking place during the holiday season. “With the show airing at the top of December, it was impossible to avoid,” Hoffman added. “Within the story, we are moving towards the end of the year. The end of last season was fall, so we had to carry through on that.” The sixth season was shot in an uncharacteristically hot summer, but the show did its best to capture an English winter “that feels a bit rainy and a bit cloudy. There will be a bit of a lean into the holiday season by the end of it all.”

Selena Gomez, Steve Martin, and Martin Short have returned for another season. Season 5 featured newcomers Renée Zellweger and Logan Lerman, while Keegan-Michael Key was elevated to a recurring role. It’s not yet clear whether these supporting characters, along with Meryl Streep and Da’Vine Joy Randolph, will return for season 6, but we’ll stay tuned.

Season 6 has also added several notable new faces. In June 2026, Deadline reported that David Tennant, Nicola Coughlan, Jodie Whittaker, Jim Broadbent, Richard Ayoade, Adrian Lukis, and Kathryn Hunter had joined the cast in recurring roles. By August 2026, Deadline reported that Doctor Who star Peter Capaldi will also be joining the upcoming season—Whittaker, Tennant, and Broadbent have all had roles within the Doctor Who universe.

Olivia Colman was added to the cast days later, with Gomez, Streep, and Short announcing the news via an Instagram video. “We have had so many amazing guest stars for Only Murders in the Building,” Gomez said in the short clip, with Streep adding, “We’re just missing one.”

Short jokingly pops into the frame before making way for Colman to appear alongside the trio.

Colman’s character will be a writer, Hoffman revealed in the Vulture interview. “The U.K. is the home of Agatha Christie. This is where Cinda Canning might have met her maker in some way,” he said. “And all of them are following along in the tradition of the cozy murder mystery. Olivia is in that line as well as a memoirist.”

Jennifer Saunders will also have a role in the sixth season, with Hoffman telling Vulture that her character takes significant inspiration from Vivienne Westwood. “I had an incredible time many years ago working on a screenplay with Brian Grazer and Ron Howard on the life of Vivienne Westwood,” he said. “So I spent a lot of time here in London developing that screenplay with her.” Though that movie never materialized, Hoffman used his research to inform Saunders’s character, which will “lean into the fashion design world in great British style, with a tip of the hat to the great Vivienne.”

Queen Camilla was extremely close to having a role in Only Murders in the Building season 6, with Hoffman telling Vulture, “We almost got Camilla...I know it’s insane, but yes, there was a moment.”

The Queen Camilla cameo never materialized because of logistics, but Hoffman “did pursue it a little bit, until I realized it’s going to be a whole thing.” Hoffman isn’t ruling out Prince Harry and Meghan Markle cameos, though—“When is she traveling? We’re leaving in three days.” He added, “I wouldn’t rule anything out.”

Season 6 of Only Murders in the Building will arrive on December 8. Gomez and the rest of her castmates revealed that filming wrapped in an August 28 video shared to the show’s social media platforms.

Yes. First look photos arrived on August 25, and feature Gomez, Martin, and Short back in costume as Mabel, Charles, and Oliver in winter-approved wardrobe staples.

The trailer for Only Murders in the Building season 6 arrived on November 17. In the trailer, Mabel, Oliver, and Charles try to find out the truth about Cinda’s death after seeing her leave her hand on the gates of The Arconia in her final moments. “Cinda died dropping a hand on our gate,” Mabel says, to which Oliver adds, “She clearly wants our help.” After discovering that Cinda was in London just before her death, the trio travels across the pond to London with the hopes of finding out who did it.

The trio struggles with getting used to how life works in England. Simple things like drinking tea prove to be a challenge when Mabel suggests that Cinda could have died from a poisonous pot of tea. The suspects—aside from Gemma Joyce, Coughlan’s character—aren’t immediately clear, but there are a host of individuals who could be guilty of the crime as the first glimpses of Streep, Ashley, Colman, and more characters are revealed in the trailer.

#6896. The Breakfast at Tiffany’s NYC Townhouse Sells for $11.5 Million

The historic residence traded at a $3.5 million discount.

Holly Golightly might have had questionable taste in men, but she had a keen eye for real estate. Memorably portrayed by Audrey Hepburn in Breakfast at Tiffany’s, Golightly lived in a handsome Upper East Side brownstone that had been subdivided into apartments. Though the 1961 classic film shot its interior scenes on a Los Angeles soundstage, the exterior of the New York City townhouse served as a backdrop throughout the movie.

In May 2025, the five-story residence hit the market for $15 million, following a total overhaul that included a slew of luxe upgrades, such as a new roof terrace and an elevator. Although StreetEasy listed the property as “in contract” last December, its official sale date is November 11, 2026. In the end, the two-family house traded hands for $11.5 million, roughly 23 percent off the original price, per Realtor.com.

Set on a tree-lined block between Lexington and Third Avenues, the East 73rd Street home is distinguished by its Tiffany Blue front door and matching window casings. Built in 1866 by architect John Sexton, the Italianate-style landmark encompasses nearly 4,500 square feet and has four bedrooms in total, three in the main residence—which spans the top four floors—and one in the garden-level apartment, which has its own separate entrance.

The seller was retired beverage executive Joseph Harkins, who acquired the property in 2015 for $7.4 million and spearheaded a complete transformation that modernized the interiors while still preserving the house’s 19th-century charm. The project even included excavating the basement level, which now has nearly eight-foot ceilings, a powder room, a second laundry room, and a wine cellar.

Outside, the 470-square-foot rear garden is fully enclosed by Walpole lattice fencing and sports stonework paving. It will no doubt be the scene of plenty of “Breakfasts” for the home’s newest steward.

Geoffrey Montes is an associate editor at ELLE Decor with a serious love for all things real estate and design. Before that, he worked at Architectural Digest, Galerie, and Preservation magazines, covering everything from jaw-dropping listings to world-famous architects and design events like Salone del Mobile and Homo Faber.

#6895. Why Everything New Looks a Little Vintage Right Now

Designers are adding depth to their ensembles by mixing and matching eras with irreverence

Backstage at the Spring 2027 Chanel show in Paris, after a parade of elegantly skirt-suited women strolled beneath an extravagant set of birds suspended midflight, Matthieu Blazy told a story of picking his teenage nephew up from school. Observing the students, he was struck by the irreverent way they dressed. “They wear a lot of vintage. They are not dictated by a decade—they are a lot more free,” he told Bazaar. “They wear something from the ’30s, ’50s, ’70s. I wanted to expand on that.”

One look in particular felt more akin to how someone would dress to attend the Chanel show rather than to walk it—an old-school Chanel jacket worn with new Chanel shoes, a pair of black trousers, eclectic jewelry and an old belt. It wasn’t an anomalous look; the vintagey vibes loomed large. Knee-length skirt suits were styled with heaps of necklaces. Men’s shirting was mixed with relaxed tailoring and leather separates; tiny white tank tops peeked from beneath tweed jackets festooned with oversized, floral brooches.

Loving and collecting vintage (or better yet, holding onto pieces until they became vintage) was once the domain of fashion insiders. But today, young people are growing up native to the secondhand market. (According to ThredUp’s 2026 Resale Report, 62 percent of Gen Z shopped secondhand last year.) As they age, dressing from a closet that contains clothing from multiple style eras has become inevitable. And designers are taking notice.

Just as the Dolce & Gabbana show was about to start in Milan a week before the Chanel show, the loudspeaker turned on to regale the audience on “the wardrobes of Sicily’s grand houses. Clothes once worn by great-grandmothers, grandmothers, mothers, aunts and daughters. A slip dress is worn over a jacket, antique lace meets contemporary pieces, lingerie and eveningwear coexist, and the precious and the everyday merge in an unexpected balance.” The styling made it feel as if a young woman pilfered grandma’s closet, mixing an antique, pussy bow blouse with embellished and ripped jeans or pairing a D&G baby tee with a leopard-trimmed, tweedy jacket.

At Gucci, Demna dissected the archetypes of the bourgeoisie, including “the rebellious aristocrat.” A series of looks paired variations of rugby shirts, pleated tartan skirts, puff-shaped bomber jackets with oversized belts adorned with horsebits and heaps of jewelry piled onto necklines. It’s what a “blue-blooded child of an aristocrat might wear to the private school that she hates,” Demna mused after the show. Within that layered ensemble, he defined a character: Her kilt is part of her school uniform; her rugby polo and leather jacket were mined from a thrift store or her dad’s closet. She wore all these pieces together with no regard for where or what era they came from.

The idea of dressing “vintage” on a runway is inherently contradictory. The runways are a place of new; vintage is, by definition, old. But the concept is more about adding depth and texture to an ensemble than it is about the age of the garment itself. It’s not about, as in seasons past, garments that are distressed to look worn and weathered. It’s about achieving a kind of idiosyncrasy in a landscape that had become samey and polished and “quiet luxury.” It’s about a desire for irreverence.

The Row is a spectacular case study here, particularly as it was one of the brands that ushered in the minimalist look of the early 2020s. So how to evolve without alienating your customer? For Spring 2027, the Olsen twins draped and knotted antique linens, lace, and doilies over their typical rotation of sleek, black trousers and perfectly mussed trench coats. Models wore layers of dainty beads or single chunky necklaces that nodded subtly towards eclecticism or bohemian.

Fashion must contend and engage with vintage; increasingly, it is just a part of how people shop. As the secondhand market gains traction, it presents new challenges; houses are in constant competition with their own archives. Just look at the resurgences of secondhand Pucci and Etro from the early 2000s. Many have embraced reissues: Chloé’s Maxime wedge or the McQueen skull scarf, for example, shown tucked into contemporary collections of recent years. Of course, creative directors at legacy houses have always had to find ways to be in conversation with their archives and predecessors. The greatest designers, however, manage to use this formula to their advantage.

Take Blazy at Chanel, for instance. He must contend with Gabrielle “Coco” Chanel’s founding legacy of the early and mid-1900s in addition to Karl Lagerfeld’s from the ’80s through the 2010s. The idea of a knee-length skirt suit in nubby fabric could seem retro, but Blazy leaned into it instead of pushing against it. He didn’t rewrite the idea entirely, but shifted our perception with subtle tweaks in materials, textures, and styling. He even sent Gabrielle’s original 2.55 bag down the runway, paired with a gauzy white cocktail dress and then again later with a black skirt suit.

Ralph Lauren mined his own greatest hits for a spring collection that was a glorious mishmash of old and new, clashing Americana Westernwear and prep with tropes of the English aristocracy, and even utilitarian workwear. He strung models’ waistcoats with pocket watches, paired brocade evening jackets with distressed denim, and matched ruffled-collar blouses with metallic skirts.

It’s that dialogue between items that conjures the feel of someone who wears vintage, someone who curates their closet with stylistic depth, nuance, and contrast. What young people achieve with aplomb is decidedly difficult to manufacture. But that’s what makes it so desirable. It turns runway models from mannequins into characters and makes you want to learn their stories.

#6885. I Just Finished My Kitchen Renovation, and This “Hidden” Island Feature Is the One Upgrade I’d Recommend to Everyone

It’s made my cook space so much more functional.

I’m always noticing something cool in someone else’s home and thinking, “Oh, I need that, too.” That happened last year when my family stayed with my in-laws. It was my first time seeing their newly renovated kitchen, and after a quick tour, one small detail caught my eye: the pop-up outlet in the kitchen island.

It was simple, practical, and exactly the kind of feature I wanted in my own kitchen. When we recently renovated our cook space, it went to the top of our wish list—and it has indeed been a game-changer with how we use the area.

Ahead, here’s why I love having a pop-up outlet, what to consider before installing one, and a few other kitchen gadgets that you might want to consider adding to your next upgrade.

Like the name suggests, a pop-up outlet is basically an electrical outlet that hides inside your countertop until you need it. Most models have a top you press or pull, and the outlet rises up so you can plug in your blender, mixer, or whatever else you're using. When you're done, you push it back down and it's out of sight.

There are a few different versions, including ones that pop up manually and others that rise with the push of a button. Some, like the one we have, include USB ports or wireless charging built in. The outlet connects to power underneath the countertop, so it's something you'll want to plan for before your new counters go in.

Like pretty much any kitchen gadget, there are a range of options available from budget to high end. We opted for something mid-tier that still included the main functions we wanted like the additional USB ports and the charging capability on the top. If you’re a chronic “let your phone get down to one or two percent before finally charging it” person like me, I highly recommend this.

There also are some that will go nearly fully flush with the countertop, and can even have part of the countertop material on the top of it to look seamless. We did not go this route, but if you prefer the look of a flawless countertop, this might be the way to go.

We put ours in the corner of our kitchen where it’s almost entirely hidden by decor. That corner of the kitchen was otherwise without any spots to plug in appliances, so this fixed multiple problems by having electricity access while remaining discreet.

You can really put them anywhere you want, but keep in mind where you’ll need extra power accessibility and where it won’t be an eyesore, if that’s a concern. I’m also a big fan of in-drawer charging outlets.

There was some back-and-forth about adding this pop-up outlet to our kitchen remodel budget. The outlet itself isn’t break-the-bank expensive, but there were extra expenses that went with it, like hiring an electrician and paying to have a hole cut in the new countertop.

Though we decided it was worth the expense, here are some of the pros and cons you should consider if you’re on the fence.

I’m always so impressed by kitchens that integrate small, helpful gadgets like this one into their design. Here are a few other popular additions that we either made or were thinking about adding to our renovation.

Maybe it’s just a me thing, but I feel like the harder it is to find an appliance or trash can, the fancier a kitchen is. The pull-out trash can was integrated into our kitchen peninsula, and we purchased a more expensive panel-ready dishwasher so it looked like a regular cabinet. An integrated refrigerator was almost twice as expensive as a regular one, so we did not go that route.

With the way our kitchen is laid out, we unfortunately have two awkward corner cabinets. For one, we upgraded with blind corner cabinet hardware, which was well worth it.

We did not integrate a bottle rinser into our current kitchen design, but we did in our last two kitchens and loved them. It got so much use when our two kids were babies, since we could quickly rinse out bottles. They’re also great for wine drinkers or anyone who hosts a lot of parties.

Admittedly, this one is controversial, as some designers don’t like this feature. But personally, we love the look and it’s next on our “cool kitchen wish list.”

He has written for multiple home improvement magazines and websites, drawing on his own experience renovating homes with his wife to create practical, inspiring content for homeowners and DIY enthusiasts alike.

#6884. Sam Heughan and Stephanie Bullock Make Their Red Carpet Debut in Coordinating Looks

Sam Heughan is making things red carpet official with his girlfriend, model Stephanie Bullock. On Sunday, November 15, the Outlander actor brought his partner to the BAFTA Scotland Awards in Glasgow for their first joint appearance.

For the occasion, Heughan wore a Siobhan Mackenzie navy jacket and waistcoat over a white shirt, paired with a blue-and-green tartan kilt and black lace-up boots. Bullock complemented his look, wearing a matching tartan shawl with a floor-length black sequined gown.

Although they have kept their relationship largely out of the public eye, Bullock was seen with Heughan at New York City’s Tartan Day Parade in April.

The couple’s red carpet debut coincided with a significant moment in Heughan’s career. At Sunday’s ceremony, he accepted the Outstanding Contribution to Television award on behalf of Outlander.

Heughan keeps a low profile outside of work. In a November 2024 interview with InStyle, the actor discussed how public recognition can affect his ability to fly under the radar.

“I think actors before social media were pretty elusive,” he said. “I love to people watch. I don’t like standing out in the crowd…I love sitting in a café or a bar and just watching people.”

He prefers not to share much about his private life because “I just think it’s who I am.” And keeping a little mystery helps with work, too. “I think an actor should disappear behind the character,” he said. “You shouldn’t be like, ‘Oh, there’s Sam Heughan onstage playing a character.’”

#6883. The 63 Best New Perfumes and Fragrances of 2026

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

We sniffed, we spritzed, we sprayed. Only the perfumes below left a lingering impression, earning them a spot on our annual best-of list.

Everything smells like vanilla. At least, that’s what perfume naysayers will tell you. And it’s somewhat true: If you’re judging by what’s popular, ubiquitous, or cool with the youth, then sure—vanilla scents and sweet gourmands are still trending.

But hundreds of other fragrance launches in 2026 deserve a spot on your vanity and a spritz on your skin, and many are far less sweet. Gourmands took a turn for the weird and fruity this year, with notes like lychee and rice milk popping up in otherwise innocuous formulas. Woods and ambers continued to reign, as did spice-forward and citrus scents with unexpected depth. Perfumers are meeting our demand for newness in increasingly interesting, artistic ways, and we smelled hundreds of their creations to choose the 63 winners of our annual fragrance awards.

See all the winners of the 2026 Harper’s Bazaar Fragrance Awards, plus quotes from our beauty editors on what makes these formulas worthy of your next blind buy.

“Perfumer Olivier Polge reinvented the beloved “Coco Mad” scent as Crush Absolu, a new juice centered around patchouli, amber, rose, and a lychee-grapefruit accord. ‘There’s something voluptuous, something very prideful, something very addictive,’ Polge says about the new scent.”

“The new iteration of J’adore was inspired by Rihanna herself. As such, it’s a sweeter, bolder take, with notes of honeyed jasmine, rose, ylang-ylang, and vanilla.” —JR

"Bazaar’s Beauty Commerce Editor, Tiffany Dodson Davis, describes Guerlain’s L’Heure Bleue Eau de Parfum as the “scent of sunset,” thanks to a blue-sky accord that evokes the moment the sun goes down and the air turns crisp, clean, and fresh." —KI

Burberry Goddess Amber Vanilla Eau de Parfum Intense

“Lavender, maple syrup, four vanilla notes—I feel like a dog hearing all my favorite words. The result is a warm, ambery gourmand perfume that’s perfect for the cooler months.” —JR

Armani Beauty Emporio Armani Power of You Eau de Parfum
