#6881. 5 Ways Warmth Has Shaped Interior Design in 2026, According to Experts

Warmth is more than a palette—it’s the secret to a home you never want to leave.

After years of cool neutrals and minimalist interiors, there’s been a shift toward warmer, more inviting spaces that feel layered, personal, and lived-in.

“Warmth has become more than just a color palette or a particular material. It's about spaces that feel authentic, comfortable, and reflective of the people who live in them,” says Laura Gerber, founder of ODE Design.

At the end of the day, a warm home should feel inviting and effortless, with materials and details that make people want to spend time there. If you’re looking to add some warmth to your home, take inspiration from these designer-approved ideas.

Wood’s organic grain brings instant warmth to both modern and traditional homes, adding depth without making a space feel overly formal.

“We’re incorporating more white oak, walnut, and rift-cut cabinetry than we have in years, along with wood ceilings and paneled walls,” explains Rachel Simpson, senior interior designer at Revive Design and Renovation.

There’s been a shift from stark white countertops toward natural quartzite, travertine, and marble with warmer veining. These materials have natural variation and movement that bring character into a space.

“Honed finishes, in particular, feel softer and more organic than high-polish surfaces. They allow the beauty of the stone to speak for itself without feeling overly formal or glossy,” explains Simpson.

These days, people really want their homes to feel like a sanctuary.

“In a recent project, the client envisioned a grown-up treehouse nestled among the evergreens. We wanted to create a warm yet contemporary mountain retreat that felt luxurious but also comfortable and welcoming,” says Charles Greenfeld, founder and principal designer at Ethan Charles Design.

Rather than relying on one or two dramatic statement pieces, the interiors were designed to be experienced as a whole, with each detail contributing to that warm aesthetic.

Creating warmth is really about creating layers of thoughtfully curated design elements. This helps to create a home you want to enter and never leave.

“Well-proportioned rugs, textures on walls, loads of art, draperies, and lighting overhead and at eye level are just some of the things we can add to our spaces to make them feel cozy and accessible,” says Ashley Powell, cofounder and interior designer of Roseberry Allen.

Designers have lately been drawn to rooms where the colors are close to one another, but the materials do different things.

“A woven rug, a textured wall, and a piece of wood with a visible grain each catch the light differently,” says Jess Klein, founder and principal of Jess Klein Studio.

That variation adds visual depth and makes the room feel warmer and more inviting, even when the color palette is understated.

#6879. The Press-On Nails That Made Me Quit Nail Salons

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

As a chronic nail biter (gross, I know), I’ve tried just about every type of manicure out there in an attempt to heal my nails and kick my habit. From acrylics and tips to builder gel and dip powder, nothing has ever worked for me. But one simple option has never let me down: press-ons. They provide a natural-looking manicure without damaging your nails, take just minutes to apply, and can be done from the comfort of your own home. There’s no drying, intense filing, or pricey appointments required. What’s not to love?

Recently, I fell down a glue-filled rabbit hole in search of the best press-on nails out there. Every week, I’d apply a new set in the name of research, meticulously assessing each for quality, durability, and longevity. I have very small nail beds, so finding a size, shape, and length that fit my fingertips was essential. After about a year of testing (and an absurd amount of nail glue), there was a clear winner: Glamnetic.

We praised the product last year, and that praise still holds true. The application is not only simple, but I actually find it quite therapeutic. I start by pushing back my cuticles with the included wooden stick and filing the entire surface of the nails, not just the edges. Doing this helps the press-ons adhere better and last longer. Next, I match each of my fingers to the best-fitting press-on. Finally, I remove any oils from my nails using the alcohol pad, and glue on the set. I start with my pinkies and move toward my thumbs, placing a dot of glue at the base of each press-on and holding it down for about 30 to 45 seconds. Before I know it, I’m left with a perfect manicure that’s affordable, natural-looking, and long-lasting.

While it’s always fun to experiment with different shapes and lengths, my personal favorite is the brand’s “Short Oval” collection. For under $20, each set comes with 30 nails in varying sizes, and all of the above materials. They are incredibly durable without looking or feeling thick, and each set lasts about two weeks.

Whether you prefer a milky pink shade like Strawberry Icing or a more festive set like Snowfall, Glamnetic is known for its impressive designs. From magnetic cat-eye polish to 3-D chrome appliqués, there’s a set for every style, season, and mood. For a nail biter who has destroyed just about every expensive manicure imaginable, finding a budget-friendly option that can actually keep up with me feels like a small miracle.

#6877. 40 Years On, the Musée des Arts Décoratifs’ Fashion Collection Remains Ahead of Its Time

An anniversary exhibition revisits key moments from MAD’s history

On my first trip to France in the mid-2000s, the Musée des Arts Décoratifs (MAD) became my go-to destination in Paris for a fashion-museum fix. With each subsequent visit, the intellectualized fashion scene became more saturated, as maisons increasingly invested in their heritage with public-facing venues (Fondation Azzedine Alaïa, Musée Yves Saint Laurent Paris, and Galerie Dior), plus the opening of Palais Galliera’s permanent galleries. As far as I’m concerned, when it comes to fashion exhibitions, the more the merrier. But I remain loyal to my first love. For art and design lovers like myself, it’s hard to top MAD’s interdisciplinary approach—and, of course, that prime Rue de Rivoli address.

This fall, the museum’s self-contained fashion collection—the Musée de la Mode et du Textile (originally Musée des Arts de la Mode)—celebrates its 40th anniversary with an exhibition titled “Look: 40 Years of Fashion at the Museum,” open through April 4, 2027. While the museum’s central hall, the Nef (“nave”), pays homage to 40 of its 70-plus shows by spotlighting a look to represent each, the surrounding galleries invite visitors behind the scenes of an atelier and the museum itself.

“As the custodian of France’s National Fashion Collection, it was really important for us to show people their heritage, as well as the diversity of the collection,” Sophie Lemahieu, the museum’s curator of fashion and textile collections after 1947, told Harper’s Bazaar. Co-curating the exhibition with MAD director Bénédicte Gady, Lemahieu sought to convey the skills of the many professionals who made each garment a reality, as well as the museum workers who care for and prepare them for display.

The multilayered approach represents a consecration of fashion as art—a concept MAD’s Musée de la Mode et du Textile has been instrumental in transmitting since its inception, when such a notion was far from the prevailing opinion. “40 years ago, it was not obvious to have fashion in the museum, even in Paris. There were many curators and art historians who were extremely mad because all these floors could have been used to show Islamic art or other decorative art collections,” said Lemahieu. “It took time to legitimize fashion history as an art history field, but now every museum wants to have fashion exhibitions because they’ve seen how meaningful they can be. People identify with fashion because it’s part of our lives. We all wear clothes.”

For context, MAD was founded in 1864 and contained textiles and fashion prior to the official establishment of the Musée des Arts de la Mode. The decision to formalize its fashion collection was twofold: first, it marked the union of two monumental collections, the Union Centrale des Arts Décoratifs and the Union Française des Arts de la Costume. More broadly, during the 1980s, under President François Mitterrand, the French government began advocating for fashion as national cultural prestige, rather than a purely commercial entity (though it was undeniably one of the country’s leading exports). In 1982, the Louvre’s Cour Carrée courtyard became a designated venue for French ready-to-wear shows, making a powerful global statement. To quote Lemahieu, “Fashion became political.”

For the Musée des Arts de la Mode’s inaugural exhibition, “Moments de Mode” (1986)—which in “Look” is illustrated by a pristine 1760 robe à la française, the oldest garment in the show—MAD pulled out all the scenography stops. Working with theater professionals, curators presented an immersive history of fashion, including a mannequin, clad in a 19th-century cycling suit, in continuous movement on a mechanical bicycle. While today, such a display would never happen due to conservation requirements, it set the tone for MAD’s myriad innovations showcasing garments at their finest.

Another fascinating example from Moments de Mode is MAD’s usage of invisible mounts, which make garments appear as if they are floating. Visitors to Fondation Azzedine Alaïa may recall that these spectral mannequins have become somewhat of a signature for the institution. Lemahieu said this is not a coincidence: “Mr. Alaïa actually came to the exhibition in 1986 and thought this was an absolutely wonderful way to show a dress, so he asked the same person to do the mannequins for his foundation.”

Elsewhere are odes to MAD’s many monographic shows, from its first, Yves Saint Laurent in 1986, to its traveling blockbuster “Christian Dior, Couturier of Dreams” in 2017. Dior is one of the few designers to have multiple MAD exhibitions—another is Schiaparelli, who was the subject of shows in 2004 and 2022. Similarly, MAD staged two exhibitions about the 1980s—in 1989 and 2022. Lemahieu noted in all cases, however, MAD never repeats its approach to the same subject.

She also pointed to several exhibitions conceived by her colleague, Denis Bruna, curator in chief of the Fashion and Textile Department, who favors a more sociological approach. “The Mechanics of Undergarments: An Indiscreet History of the Silhouette” (2013), for example, explored techniques used by women and men to shape their bodies, while “Proper Attire Required: When Clothing Causes a Scandal” (2016), focused on controversies that marked turning points in fashion history.

Nearly all the garments in “Look” were previously featured in their corresponding exhibitions. The one exception is a tricolor bodysuit that Gilles Asquin created for the 2024 Paris Olympics Opening Ceremony (several young designers contributed looks for the momentous event). The garment, which the French National Olympic and Sports Committee entrusted to MAD, is displayed to represent the exhibition “Fashion and Sport, From One Podium to Another,” 2023. Lemahieu said the bodysuit’s inclusion demonstrates how museum exhibitions can open pathways for future donations.

Acquisition, documentation, storage, conservation, and mannequinage (the art of mounting a garment) are some of the internal museum processes discussed in the “How Clothing Becomes a Work of Art: Backstage at the Museum” galleries. Meanwhile, “Backstage: Creating Couture” focuses on inspiration, fabrication, ornamentation, and photography. It’s here where MAD can proudly show the range of its holdings (more than 150,000 pieces from the third century to the present day), which include preparatory sketches, fashion illustrations, embroidery samples, and more. Rarities include a mannequin upon which Madeleine Vionnet draped her fabrics, as well as pleating boards used by Maison Lognon, a historic Parisian pleating atelier founded in 1853.

Running concurrently this fall at MAD are exhibitions dedicated to the Thai court’s fashion evolution, Brazilian photographer Rafael Pavarotti’s cutting-edge imagery, and Marion Delarue’s wearable art created during her residency at the museum. Forty years on, this mix of shows, in scale and subject, has become MAD’s unique strength, as Lemahieu described: “It’s important for us to not just make bigger and bigger exhibitions. We need to be involved in what happens in haute couture, but we also need to be here for young designers and to be aware of what’s happening in fashion socially.” It’s that distinctive cocktail of programming—not to mention all the period-room interiors, jewels, and posters—that still keeps MAD at the top of my Paris museum list after all these years.

#6876. The One Kitchen Feature Designers Always Splurge on—and Why It’s Worth It

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Pictured above: Meghan Jay selected a brass chandelier with ribbed glass orbs to modernize this traditional kitchen.

The kitchen is the hardest-working room in your house, but it’s also one of the most expensive spaces to renovate. Depending on the size and scope of your redo, reimagining this space can cost up to six figures, so it’s important to be wise about allocating your budget. While some people may overspend on high-end appliances or really luxurious countertops, you should never overlook the room’s lighting. Turns out, that’s where designers always splurge in the kitchen.

“Lighting has such a powerful ability to change how a room feels,” explains Meghan Jay, an interior designer in Winnetka, Illinois. “You can have the most beautiful cabinetry, stone, and hardware, but if the lighting isn't right, the space can feel flat or even cold.”

Meghan Meehan of Hudson East Design in New Jersey agrees and says the right fixtures can provide height, dimensionality, and lots of mood to the space. “Don't think of lighting as the finishing touch,” she adds. “Think of it as one of the main design elements that makes the entire kitchen.”

Ahead, here’s why spending a little more on your kitchen lighting is worth it—and exactly how much you should budget for.

Every kitchen deserves to glow at full wattage, but it will cost you. After all, illuminating a kitchen isn’t as simple as adding a budget-friendly portable lamp and calling it a day: The best and brightest kitchens usually include several sources such as sconces, recessed ceiling fixtures, and under-cabinet lighting to illuminate meal-prepping zones.

“It needs to provide the right illumination for cooking and everyday tasks, but it also creates warmth and ambiance when you're entertaining or gathering with family,” Meehan explains. As a rule of thumb, she recommends homeowners allocate at least 8 to 12 percent of their kitchen renovation budget to lighting.

That said, anyone who wants to wow with some statement fixtures might wind up spending more. Jay, for example, suggests setting aside $3,000 to $7,000 for decorative wares such as pendant lights above an island.

Admittedly, spending thousands of dollars on lighting is bound to elicit some sticker shock, and some people may wonder if it’s worth it. However, Lauren Wodicka insists it’s worth the investment. “Unlike a sofa, rug, or an appliance, lighting doesn’t experience the same daily wear and tear,” the Northern Virginia designer reasons. “A beautiful, well-made fixture can last for generations and become the focal point of a room.”

Oftentimes, less expensive fixtures can read cheap. “You may notice overly shiny or artificial-looking brass finishes, visible seams, thin metal, or cheaper-looking glass,” Jay says. “Higher-quality fixtures often have more substantial materials, beautiful patinas, and thoughtful details that become more apparent the closer you are.”

If you want to get the best bang for your buck, devise a shopping strategy. Santa Monica-based designer Christine Markatos Lowe first selects functional lighting and then spends the rest of her budget on “decorative pieces with personality.” But make no mistake, a personable light fixture should still have staying power: Lowe often reaches for timeless silhouettes from retailers like Urban Electric and Ann-Morris Lighting and is a sucker for details like handblown glass or “beautifully finished metals.”

“The key to finding the right balance between statement lighting and a cohesive kitchen design is to make sure every element feels intentional,” Lowe explains. “It’s all about allowing each material or element to have its moment without overwhelming the space.”Wodicka—who calls Soane, Hudson Valley Lighting, and Visual Comfort her go-tos—says less is more. “Not every kitchen island needs two or three lighting pendants,” she explains. “Sometimes one beautiful fixture can make a stronger statement.”

Designers often say lighting is the jewelry of a room, so layering different materials, sizes, and finishes adds more visual intrigue. Wodicka wants kitchen lights to complement each other—and the fixtures in surrounding rooms—but they don’t have to be identical. “A light fixture doesn't necessarily have to match the cabinet hardware or plumbing fixtures,” Jay adds. “I often prefer mixing finishes to create a more collected, layered look.”

If you want to keep your fixtures cohesive—without identical finishes—stick with a particular style. For example, a sconce with pleated fabric shades will look out of place next to a midcentury-style pendant that looks like it belongs on the set of Mad Men.

For the perfect finishing touch, Meehan recommends putting all lights on dimmers, which is a simple detail that can make a huge difference. “This allows for functional lighting when cooking, but softer light to warm the space when you are entertaining,” she adds. And, if a small detail can make your investment work even harder, even better.

#6875. Ella Rubin on Her Banner Year: ‘It’s Out of Body in the Best Way’

The Sterling Point star shares her hopes for season 2 and the watch she’ll have “forever.”

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

It’s a good time to be Ella Rubin. The Sterling Point star has had the breakout year actors only dream of, after her role as Annie Jacobson in the hit Prime Video series launched her to viral heights. “It’s out of body in the best way,” she tells ELLE. “It’s a one-in-a-million chance to get to work as an actor, especially on a show that you love and you’re proud of.” Also on her agenda? An intimate holiday dinner with Chanel to celebrate its watches and fine jewelry.

On an unseasonably warm Tuesday night in New York, the French fashion house hosted ambassadors, friends, and more at Daniel Humm’s new, already hard-to-reserve restaurant, HUMM. As she got ready for her outing, the 25-year-old was as relaxed as ever. In a downtown hotel room, a scene unfolded that any 20-something woman would recognize: “Wide Open Spaces” by The Chicks played on a speaker (a song Rubin calls her “Annie anthem”), a light girl dinner of French fries and soda splayed across a table, and a friend on hand for moral support.

Rubin, an Upper West Side native herself, knows that for a Manhattan night on the town, sometimes less is more. The actress opted for a black tweed jacket—in true Gabrielle Chanel fashion—from look 40 of the Métiers d’art 2026 collection. Paired with a simple white tank and perfectly fitting Levi’s, “it’s my ideal dinner look,” she says.

In terms of accessories, Rubin wore a Chanel Première Ribbon watch, with diamonds elegantly placed around the face. “A beautiful gift,” she says, “and also something you’ll have forever, which is sort of the cornerstone of why I love Chanel jewelry so much, as it’s pieces you’ll know and love, and hopefully, give to someone someday.”

Despite not having her ears pierced, Rubin’s accessories game remains strong. The simplicity of Rubin’s outfit allowed for her jewels to truly shine as she finished off her look with stacked Chanel Fine Jewelry Coco Crush ear cuffs and rings in 18K white gold and diamonds. For glam, makeup artist Shayna Goldberg kept things natural and dewy, starting with a seamless base of Chanel Beauty Les Beiges Healthy Glow Foundation, topped off with the Les Beiges Healthy Glow Natural Eyeshadow Palette and Les Beiges Mascara. The finishing touch? Rouge Coco Longwear Satin Lipstick in 124 Marie and Le Crayon Lèvres Precision Lip-Defining Pencil in Nude Brun.

Below, Rubin reflects on her busy year, plots her holiday gifting list—Chanel included, of course—and reveals who she’d most like to guest star on season 2 of Sterling Point. Someone get Julia Roberts on the phone, stat!

I’m in a really lovely structured jacket, with this high kind of collar, and really beautiful buttons. They’re just so ornate and detailed. I find the jacket to be incredible, [and] my favorite kind of thing to wear. And then, I’m in just some Levi’s and a heel. Because of the high neck, we went with a ponytail, sort of like, a 60’s influence. And then the jewels, part of the reason for the ponytail is because I’m wearing some ear cuffs, which…I don’t actually have my ears pierced. So, I’m really glad that I’m getting to explore that side of jewelry. And then, I love a watch, and I love rings. Those are sort of my staples, my favorites.

It almost feels too late for me. I did it when I was a kid. And they closed up immediately, because I played soccer, and I just wasn’t committed. Now we’re just rocking with it, and it almost feels like, “Okay, this is just where we're at, and where we're staying.” But because Chanel makes such wonderful clip-ons, I get the opportunity to wear lovely earrings now.

Oh, my God. All of them. So many of them. Some of the cuffs I’m wearing tonight, honestly, luckily, they don’t need me to have my ears pierced for them, but if I had to, I would.

Something that’s comfortable, that feels like a part of you, and a part of your outfit. Something where you feel like yourself, it’s an extension of you.

There is this Coco Crush ring that I have now, very, very luckily. It’s on one side, just metal. And then, on the other side, it’s diamonds. And it’s just shiny and beautiful, and lovely, and it feels both, really simple and casual, and also, a party in the back.

That’s a great question. I think a watch is an incredible gift. I’ve had the pleasure of wearing a Chanel watch tonight, and on the show I’m on. It feels just like an incredible staple that, of course, also has utility, and it’s really classic and useful, and a beautiful gift.

It’s just so wild. It’s very surreal. It’s out of body in the best way, but also, I feel unexpectedly grounded, because I’m surrounded by wonderful people, but also, it means I get to work more, hopefully, and be more committed to the work I am doing, and feel more self-assured within it. So that's really the ultimate goal. I just feel unbelievably fortunate to be in the position to keep working. So, I guess what I make of it is just trying to really not let it get old in any way, and keep enjoying myself, and feeling as grateful and in my body as I possibly can, which I do. So it's pretty wonderful. It's solely wonderful, yeah. I’m just going to choose to keep enjoying it, and sharing it with the people I love.

Always Sex in the City, of course. I watch a lot of movies. I recently saw Wild Horse Nine. I think, for fall and winter, going to the theater is the best activity. It’s my favorite thing to do in the world, but especially as it’s getting colder, it’s just an incredibly cozy, wonderful time to go do that kind of thing. I’ve been in the theater almost every single day. Catch me at any AMC or Film Forum.

I’m obsessed with Alison Oliver. Maybe we could discover another sister, and it’s a biological sister, and it’s Annie’s sister. I’m just desperate to work with so many actors. So I’m thinking of Julia Roberts, Viola Davis. I’m naming all my favorite actors in the world. Oh, my God. Susannah Perkins. I just did God of the Woods with them. They’re fantastic. I would do anything to work with them again.

Get ready for more laughing and crying. Season two is still being figured out, so there's so much I don't even know, but I will say I think we're going to only become more intimately close with these characters, and know them more deeply.

#6873. The “Scottish Country House” Look Is the Cozy Interior Trend Designers Say to Know This Season

You don't need a Highland cottage to nail this heritage-inspired look.

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

You don't need a sprawling stone cottage in the Highlands, a sweeping countryside view, or a roaring fireplace to bring a little Scottish country-house charm into your home. In fact, the appeal of this cozy, heritage-inspired aesthetic is that it can work just as beautifully in a city apartment as it does in a centuries-old estate.

The secret is mixing those old-world details with pieces that feel fresh and livable, so your home doesn't end up looking like a period drama set. A tartan runner on the dining table, a cashmere throw draped over your favorite reading chair, or a few leather accents on the coffee table can bring just the right amount of countryside character to an otherwise modern space. Strathberry's new tartan homeware collection is another lovely entry point, giving the traditional pattern a contemporary spin that feels right at home in all kinds of interiors.

Whether you're fully embracing your countryside daydreams or just looking to make your space feel a little warmer and more layered this winter, there are plenty of ways to bring the look home. Read on for the Scottish country-house-inspired touches that make this aesthetic so charming—and how to work them into your own space.

If there's one pattern that deserves a starring role in a Scottish country-inspired home, it's tartan. And you don't have to limit it to a throw blanket or a few accent pillows. Designer Kaitlin Smith recommends “using plaid for a wallpaper or even as an accent pillow” to bring that distinctly Scottish feel into a space.

For a bolder take, try a tartan wallpaper in a powder room, entryway, or tucked-away reading nook. If you'd rather ease into the look, a single plaid pillow or upholstered accent piece can make just as much of a statement. With so many different tartan patterns and colorways to choose from, you can find one that brings a little heritage-inspired character to your home without making the whole room feel like a theme.

A Scottish country house should feel like it's been lived in for generations, not styled within an inch of its life. That's where woven baskets come in.

Noel Gatts, a designer and HGTV personality, recommends introducing “some slouchy woven baskets” throughout the home, whether at the entryway for shoes and umbrellas or in a living room or bedroom to hold extra blankets. Their slightly relaxed shape and natural texture add to that collected, lived-in feeling while giving everyday clutter somewhere to go.

Try a larger basket beside the front door, a smaller one tucked under a console or an oversized version next to your favorite reading chair. It's a practical detail that also brings in the natural materials that make this aesthetic feel so grounded. Plus, having a cozy throw within arm's reach is never a bad idea.

You can practically picture a pot of tea warming by the fire in a Scottish country house, and Gatts recommends leaning into that feeling with a thoughtful arrangement of crockery and candles.

“Feature a fireplace or dining table with tea pots, earthenware, and an installation of candles and/or candlesticks,” she suggests. This is a lovely opportunity to bring out those pieces you might otherwise save for special occasions, from vintage teapots and handmade ceramic bowls to mismatched candlesticks and well-loved serving dishes. Grouping them together creates a charming focal point without making the space feel overly formal.

Candlelight is especially important here, adding a soft, welcoming glow that feels worlds away from bright overhead lighting. And if you don't have a fireplace, a dining table, mantel or console can give you the same cozy effect.

A Scottish country house shouldn't feel too coordinated, so don't be afraid to bring a few different patterns into the mix. As designer Sarah Cole suggests, “try pairing a wool plaid with a botanical print, a small geometric pattern or a traditional rug.”

The combination adds visual interest and gives a room that layered, lived-in quality that makes country-house interiors so inviting. You can keep the look cohesive by pulling a few warm, autumnal colors throughout the patterns—like russets, burgundies, forest greens and ochres—while varying their scale so they don't compete with one another. It's a simple way to make the space feel more personal and collected over time.

The Scottish country-house look has a wonderful appreciation for pieces that feel like they've been around awhile. Rather than filling every surface with brand-new accessories, Gatts suggests bringing in “found objects with a sense of history” made from weathered wood, pottery, or patinated brass. These are the kinds of details that give a room its personality, whether it's an old wooden box on a coffee table, a vintage ceramic vessel or a brass object with a little wear and patina.

Don't worry about everything matching, either. A mix of materials and finishes makes the space feel more authentic and less like you ordered the entire room in one shopping trip. Gatts recommends using these pieces on a coffee table for “the perfect antique flair,” but a bookshelf, sideboard, or entryway console would work just as well.

Finally, a few well-chosen antique details can give a room that Highland lodge feeling without requiring a full redesign. Jill Wheeler of Birch Hill Interiors recommends adding “a touch of polished brass like antique candlesticks or wall sconces to give the space a refined look.” Try styling a pair of vintage brass candlesticks on a mantel, placing one on a coffee table, or swapping in brass wall sconces beside a favorite reading chair.

Wheeler also suggests incorporating antiques on a mantel, coffee table, or built-in, noting that “antiques can help bring the outside in and tell a story.” The mix of aged finishes and meaningful finds adds a sense of history and character that makes a Scottish country-inspired space feel genuinely lived in.

#6871. Frog Closures Are the Unexpected Detail Defining Fall Jackets

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

It’s all in the details, from intricate knots to elaborate braiding

There are plenty of ways to make a statement with your outerwear, from an oversize silhouette to a high collar or bold color. But this fall, designers are turning their attention to a smaller detail: the fastening. Jackets featuring decorative knots, elaborate loops, and ornate toggles have emerged as one of the season’s biggest outerwear trends, offering an alternative to standard buttons and zippers.

In particular, frog fastenings are having a resurgence, bringing a fanciful finishing touch to everything from minimalist wool peacoats to tailored military-inspired jackets. Typically constructed from fabric or braided cord, the fasteners consist of a knotted button and a loop. Originally designed to secure garments, the closures have evolved into a more ornamental role with intricate shapes and floral-like designs.

While they may seem new for the season, frog-fastener jackets have a history that spans centuries. In Chinese dress, knotted closures known as pankou have long appeared on garments such as the qipao and tangzhuang. By the 18th and 19th centuries, frogging had also become a feature of European military uniforms, worn by cavalry regiments whose fitted jackets were adorned with braided cords.

Fashion has revisited the detail since then. Oscar de la Renta incorporated frog fastenings into his designs in the 1990s, while Valentino explored versions for its fall 2015 couture collection. More recently, for fall 2026, designers reinterpreted knotted closures: At Kallmeyer, black toggles punctuated a gilded jacquard jacket, while Kim Shui incorporated the detail into fur-trimmed blazers and opera jackets. Meanwhile, labels like Khaite and Ann Demeulemeester embraced military-inspired looks featuring ornamental braiding and embellished fronts.

Today’s store-rack versions range from collarless blazers with understated knots to dramatic outerwear designs featuring detailed cording. Brands like Róhe have introduced contemporary takes on frogging, while Hong Kong–based fashion label Sau Lee has reimagined traditional Tang-style jackets with modern silhouettes.

Rather than reinventing jackets entirely, designers are proving that something as minuscule as a fastening can make a familiar silhouette feel new again for the season. Ahead, shop our favorite takes on the look.

#6868. High-Gloss Furniture Will Be Everywhere in 2027—Here's How Designers Make It Feel Fresh

A single lacquered piece can make a neutral room feel instantly more interesting.

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

After years of quiet luxury, soft neutrals, and interiors that favor understated details, a little drama feels like a very welcome change. High-gloss furniture is making a comeback, bringing shine, color, and a bit of personality back into our homes. Whether it's a lacquered accent table, a sculptural console, or a statement cabinet with a reflective finish, these pieces have a way of catching your eye and making even the simplest room feel more interesting. And honestly, who says every piece of furniture has to blend into the background?

The look also ties into Neo Deco, a modern take on Art Deco that brings together glamorous materials, bold silhouettes, and a fresh approach to decorating. But you don't need to turn your living room into a scene from The Great Gatsby to get in on it—a single glossy piece can add just the right amount of contrast to a neutral space, while mixing lacquer with natural wood, textured upholstery, or warm metallic accents keeps things feeling layered and livable.

Ahead, designers share how to bring high-gloss furniture into your home and embrace a little more shine without going over the top.

Not ready to go all-in on the high-gloss trend? An accent table is your low-commitment, high-impact way in. Tucked beside your favorite armchair or pulled up next to the sofa, a lacquered side table brings just enough shine to make the whole corner feel a little more interesting. The best part is the contrast, so have fun mixing finishes. Carol Alda, interior designer at Z Gallerie, loves pairing a glossy table with a “velvet or textured woven chair.” Just make sure the table sits at a comfortable height next to the chair so you can actually use it for your coffee (or cocktail).

Color is another way to make the piece feel right at home. Ali Esmaili, design expert and owner of Esmaili Rugs, suggests pulling the shade of your accent table from a patterned rug, which creates an easy connection between the two. An oxblood, emerald, or classic black table brings plenty of Neo Deco attitude, especially in a sculptural shape. It's a small swap, but one that can give even the most neutral living room a welcome dose of personality.

A little high-gloss feels right at home in a bar or entertaining area, where a touch of drama is practically part of the assignment. A lacquered bar credenza or sideboard in a saturated shade can turn your glassware and favorite bottles into a whole display moment.

Jennifer Fitzpatrick, founder of Studio J & Co., explains that high-gloss finishes bring extra energy to a room by reflecting daylight and catching the flicker of candles and lamps in the evening. In a bar area, that means your decanters, glassware, and cocktail accessories get their own little spotlight. Fitzpatrick recommends pairing high-gloss pieces with unlacquered brass, fluted glass, and textured textiles.

The coffee table is already the unofficial centerpiece of the living room, so why not give it a little extra attention? A lacquered version in a rich color or an unexpected shape can instantly make the space feel more interesting. And don't worry about all that shine feeling too formal. Fitzpatrick says today's high-gloss pieces are all about sculptural shapes, from curved profiles to clean geometric forms. A lacquered cocktail table can also catch the glow of your lamps in the evening, adding a little atmosphere without visually crowding your seating arrangement.

To keep the look feeling relaxed, mix that reflective surface with pieces that have plenty of texture. Esmaili loves pairing high-gloss furniture with vintage rugs, noting that “the gloss adds depth, movement and a touch of theatre.” The contrast between a shiny coffee table and a well-loved rug with plenty of pattern and texture gives the room a more layered, lived-in feel. Add a soft sectional and a few simple accessories, and you've got a statement piece that still feels right at home.

#6867. Harry Styles-Approved Designer Macy Grimshaw Just Won the 2026 H&M Design Award

Her off-kilter pieces have also been worn by Emma Corrin, Simone Ashley, and Anya Taylor-Joy.

Earlier this year, Macy Grimshaw presented her graduate collection from Central Saint Martins at London Fashion Week, styled by Harry Lambert, the mind behind the wardrobes of Emma Corrin, Alexander Skarsgård, and Harry Styles. Since then, the 25-year-old designer has continued to rise, dressing the likes of Anya Taylor-Joy, Simone Ashley, and several of Lambert’s clients—Corrin and Styles among them—in her semi-surrealist separates and top-notch leatherwork. Now, Grimshaw has a major accolade to cap off her standout year: the 2026 H&M Design Award.

At the presentation on Wednesday afternoon in London, Grimshaw was chosen among seven other finalists from top design universities across the globe: Chico Yu, Ennis Finnerty Mackay, Evelina Kryvopust, Fantin Delattre Pirenne, Jessica Lin, Macy Grimshaw, Yichun Liu, and Yvonne Schichtel. Grimshaw will receive a €150,000 ($164,655) endowment, a yearlong mentorship, and will have her collection produced and sold in selected H&M stores worldwide and online in October 2027. Each finalist was awarded a €10,000 ($10,980) endowment.

“She left me with desire and intrigue to see how she can actually develop the work,” said Beka Gvishiani, founder of Style Not Com, who joined H&M’s creative advisor Ann-Sofie Johansson, model Paloma Elsesser, and designer Ib Kamara on the jury. “For the moment, it’s very much the artist’s work,” he continued. “But she has ideas about how to make it wearable for daywear, because some of the pieces are great for an event or a performance.”

Gvishiani highlights the collection’s more experimental pieces, like a rusted, laser-cut mesh minidress ornamented with padlocks or a terracotta-hued molded bustier scattered with discarded cigarette butts. The scenography of the latter was inspired, naturally, by the brick wall where CSM students gather on smoke breaks.

But in between those more experimental moments, it’s easy to see why Grimshaw walked away with the prize, H&M’s first time awarding it since 2020. While fodder for celebrity placements and red carpets, her work also carries “hanger appeal,” clothes that are both wearable and appealing but also evoke something unexpected and slightly “off.” Like a texturized, scalloped suit jacket with a taut waist à la the “New Look” or the almost wet-looking crumpled silk slip dress done in a graphic, almost watercolor effect.

The line took cues from the designer’s college years in London (at 18, she moved there from Hong Kong to attend CSM) and speaks to her introspective way of working. “My whole practice is inspired by my grandmother having Alzheimer’s and trying to represent the fading of memories by using everyday objects and to recontextualize them,” Grimshaw told ELLE after the award was announced. “I want to remind us not to take the everyday for granted; that’s why a lot of my work is pencil shavings, cigarettes, locks.”

Next up, the designer will head to H&M’s home base of Stockholm, where she’ll get started on her collaboration with the brand. “This opportunity is going to be a massive help to make my work accessible to everyone,” she adds. “I’m very excited because this collection was not designed with the idea of commerciality. So I’m really interested to challenge myself and also work with other people to see how it can become that.”

#6864. Kylie Cantrall Wants to Be Your Modern-Day Carrie Bradshaw

After all, the rising star has a single named after the Sex and the City character.

Last night at the Moxy Downtown Los Angeles, rising pop star Kylie Cantrall teased her next project. “It’s gaggy. It’s giving 21,” she joked onstage during a small, intimate performance. “I actually haven’t written any ballads so far. I’m healed.”

Cantrall, who is perhaps best known for portraying Red, daughter of the Queen of Hearts, in the Descendants film series, has had a banner year. The L.A. native just wrapped a nationwide tour, appeared on Fox’s The Masked Singer, is set to perform at iHeart’s Jingle Ball, and dropped her EP Valley Girl Problems. A standout on the release is “Carrie Bradshaw,” which examines the similarities between Cantrall and the iconic Sex and the City character, played by Sarah Jessica Parker. “I think my fashion sense has been influenced by Carrie,” Cantrall says. “Also, she’s just kind of a mess, but she keeps it together on the outside. That’s honestly how I feel about myself. The choices are questionable, sometimes. Choices in dating, questionable.”

At the show, Cantrall played four tracks, including “Carrie Bradshaw.” She took pictures with her fans, signed vinyls, and, for one fan, even signed their chest. The experience is a part of a larger effort to use music as a connector at the Moxy, which has also hosted Ayra Starr at its London property. The brand unites artists with their fans in private settings and gives travelers the opportunity to relax with a musical backdrop. “I’m so excited to be here at Moxy,” Cantrall told ELLE before taking the stage. “I love doing these little underplays and acoustic shows, because it’s a different side of myself that I feel like people don’t usually get to see.”

Ahead of her performance, Cantrall sat down with ELLE to discuss Valley Girl Problems, her acoustic set, and new music on the horizon.

Full production, dancers, props. It’s always very razzle-dazzle, Kylie the performer, but I feel like this is more Kylie just the singer and songwriter. I like getting to showcase that aspect.

Honestly, I get nervous for it all. I think I put so much thought and care into every show, so there’s always a pressure I put on myself. But I feel like once I get out there and see the cuties’ faces, it feels chill. I know so many of them. We have more of a friendship bond.

It’s everything. What’s really special about me and my fan base is that we’ve really grown up together, because a lot of them are my age now. They had maybe started watching me from YouTube or from my first Disney show when I was 13 and have stayed along with my journey. It’s crazy. Our bond is kind of familial at this point.

I think they’re quite different. It’s so different playing a character. Of course, with someone like Red, I’m very grateful I got to play a character that I felt like had a lot of me in it already. But, Kylie the musician, Kylie the artist, that’s completely, 100 percent raw me. I have so much freedom to take risks and experiment. With Valley Girl, I feel like that perfectly encapsulated the person I was a couple months ago when I wrote it. I’ve changed so much since then. I’m just excited for the next project that’s going to reflect who I am now as a 21-year-old. I want all of my bodies of work to reflect the person I am in whatever chapter that is. It’s like a time machine. It’s very powerful in that way.

No. I listen to it so much when I’m creating it, and obviously it’s just nonstop before it comes out. The minute it comes out, it feels like I have no control over it whatsoever. It’s not mine. It’s for everyone else. I have to have that kind of separation. If I am still listening to it after it’s been out, I’ll start to pick things apart and be like, “Oh, I wish I did that. Oh, I could have done this differently.” Then, you have people’s criticism or opinions also in your brain when you’re listening to it, which can taint the way you view it. I was so proud when I finished it. I knew how I felt once I submitted it to the label and was like, “Okay, this is the final product. Here you go.” I just want to remain in that place.

It’s always the vocal fry—holding the ends of my words for a long time. Definitely the way I talk. I guess hiking and matcha are also kind of a worldwide thing at this point, but for some reason growing up I always felt like that’s such a Valley thing. In the Valley, it’s either matcha cafes or Pilates studios and fruit carts on the corner, and that kind of encapsulates the Valley for me.

So excited. At the top of the year, I wrote in my little manifestation journal, I’m going to perform at Jingle Ball this holiday. It was before it was even a topic of conversation. I just threw it out there because I was like, I really want to do Jingle Ball just to be a part of a lineup. I’ve watched every single Jingle Ball since I was seven years old. The fact that I’ll get to share the stage with Katseye, Olivia [Rodrigo], the Jonas Brothers, Adéla, these are all artists that I look up to. I’m just excited to be in that orbit.

I was probably enjoying my days off for the first two days when I got back home from tour. Then, the third day I was like, “Okay, I’m ready to get back out.” I’m already thinking in my head what the new set list is going to be with the new songs that I’m recording at home right now and getting ready for the next album. My wheels are turning.

New York had a crazy energy. The New Yorkers know how to pull up and show some love. And, of course, L.A. I got to end the tour here, so there was something that felt very full circle about getting to come home and celebrate with my friends and family that all came out to the show, and a lot of my L.A. fans who are here tonight at the Moxy. It’s also just so fun anytime I get to do anything in L.A. It’s all my closest fans, and all my first pop-ups I had ever done were always here in L.A. A lot of my foundational fans that have been with me, they’re all here.

I’m just taking a lot of inspiration from my life right now, as a 21-year-old who’s going out and just kind of embracing this next chapter. It feels like I have all the freedom in the world. I’m just excited to kind of show people maybe a little more of a wilder side. A little more messy, a little less curated. Kylie, outside of the work I’ve done or the image, the narrative, whatever that may be. Here’s who I am right now. I like to have fun. I like to go out. I’m going through it all the time, but I get to pour it into my music, whether it’s just discovering my kind of identity in this era, dating, or all the things I’m learning about myself. It’s all going to be poured into this record.

I’m a Cancer. I’m very in touch with my emotions at all times, and music is my chance to fully just, without censoring anything, explore that.

#6863. Inside a Denver Tudor That Blends English Eccentricity With Family-Friendly Design

Refurbished original details give the renovation a touch of old-soul charm.

When designer Kaitlyn Hoffman and her family returned to the United States after eight years in London, they had grown accustomed to the tighter quarters that city living often dictates. Needless to say, settling into a five-bedroom Tudor in Denver’s Cheesman Park neighborhood was a big leap. Built in 1900, the house was in good condition when Hoffman and her husband bought it, but earlier renovations had left it feeling at odds with its architectural style.

“The biggest design challenge was anticipating how we would actually live in the home,” Hoffman says. Because they renovated the space before moving in, she had to guess at everything from where they’d gather to how the house would work for their two young kids. “Ultimately, it was an exercise in designing for a life we hadn’t quite experienced yet and trusting our instincts about how we wanted to live.”

The nine-month project, which also launched her firm, Studio Proper Design, restored the home’s Tudor character. Hoffman and her partners at Alter Design + Build gutted the primary and powder bathrooms, overhauled the kitchen, and made smaller updates almost everywhere else. Along the way, plenty of the home’s charming original details got a second life: A pair of French doors found in the basement now anchors a custom pantry, and diamond-pattern mullions in the primary bath windows make the room look as though it had always been part of the house.

The result is a home that reflects Hoffman’s signature “Brit-ish” style: layered, collected, and not too precious. “I think the magic is in the mix,” she says, crediting her years abroad with inspiring a fondness for eccentricity, coziness, and humor in her design projects. Even the house’s lore fits the mood. Neighbors insist it’s the former home of ‘60s country-rock group Nitty Gritty Dirt Band—though Hoffman admits she’s never been able to confirm it. “Whether it’s true or not, it’s fun to imagine that these walls might have witnessed a little rock-and-roll history.”

FAST FACTSDesigner: Kaitlyn Hoffman of Studio Proper DesignDesign Partner: Alter Design + BuildStylist: Natalie WaradyLocation: Denver, ColoradoThe Space: A five-bedroom, three-and-a-half-bathroom Tudor across 6,345 square feet

A place where the whole family gathers and hosts, this living room is packed with personality and beloved furnishings, many of which have changed hands in the family throughout the years. While the decor is decidedly eclectic, the architecture itself is classic and stately. “We wanted this room to have the elegance of a traditional English drawing room, so we introduced egg-and-dart crown molding and a hand-carved Louis XV–style marble fireplace to give the space a greater sense of formality and architectural presence,” Hoffman shares.

According to Hoffman, this area saw a true “everything but the kitchen cabinets” renovation. Existing wall ovens gave way to a freestanding range with a custom hood, and a new pantry took their place. It’s built around a pair of original French doors she found in the basement, adding key storage while hiding away the appliances.

To soften the modern lines, she added a paneled backsplash, unlacquered brass hardware, and sealed walnut butcher block countertops for a warm, rustic touch. “I wanted a kitchen that worked hard for our family but felt every bit as inviting as the rest of the house,” Hoffman adds.

Though the dining room shares space with the living room, both areas needed to have distinct identities. To make guests feel like they’re entering into a completely separate room, Hoffman sourced several statement-making pieces. “I found all six antique Louis-style dining chairs on Facebook Marketplace for just $100, while the mirrored faux-bamboo armoire is a favorite piece we brought back with us from London,” Hoffman says.

Tucked beneath the stairs between the kitchen and living room is a clever nook for mixing up the drink of the hour. “This multipurpose space houses my husband’s (many!) coffee supplies by day and transforms into a bar by night,” Hoffman says. Like in the kitchen, she preserved the existing cabinetry and gave it a fresh coat of blue paint, new Negresco honed granite countertops, wallpaper, and updated fixtures. Now, it rivals the kitchen as the go-to entertaining spot in the home.

While gut-renovating this powder room, Hoffman fell in love with the botanical wallpaper, and she would’ve covered the entire space in it if it were a little more kid-friendly. “I opted for wainscoting on the lower half of the walls to protect the wallpaper from sticky fingers,” she says.

This is one of two generously sized guest bedroom suites, so friends and family will never feel short-changed when it’s time to hit the hay. It also has some of the most intriguing decorative details in the entire house. “The gingham four-poster bed is the star of the room, and I love the playful contrast between its bold check and the floral Roman shades,” she says. The wall accents are equally as enviable. “The framed Hermès scarf is another longtime favorite, which I sourced at Round Top over a decade ago, before Round Top was cool!”

Hoffman took a light-touch approach to her daughter’s room, knowing that the seven-year-old would eventually want to put her own stylistic signature on it. “I wanted to create a room that felt charming and playful without being overly childish, with pieces she could continue to enjoy as she grows,” she notes. Beyond painting the walls a pretty blush pink, the only real change here was adding custom Roman shades, which give the room a more tailored, finished feel.

This primary bathroom saw a full gut renovation to bring it up to the standards of modern living. Along with adding his-and-hers sinks and a freestanding tub, Hoffman took the opportunity to correct an architectural eyesore: the two mismatched windows that created irritating asymmetry. “We ultimately replaced both windows, incorporating diamond-pattern mullions in the upper third to complement the home’s Tudor architecture,” she adds. “It was a significant expense, but it gave us greater flexibility with the layout and brought a sense of continuity to the room.”

This guest room is an homage to the family’s life in London, as much of the decor came from their former home across the pond. The art, too, was sourced from a local printmaker whose studio was down the street in the family’s old neighborhood. Any new additions here were smaller and strategic. “The Schumacher floral fabric on the curtains and pillows became the thread that tied everything together, bringing color, pattern, and a sense of cohesion to the room,” Hoffman says.

The kids’ bathroom was reworked with total flexibility in mind. “With a son and a daughter sharing the space, I wanted a palette that felt playful and fresh without leaning either too feminine or masculine,” Hoffman notes. While the existing floor tile stayed, she added a custom double vanity so both kids can brush their teeth at the same time, as well as painted beadboard wainscoting to extend the country-home atmosphere into the washroom.

Kaitlyn Hoffman is the founder of Studio Proper, a boutique residential interior design firm located in Denver, Colorado. She’s a British citizen and lifelong Anglophile whose signature style was honed over nearly a decade living in London. Her design sensibility takes inspiration from traditional English interiors but adds a modern, practical twist.

#6858. Why Travis Kelce Didn’t Join Taylor Swift at the 2026 CMA Awards

Taylor Swift returned to the CMA Awards for the first time in 10 years this evening, but her husband, Travis Kelce, wasn’t seen there with her. Though the singer posed on the red carpet solo, she wasn’t alone during the ceremony—she was spotted beside her friend Brittany Mahomes, a fellow Kansas City Chiefs WAG. (She is married to Kelce’s teammate Patrick Mahomes, and they’re expecting their fourth child together.)

Kelce is in the middle of his football season. His next game is at Arrowhead Stadium on Sunday, against the Arizona Cardinals, which could explain his absence this evening.

This past weekend, Kelce left the field for part of the team’s game against the Atlanta Falcons due to an ankle injury. Postgame, he gave USA Today an update. “I’m all good,” he said. “I probably could have went more than I did when I came back, but it was the direction the team wanted to go, so I was right there with them. We had a lot of young guys step up, so it was really nice.”

While he and Swift are supportive of each other’s careers, the pair hasn’t made their red carpet debut at an awards ceremony yet. Last March, they attended the iHeartRadio Music Awards together but skipped the red carpet. Kelce remained by Swift’s side throughout the show.

In an October 2025 episode of their podcast New Heights, Kelce’s brother Jason asked him about being Swift’s creative muse for The Life of a Showgirl. The tight end coyly replied, “I’m just the lucky man that gets to be, you know, the support system for Taylor while she drops one of the coolest albums that I’ve ever listened to, that the world’s seen. I’m excited for her. I’m happy for her. Everything seems to have gone perfectly, all this planning.”

He detailed what it was like watching her make the album, revealing that he got to listen to it before it was released. “You see it from start to finish,” he said. “She started this thing in the middle of her [Eras] Tour in Europe. To see it all unfold and come together and her connect the dots—from the strategic planning to how the album was all going to look—then to listen to the music, and everything comes together and her finally getting that 12th song and being like ‘I think it’s done.’

“From that point on, it’s like her and her label are just working their ass off to make this one of the biggest releases of all time,” he added. “I’m just happy as hell for it because she’s happy with it. She’s proud of this and obviously I’m proud of her for going through the hoops to live out her dreams.”

#6857. IKEA’s 2027 Color of the Year Is Deeper, Sweeter, and a Little More Daring Than Your Average Neutral

You’ll want to bring this jammy shade right into your kitchen.

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

As a long year of colorful inspiration winds down, so many design brands are coming out of the woodwork with their 2027 trend predictions. IKEA has just joined the cohort with its 2027 Color of the Year: Wild Fig. Yes, the fiber-rich ficus fruit is setting the standard for the Scandi design destination.

IKEA’s interpretation of the jammy color is deep and dark, with red, lilac, and brown undertones. It’s more imaginative than your typical neutral, but still grounded in nature. “Wild fig weaves together the richness of a deep botanical shade with the personality of a color that isn’t afraid to stand out,” the brand wrote in its release. It fits right in with the earthy color trends we’re anticipating next year.

We’ve been keeping track of all the Color of the Year launches, and there’s a clear theme. Every pick so far can be found in your backyard. Valspar opted for the mid-toned blue of a cloudy sky, while Dutch Boy focused on the reddish-brown clay you’d find in the earth. And there’s been no shortage of greens, from Benjamin Moore’s pine-reminiscent pick to Sherwin-Williams’s bright celery shade. The trends reflect all the peace that’s found in a garden escape, and IKEA’s Wild Fig adds some sweetness and play, daring a bit past the typical tree shades to the fruit that blooms from its leaves.

The key to mastering this color, not unlike the fig jam on your charcuterie board, is what you pair it with. IKEA recommends pairing it with its 2026 Color of the Year, Rebel Pink, or an electric blue for contrast that brings out the character of the shade. And no matter your budget, there are so many easy ways to incorporate the look, from a patterned tray or tablecloth to a low-pile, checked rug. If you want to have some fun with your 2027 refresh, we rounded up some easy pieces to get started below.

MARKBLÅMME Low-Pile Multicolor Checked Rug

#6851. Consider the Pajama as Partywear

Here’s a little holiday dressing trick: Pajamas look great at parties when styled well.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Pajamas have officially left the bedroom and entered the party circuit. Soft, slouchy, chic, easy-to-wear pieces are everywhere on the recent runways. And some of the most interesting examples also come with a strong, literal interpretation of pajama dressing. But even for those who aren’t as daring, the runways are filled with slips, pajama-inspired tops, robe coats, blouses, and lingerie undone dressing. Luckily, fashion’s pajama fascination comes at the perfect time for occasion dressing season.

For fall/winter 2026, Sandy Liang’s jackets were refashioned from bedspreads and silky pajama tops were reinvented with major ruffles, then turned into dresses. The point of reference was a cross between Marie Antoinette and Kiki’s Delivery Service. Elsewhere, Ashley Williams sent out models wearing dresses with silky bed appliqués inspired by ’80s bedrooms and childhood furniture. Marie Adam-Leenaerdt showed neon reversible robe jackets and hot pink lace sets inspired by the kind of slips one might normally use as underpinnings or wear to sleep. “This season is about making with care. About transforming what already exists. About allowing imperfection. More attention,” stated the show notes.

What these looks all also have in common is that they feel like a new kind of pajama dressing through the female gaze.

The aforementioned pieces make a strong case for statement dressing on their own, with little styling needed. But if you want to go in a different direction, there is also ample inspiration from the fall/winter 2026 collections. Loewe, Rabanne, Stella McCartney, and Isabel Marant are just a few of the labels that did slip dressing with lace, embellished crystals, and surprising finishes like wax-coated latex in neon orange. Sheer lingerie dressing with a twist is everywhere you look in the form of lace skirts, lace-accented satin camisoles, and slinky little dresses à la Staud and Doen.

But even the humble pajama top has its place in partywear today. It’s impossible not to notice the many silky, vintage-inspired blouses that pull from the everyday roots of the pajama shirt. Dressed up in vibrant shades of turquoise or trimmed in feathers and paired with It shoes and a statement coat, a pajama top is party-ready even with a pair of jeans.

Designers clearly agree: At Dior’s spring/summer 2027 show, models walked the runway in soft striped blouses that mirrored the classic pajama shirt, redone with pizazz. With relaxed silhouettes, they were styled with rounded jackets worn open, then paired with wildly patterned bell-shaped skirts or metallic-textured pants. It was just one of the many examples of how comfy sleepwear-inspired staples work anywhere.

Dressing for a party means you can take some fun fashion risks. Take inspiration from your own sleepwear and the runway, but think less bedtime, more best-dressed.

Harris Tapper Camilla Rose Sheer Lace Midi Skirt

#6850. Designers Reveal the Fastest Ways They've Upgraded a Kitchen Under $100

No renovation needed for these designer-approved swaps.

As you whip up a weeknight meal or prep for a dinner party, you might find yourself daydreaming—and envisioning the kitchen you wish you had. While your current cook space might be perfectly functional, it just doesn’t have the aesthetics you long for.

Alas, the kitchen is widely regarded as the most expensive room to renovate. As Houzz reports, the average cost to remodel a kitchen is currently $26,500. But if you’re happy with things like cabinets and appliances (or a renovation is far off in the future), and you want to focus on just the visuals, you can give the space a glow-up with as little as $100.

It can indeed be done, and ahead, designers are sharing their best budget-friendly upgrades.

Rugs are such thrift-able items. A good rug is basically a necessity in a kitchen, and it can easily be swapped with a new one that refreshes the visuals.

Brooke Mennen-Talsma, principal interior designer at Brooke MT Interiors, shares that in the above kitchen she designed, she added a thrifted runner that she discovered for just $40, adding a focal point to an arched pantry.

She says to not underestimate the power of secondhand, and a lot of her favorite pieces in her portfolio are thrifted items.

Switching in fresh hardware for your cabinets is something that can be done in an afternoon, and it can be gentle on the wallet, too. You can also choose to add backplates to your existing hardware, as Sherrell Neal of Sherrell Design Studio recommends. She calls it a “quick upgrade that can stay under $100,” depending on the size and number of cabinets.

While new lighting in a kitchen can quickly add up, you can add light in other, more economical ways.

For instance, as Kira Young, founder and principal designer at High Tide Design, says, you can simply go with a “cute countertop lamp with a pretty little shade.” She advises specifically purchasing a rechargeable lamp, with no messy cords and no need for an outlet. It can also be easily moved anywhere.

Homeowners are increasingly opting for collected, and even pleasantly cluttered, looks in their homes. But if you really want your kitchen visuals to look polished and pleasing, Heather Kirk, principal interior designer at Kirk Riley Design, suggests highlighting a “hero piece,” or a favorite item that shines and steals focus.

Think strong statement pieces that offer both beauty and function, “like a cheerful tea kettle on the stove or a dramatic footed bowl on the counter,” as Kirk recommends.

Emily Spanos, creative director of Emily June Designs, says that in one kitchen she designed, she painted only the lower cabinets a bright turquoise and kept the walls and upper cabinets a soft white. Paint is one of the cheapest ways to quickly upgrade a home, and it’s something that can effectively be used in the kitchen.

“Painting one element costs a fraction of painting everything, but still highlights what you want the eye to fall on,” Spanos says. “A gallon of paint and a weekend’s work keeps you under budget.”

In addition to going with lower cabinets, you could consider painting an island, pantry door, the trim, or the inside of a glass-front cabinet.

To allow your eye to have multiple things to admire in your kitchen, go with a “layered look,” as designers call it. Think: linens, decor, rugs, and artwork.

Spanos says that she has accomplished this look for under $100 in a few projects she’s worked on. In one art-filled home, she carried the creative feel into the kitchen by displaying art on a couple shelves above the sink area. In another kitchen, she laid down a black-and-white geometric rug for visual interest, which tied into the matte black hardware.

She says, “A few quality tea towels, a small rug or runner, and a piece of art can all be found affordably, especially if you shop vintage or, even better, your own home.”

#6848. 28 Thoughtful Gifts Under $25 That Don't Feel Tacky, Useless, or Weird

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

You'll want to get something for yourself.

Gift-giving during the holidays is a delicate dance: you want to find the right item that feels thoughtful and cool but also doesn’t kill your budget or feel like something they already own. Our team is passionate about helping people find the right items, from hosting gifts to products you can bring to your next white elephant to even what to put in your family’s stocking stuffers. The topic we’re tackling? Best gifts under $25 that don’t feel tacky or cheap.

If you dig hard enough, there are actually plenty of unique, thoughtful, and fun finds for under this price point. Whether it be a martini-shaped candle, colorful oven mitts, or a deck of tarot cards, the variety of gift ideas ahead will work for your groups’ gifting needs. These nifty finds are proof that you can buy something that feels special without spending a ton. We’ll raise a glass to that.

For the person who always orders a martini, gift them this candle that nods to a dirty martini! It has notes of greens, herbs, and juniper to make their home smell fresh. However, they may not even burn it, to keep the wax intact for maximum cuteness on the table.

The sentimentalist in your life will appreciate a framed photo, especially if they’re moving away, just moved into a new home, or have been talking about filling their space with meaningful items.

Bedding and towel brand Hommey recently launched tableware, and the pieces are just as colorful and fun as their sheets. We love these two-toned bowls, perfect for olives, nuts, and other small snacks for gatherings.

Store-bought lighters aren’t the prettiest, so give them a case to hold their lighter in. This option from Etsy can be personalized, and we’re guessing they’ll want to proudly leave it out on their coffee table right next to their candle.

Everyone talks about their coffee setup, but can we take a moment to talk about a proper matcha setup? Their morning ritual at home isn’t complete without a fun bowl and whisk stand set, and this Anthro pairing comes in many colorful options.

With winter heading our way, a heated blanket is a must. This soft flannel option comes in a classic plaid pattern that oozes holiday vibes and has 10 adjustable heat settings ranging from 86°F to 122°F.

If you catch them watching tarot readings on TikTok and YouTube, you know it’s time to get their own deck. This one is perfect for beginners because it comes with thought starters and features cat illustrations for a lighthearted touch.

Whether they just moved into their first apartment or recently invested in an espresso machine, they will need espresso cups. We love this set because it comes with six earthy shades, so if they have a few guests over, everyone has a cup.

Not only does a pie bird look adorable in the middle of a piping-hot apple pie, but it makes it taste better too. Pie birds (or whistles) vent steam during the baking process, preventing the filling from boiling over and the crust from getting soggy.

We had to test this mini water bottle ourselves, and we found it's incredibly handy to take on the go. It's just the right size to fit in a pocket, holding your morning coffee or a drink for the beach.

For the friend who loves a vinyl dig or a new pressing of their favorite artist, help them keep their collection in tip-top shape. This cleaning kit stores easily in a record cabinet, and keeps even the dustiest records sounding brand-new.

To fill your new taper candle holders (and support a small business in the process), snag a set of these hand-poured beeswax taper candles. They add instant drama, and they're paraffin-free. Plus, they're unscented, so they won't clash with the scentscape of your home.

Art therapy can be a great way to release stress and celebrate your inner child. This takes that joy on the road, with a brush that you only need water to use, and a design that disappears over time. No mess, and no requirement to get every stroke perfect.

Fluted designs are back in, and this adorable vase is perfect to display a few choice blooms. The U-shaped design also works as standalone decor, if you just want it to be a striking sculpture.

Though this giant farfalle looks like the real thing, it actually smells more like a traditional candle, with notes of jasmine, neroli, and orange blossom. It also comes in a rigatoni shape, if that's more the recipient's thing.

Of course, you can gift a loved one a candle and a set of matches from the drugstore, but that's hardly any fun. Instead, grab a much more stylish version of the traditional matchbox that they can leave out on display. Worry not; there's a striker on the bottom. The matches, however, are not included!

Anyone on your list who appreciates the kitschier things in life will adore this ceramic soap dish shaped like a claw-foot bathtub. Just like an actual tub, there is a tiny hole for drainage so that the suds don't sit (and get moldy) in the basin.

As far as we're concerned, no one can ever have too many oven mitts. After all, they get stained quickly, so having a fresh, clean pair on hand is always a good idea.

There's no shame in being afraid of the dark, so we say embrace the fear and snag this charming (and portable) touch lamp. It gets brighter and dimmer with just the tap of a finger. It almost mimics candlelight, but unlike a flickering flame, it is safe to use all night.

It may be hard to believe that something so artistic is from Amazon, the place we buy our sponges, gym socks, and YETI water bottles, but here we are. You can rest assured that, unlike some of the other goods you may buy on Amazon, this bowl is not made in bulk. In fact, it's from one of the thousands of small businesses that sell their wares on the platform. How's that for a lucky find?

#6846. Supersized Fashion: The Rise of Giant Bags, Jewelry, and Accessories

Rosalía was recently spotted walking through the New York City streets with a Dana Toder bag over three feet long. Paired with her vintage pink satin ballet platform heels from Junko Shimada, the massive, hulking bag from the emerging designer was the kind of shocking jolt of life and newness that typical fashion paparazzi shots lack.

In case there was any doubt, the point of view was the bag. Supersized bags are ubiquitous right now: Chanel, Acne Studios, and Bottega Veneta all showcased their own versions for fall/winter 2026, but Toder’s took the idea to a new level with its extreme size and concept. Consider the bags just one of the many examples of supersizing the everyday. Chandelier earrings and oversize necklaces have never been so big at Saint Laurent and Valentino. Elsewhere, labels like Comme des Garçons, Vaquera, and Givenchy amped up their usual voluminous silhouettes even more for spring/summer 2027. Rising designer Beata Rydbacken is also making the biggest hair clips you’ve ever seen in your entire life.

There’s something undoubtedly beautiful about taking an everyday object and making it obscenely huge. It puts a focus on what some may consider the everyday or mundane and makes it sort of beautifully ludicrous. Dana Toder originally designed her “Baggage Bag” for Krähe, a fashion film by Lili Conzen, shot on 16mm, which will be released in spring 2027. Accompanied by exhibitions in London, Paris, Berlin, and Vienna, which will also showcase the bag, the film questions who gets to be seen in fashion and gives older women the leading roles. “When Lili shared the film’s concept with me, it connected really well with how I see clothes and accessories: as things that go through life with us and hold memories and emotions,” Toder tells ELLE. “I see a woman’s relationship with her bag or her garments as something very emotional and complex. The focus on older women felt especially meaningful when thinking about how those relationships develop over a lifetime.”

For Toder, it’s also a representation of metaphorical and physical baggage. The print featured on the big bag combines analog photographs from the ’80s to today, taken by the designer, director, and actresses in the film, and plays a role in showcasing the shared experience across different women, ages, and times. The bag itself was also made with the help of her 80-year-old leatherworking mentor, Shmulik.

“I’m interested in everything that becomes emotionally attached to a bag: the things it holds, the places it has been with us, and how the material changes over time. It might hold an object that reminds you of someone, or the bag itself might remind you of a certain period in your life,” she says.

“We carry all of that around too. I’m always carrying so much stuff, and I love the sight of a woman digging through her bag, with things falling out. I honestly think it’s one of the most beautiful sights. You’re looking for your keys and suddenly going through things that hold so many memories. Some might have been there for two years, or tucked away in your wallet for what feels like forever. I find something very emotional in that everyday moment, coming across parts of your life that you carry without really thinking about them.”

Seeing the familiar through a campy, oversize lens gives one both feelings of nostalgia and whimsy, and that’s perhaps why we’re experiencing so many humungous accessories lately. In the case of Rydbacken, in addition to her giant hair clips, she’s also made supersized wired earbuds, the biggest studded belt to ever exist, and jumbo pins, with many going viral. In fashion’s current game of scale, the bigger the accessory, the more room it leaves for memory, meaning, and a little bit of spectacle.
