#6843. Before & After: Two Best Friends Turned a Stripped-Down Tudor Into a Colorful, Antique-Filled Dream Home

Designer Anne Hulcher Tollett understood her client’s signature aesthetic better than anyone.

Anne Hulcher Tollett can’t remember a life without her best friend, Van. They met in a playpen as toddlers and quickly became an integral part of each other's lives, with many childhood memories taking place in Van’s house growing up. Tollett has been with her best friend through every milestone, including as her maid of honor at her wedding to her husband, Sean, and when the couple put an offer on a suburbanized Tudor home in Richmond, Virginia.

The five-bedroom house had good bones and an intuitive flow, but it needed help getting some of its charm back. When Van and Sean bought it for their family of four (plus their dog, Jelly Bean, and a small flock of chickens), it was whitewashed and lacked architectural character. In short, it wasn’t up to par with Van’s eclectic style, according to Tollett, but as the principal designer of Hanover Avenue, she reassured her bestie that she could easily take it from suburban chic to warm, layered, and personality-filled without having to completely rework the interior.

“Van’s aesthetic is diametrically opposed to ‘simple’ or ‘builder-grade,’ and although this house had been meticulously renovated and maintained by the previous owners, some of the charm she loves had been removed,” says Tollett. The designer and her team focused on lighter renovations and cosmetic upgrades, walking her best friend through every decision, from wallpaper choices to reworking the staircase to highlight the amazing treasures Van has collected on her travels over the years.

Throughout the renovation, Tollett and Van leaned into their sisterly shorthand, resulting in a project with very few disruptions. “Our biggest challenge was creating unexpected quirky moments without taking down walls or undoing all of the thoughtful and well-built upgrades that came with the house,” Tollett explains. “We found places on tables, walls, and bookshelves to highlight everything Van and her family have collected over the years. It makes the house feel like theirs and not the product of some design firm.”

Walls were covered with grasscloth, patterns were layered on throw pillows and vintage rugs, and anything that wasn’t antique or pre-owned was chosen to add a touch of patina to the space, allowing Van’s collections to truly sing.

Once Tollett was finished with the renovation, the house told the story of their friendship and a life well lived. Because the designer knows Van like the back of her hand, she was able to create a retreat that feels like a true reflection of who she is and how her family lives without editing out any of Van’s eccentricities. “We understood her design sensibility and took something well-built but fairly plain and made it fun, pretty, and interesting at every turn, which is exactly how anyone would describe Van!” Tollett adds. Besides, who better to design your house than the person who knows you better than yourself?

FAST FACTSDesigner: Anne Hulcher Tollett of Hanover AvenueLocation: Richmond, VirginiaThe Space: A 4,600-square-foot Tudor home with five bedrooms, four full bathrooms, and two half-baths

The barrel back and turquoise chairs were finds that Tollett sourced for Van’s previous home, proving that timeless profiles and accessible sizes can work in many different spaces throughout their lifetimes.

“A room like this with a vaulted ceiling and white walls creates a wonderful backdrop for bold pops of color and patterns because it can visually hold its own against furnishings with personality,” she says. “Van had the beautiful sideboard, and we loved pairing it with the tattooed table.”

Possibly one of the most remarkable makeovers in the entire house, Tollett coated the entryway in deep blue paint, topping it off with darling wallpaper on the ceiling for added dimension. “This is a great example of how a can of paint, a roll of wallpaper, and some strategically placed treasures can transform a room,” she says.

Large-scale grasscloth wallpaper instantly gave the once-all-white box character and rhythm, setting a serene backdrop for the rest of the unique finds in this dining room. The chandelier hanging over the table had long been on Tollett’s list to use in a project, and Van’s home was the perfect spot.

“The dining room is right near the front door, so it’s hung to look like it's being blown by the entrance breeze—a true classic with a twist!” the designer says.

Above the antique bar (whose natural patina plays so well with the wallpaper), are two framed photos of puppets, which Van found during one of her globe-trotting trips. She carried them around in her backpack, and Tollett loved the story behind them so much that she had them framed. On the opposite wall, behind the piano, is a portrait of Van’s grandmother in a vibrant green dress.

The kitchen was well-maintained when Tollett got her hands on it, but it didn’t feel all that fun, and it needed more than simple cosmetic refreshes like other areas of the home.

“We removed the big-box island lighting and reworked the storage, hood, and casework on the range wall,” Tollett explains. “We did the same on the wall opposite the range,” she adds, which was in fantastic shape, so they kept it.

To give the cook space more character, the designer installed a custom Delftware tile backsplash. The island was also in good shape, so Tollett upgraded the hardware, faucet, face frames, and lighting above it, opting for three Pinch pendant lights which bring an interesting, natural texture to the space.

“Open concept kitchens are tricky because they are seen from every angle, so we knew we needed to improve the storage to hide clutter,” she adds. Opposite the range wall, Tollett removed the open shelves, creating closed casework instead and topping everything off with pretty hardware and colors that play well with the adjacent dining and living rooms.

Tollett knew she was going to wrap this powder room in a beautiful butterfly-and-bird wallpaper, so she custom-matched a paint to fully drench the room in a whimsical emerald green hue. They even had their wonderful wallpaper installers cut out a few extra birds and butterflies to extend the design onto the ceiling, creating a more immersive experience. “We wanted the powder room to pack an equal punch to the bold, blue entryway it connects to,” she explains.

One of the mantras Tollett and her team at Hanover Avenue follow is “wonderfully weird,” which the hand sculpture extending through the wall to hold the soap perfectly captures. “The space feels not only more fun but also much more personalized to our clients!”

During the renovation, Van’s mother passed away, so Tollett transformed this almost-forgotten area into an homage to the matriarch. “We reused her beautiful plate rack and hung all her flawless collections of plates on the walls,” the designer says, officially turning this room, which overlooks the vegetable gardens, into the “plate room.”

They papered the walls in warm neutral grasscloth and hung relaxed Roman shades to softly filter the natural light, furnishing the room with many of the fabulous pieces Van already owned (and loved). “This is a wonderful example of ‘reimagining what you have,’ and finding ways to remember the ones we love in our own rooms,” Tollett adds. “Van’s mom was the coolest!”

“Decades of sleepovers in Van’s childhood bedroom allowed me to execute exactly what she was hoping to achieve,” Tollett explains. Though there are many layers of pattern in this sitting area, the designer managed to prevent it from feeling busy by keeping the same serene color palette for most of the textiles, minus a few pops of red for a warm balance.

“This room is a grown-up translation of Van’s entire design sensibility that began when she was young and was nurtured in her adulthood during her travels,” Tollett adds.

On the other side of the room is a calming wash of warm white paint and natural textures, giving Van a visual place of rest ideal for sleeping. Tollett calls it a “well-collected, well-traveled, layered space that seems to have evolved all on its own—a sanctuary for Van and Sean,” which makes the designer especially happy since she was the maid of honor at the couple’s wedding.

The primary bath really only needed some cosmetic upgrades, but they did a lot of heavy lifting. Airy Roman shades soften the space while Van’s mother’s bench and one of her existing rugs give it personality. By opting for two warmer paint colors and swapping out key fixtures, such as the lights, hardware, and faucets, Tollett created a serene, sophisticated escape that looks completely new, all with the same bones.

Tollett tapped into her childhood memories with her best friend when designing this room. “During slumber parties, Van and I fell asleep across from each other in twin beds, so it felt fitting to create another bedroom that fosters the same kind of childhood chatter,” she says, adding that stepping inside is like stepping into a time machine going back to 1983.

Along with the beds, much of the look was directly inspired by Van’s childhood bedroom, the round green pillow on the bed having been plucked directly from it. The pre-existing Tudor windows were perfect for the vintage vibe.

Tucked away at the far end of the home is a spot connected to the sunroom lounge. Dubbed the “dayroom”—an informal spot dedicated to relaxation and reading—Tollett wanted it to feel cozy and as collected as the rest of the home but still a bit private. They washed the walls in a soft gray-green hue and did the same to the custom day bed, finishing off the room with antique bookcases in a complementary gray tone. It’s easy for small rooms like these to feel overcrowded, but the pale color palette takes away some visual weight.

As founder and principal designer of Hanover Avenue, Anne Hulcher Tollett is intimately involved in every project the Richmond-based design firm takes on, from high-end residential to boutique commercial. Tollett finds a thrill in uncovering her clients’ design styles and endless joy in creating their dream homes that reflect who they are.

#6839. The Rise of the Maximum Visibility Bra

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

The summer of 2014, I was a college student working at a small-town lingerie store that lined its walls exclusively with neutrals. Every day, I sold women flesh-toned bras—black if they were really feeling kinky—all while filling my own intimates drawer with the same styles. Undressing in front of a guy that same summer, he voiced genuine disbelief over my nude, industrial-strength strapless bra. He wasn’t so much confused over its gravity-defying function as the fact that it was there, at all.

The early 2010s set a nearly impossible goal for young women: naturally perky breasts without any apparent scaffolding. But that’s all changing. Pop princess Adela’s exposed bras have become as central to her identity as her electric pink hair. Once left to jiggle freely beneath semisheer cotton, Charli XCX’s chest is now more often displayed in intricate lingerie. Several months back, Saint Laurent’s Spring 2027 front row was littered with boudoir-inspired looks while Prada and Fendi literally sent bras-as-tops down the runway. Bras have come out of hiding.

Somewhere between the “free the nipple” movement in 2012 and the impending #MeToo revolution of 2017, boobs became less of a sexualized spectacle and more of a political battleground. Victoria’s Secret canceled its fashion show as basics-driven intimates brands like Cuup and Arq gained traction for destigmatizing natural shapes. Then, breasts bounced back into cultural focus. The shift might be credited to the climate—conservatism generally propels women to take shelter in traditional femininity, or it is thrust upon them—as well as the twin forces of Sydney Sweeney and semaglutide weight loss. Either way, women got thinner while their chests grew larger—reflecting a beauty standard we hadn’t seen in a generation. Implants entered a new era of demand and dialogue, as celebrities like Kylie Jenner and Megan Fox transparently joined the big-breasted brigade.

But boobs aren’t just bigger—they’re bolder. Gone are the days of basics and bralettes. It’s all about va-va-voom underwire, satin, and lace—even push-ups have returned to the conversation. Still, “there’s a difference between showing your bra and showing off your bra,” discerns Tara Ocello, director of brand marketing at intimates brand Skarlett Blue.

Last month, the recently revived Victoria’s Secret Fashion Show broadcast Jenner strutting down the runway, her 445-cc-moderate-profile silicones outfitted in a record-breaking $50 million bra. Bella Hadid, Candice Swanepoel, and Adriana Lima showed off the iconic mall brand’s finest embroidered balconettes, lacy push-ups, and satin plunge bras. Angels aside, the reintroduction of these runway collections is also indicative of a shift in consumer product demand—a spokesperson for Victoria’s Secret claims there’s “strong interest” in fashion-forward details like lace, satin, logo hardware, and the brand’s statement shine straps. The Very Sexy Push-Up Bra remains the number one of the three mainline bras in its “Very Sexy” category. “Today’s customer wants a bra that performs beautifully and deserves to be seen,” says a Victoria’s Secret spokesperson. “It’s expanded how customers engage with [our bras] as both foundation pieces and intentional styling elements.”

The luxury London retailer Agent Provocateur has been positioning its designs as statement pieces since it launched in the mid-’90s—the last time exposed bras enjoyed prevalence. Creative director Sarah Shotton noticed the trend bubbling several years back on the Glastonbury grounds and immediately returned to the design studio. “We predicted this and designed [for it] about a year and a half in advance. I am constantly thinking, could it be worn as an outerwear bra?” she says. “I think if customers are going to invest in it, they want it to work harder than just in the bedroom. Lingerie as outerwear is just getting bigger and bigger.”

Category transcension is, of course, good for business. Victoria’s Secret reported sales up 10 percent in its second quarter, and Agent Provocateur has also seen revenue growth this year. Eager to capitalize on the movement, contemporary brands like Lioness are now engineering built-in underwear to imitate the effect of an exposed bra. This week, Charlize Theron wore a similar style by Tom Ford to the Governors Awards.

I have, undoubtedly, been influenced. This year, I have spent more money at Victoria’s Secret than cumulatively on lingerie in the past decade—acquiring my own Very Sexy Push-Up, and a turquoise polka-dot number. These are bras I’ve never gravitated toward, let alone purchased, because they seemed like impractical additions to my wardrobe. I’ve had to ask myself: Can you wear leopard-print under a white tee? What if the lace breaches my neckline?

Somewhere between Erin Brockovich and Blake Lively’s character in The Town, an exposed bra—particularly a colorful one—became a media shorthand for unsophistication, and more often than not, sexual availability. In simpler terms: tacky. Now, tacky is trending. Along with diamanté-encrusted snapbacks and sky-high peep-toes, straps in dynamic hues are stretched across the décolleté of It girls en masse. Printed cups are peeking out of the necklines. Some fashionistas are forgoing the shirt altogether. “There's a real cultural shift from polished perfection toward something more expressive, unpredictable, and a little undone,” claims Ocello. Or, perhaps a little more human.

This certainly isn’t a trend for everyone. According to Shotton, Agent Provocateur’s biggest sellers are corsets—a style that was once also considered strictly lingerie, but has since sashayed into the light. One wonders if their popularity is a gateway to full bra-baring, especially as GLP drugs become more readily available, and women more comfortable in their bodies. But with a Blue Wave washing in Woke 3.0, how long do boobs—and their bold accessories—have left? In all seriousness, might we look back on this moment with the same disdain we once reflected on early-aughts raunch culture? Only social media will tell. In the meantime, I plan for my collection to keep growing.

#6838. This Sleepytime Face Cream Has an Ingredient That Mimics Melatonin

Useful even on nights when I don’t get much sleep.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

I am what you might call a problem sleeper. I often toss and turn for hours before falling asleep. Sleep aids don’t work on me (if I’m sick and take NyQuil, I end up with insomnia rather than a deep slumber). I’ll wake up at witching hour and worry about something before falling back asleep. All of this has been known to wreak havoc on my face. After nights when I barely clock five hours, I wake up to dark circles, a duller complexion, and puffy skin. But Augustinus Bader’s The Overnight Restorative Cream works like an insurance policy for sleepless nights.

Skin repairs itself overnight, and just like every other function in your body, it doesn’t do it as well when you don’t sleep. This can lead to inflammation, a damaged barrier, and even accelerated aging (not exactly what I want to hear as a chronic insomniac). While many beauty products have promised to fake the appearance of eight hours of sleep, Augustinus Bader’s new cream has a unique rosehip extract that mimics the effects of melatonin. While the hormone regulates your sleep cycle, melatonin could also be good for your skin. One study even found that it might have antiaging properties thanks to its ability to protect cells. This cream is also made with both polyglutamic and hyaluronic acid to seal in hydration. There’s cica to calm skin and combat inflammation. And of course, since it’s an Augustinus Bader product, there’s also an advanced form of TFC8, the brand’s proprietary ingredient, which facilitates communication between skin cells for repair and renewal.

Since using this moisturizer, my skin looks clear, toned, and glowy most mornings. While I do look bright-eyed and bushy-tailed when I manage to sleep that long and use this cream, my skin looks just as good when I get six hours of sleep. Or—gulp—five. (Any less and my brain isn’t even functioning enough to examine the state of my skin.) My next trick will be to find a face cream that puts me to sleep, but since products can’t, at the current moment, work miracles (including this one), I’ll settle for one that helps mitigate the effects of a bad night.

#6837. Take Note: This Is How Cool People Are Decorating for Thanksgiving This Year

These tastemakers are bringing their personal style to the holiday.

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Pumpkin centerpieces, rustic garlands, dried-fruit wreaths, and cornucopias are classic Thanksgiving decorations. They’re timeless and beloved, but they can also feel predictable. Traditional decor has its place, of course, but Thanksgiving tablescapes—and dinner parties in general—are evolving, much like interiors, to feel more personal, lived-in, and layered. So we asked nine tastemakers and experts how they’re decorating for this family-centric holiday.

From traditional touches to wholly unexpected looks, read on to see how they’re making their homes feel festive this autumn, and maybe you’ll be inspired to try something new this year.

Ever since designer Mikel Welch moved to sunny California, his family has never hesitated to go to his for Thanksgiving to escape the chilly Michigan weather. It’s become a tradition to gather at his house, and similarly to how he grew up, Welch likes to keep his decorations for the holiday understated and warm.

“I love getting the exterior of my house into full fall mode with mums, pumpkins, and seasonal greenery,” Welch tells House Beautiful. “The beauty of fall decorating is that it can carry you from October straight through Thanksgiving without having to change much.”

The designer’s house is filled with dark, moody colors and earthy textures year-round, so he focuses mainly on bringing natural elements inside to do the work for him. “I also have a little tradition with my dad,” he adds. “For the past couple of years, we've made trips to the nursery together to pick out flowers, greenery, and sometimes bales of hay for the front of the house. It's become something I really enjoy doing with him.”

“Thanksgiving has always been a big family affair,” real estate expert and maximalism extraordinaire Harvin Greene says. She’s happy to keep that tradition going as an adult, just in her own way. In the past, Greene’s aunt and uncle would have everyone at their house for the big meal, but last year, they passed the hosting baton to Greene for the first time. “I may have gotten a little carried away...in the best possible way.”

Getting to decorate two tables—one for the “adults,” another for the “kids,” even though they’re technically all grown-ups, too—Greene used this opportunity to design two completely different tablescapes. “I grew up surrounded by women who understood that a beautifully set table and a genuinely good time should go hand in hand,” she adds.

The one in the formal dining space (i.e., the “adults’” table) was theatrical and dramatic, and nearly everything on it had some sort of familial connection, from her parents’ engraved goblets to her husband’s grandmother’s sterling flatware. The kids’ table, on the other hand, was whimsical and vibrant, complete with a yellow patterned tablecloth, colorful glassware, and playful animal candlesticks. “I don't feel the need to follow the traditional Thanksgiving color palette,” Greene says. “Who decided Thanksgiving had to be orange and brown? Yellow, pink, turquoise, and green can be just as festive.”

The holiday isn’t over until they bring out the Bingo cards, a spirited Thanksgiving tradition that Greene’s Aunt Jean started—which involves cash prizes. “It's one of those family traditions that makes the holiday feel like ours, no matter whose house we're celebrating in.”

Talia Hubble is no stranger to a good dinner party—much of her content actually centers around her love of hosting. But Thanksgiving is a bit extra special for the Rhode Islander, especially since it’s being held at her cottage this year. “Growing up, it was just me and my mom, and she was too busy to decorate,” Hubble says. “I think that’s part of why I’ve fallen in love with seasonal decorating as an adult, I get to bring all my cozy childhood decorating dreams to life!”

As soon as the calendar switches from October to November, Hubble starts decorating for fall, opting for an eclectic mix of subtle Christmas decor (think bows, garlands, and wreaths), an excessive amount of taper candles, and a traditional Thanksgiving palette of oranges, browns, greens, and reds. Every year looks a little bit different, but there are pieces in her collection that are simply too cute not to bring out every holiday season.

“Half the fun of pulling everything out for Thanksgiving is remembering where it came from,” she says. “I think the best decor is a culmination of pieces you’ve been collecting for years, and the memories behind those pieces are what make the table feel special and uniquely yours.”

By the time she’s done decorating, her home feels like a warm, welcoming escape, somewhere her guests can exhale. “My philosophy is that you can’t go wrong when you aim for cozy, colorful, and embrace a little organized mess,” Hubble adds. “My favorite Thanksgiving tables are the ones that look even better halfway through dinner, when the napkins are rumpled and everyone’s reaching for seconds.”

Chef and food stylist Romilly Newman can’t always count on being in the same place as her family for Thanksgiving since everyone’s scattered about, but she always makes a point to throw an autumnal dinner with friends around the holiday time, complete with beautiful fall decor.

Her mom never decorated their home while she was growing up with specific Thanksgiving decorations—instead, she would decorate with deep orange and red flowers to capture the fall vibe. Newman’s style as an adult is similar. “I am fall-obsessed, so as soon as the temperature drops from summer, I'm setting up branches, candles, and darker accents,” she says. “I love the atmosphere inside and outside around Thanksgiving time, and I try my best to bottle that up in my home.”

Natural thicker linen, pops of silver, dark florals, and moody lighting envelop her space as the chef relies mostly on candlelight and organic elements—like figs, sage, chestnuts, and mandarins—as the decorative accents.

“Thanksgiving is great because it brings family together and offers time to enjoy food, friends, and being at home,” Newman adds. “Whatever your style is, try to create an environment that allows for great conversation and a festive evening. You want everyone to be comfortable and feel the holiday spirit.”

When writer Olivia Noceda was a child, she would stay up late the night before Thanksgiving and design custom menus for everyone on Microsoft Paint, which she would then print out, frame, and place on each table setting in preparation for the big meal. The holiday was a big deal for Noceda’s mother, who would always put on the most beautiful Thanksgiving gatherings. “I think that's where my love for hosting and setting a beautiful table really started,” she explains.

However, for the last six years, Noceda has been having Thanksgiving with her husband’s side of the family, so when she does host around the holiday, it’s typically for Friendsgiving. “I don't necessarily pick a theme, but I do like everything to feel cohesive,” she explains of her decorating process. “I usually start incorporating little autumnal touches sometime in October and let them carry me through Thanksgiving.”

The writer and recipe developer lets the season guide her, building her tablescape and decor around a beautiful branch from the farmers’ market, a bowl of pears or persimmons, a collection of little pumpkins and squash, or a few taper candles on the dining room table. Ingredients play a big part in her decor—she can even take a fresh loaf of bread and make it feel special on her table.

For the rest of her decor, she likes to incorporate vintage serving pieces, everyday elements, and elevated details like antique silver and linen napkins with things she already owns to lean into that lived-in, warm feeling. The last thing she wants is her home to feel like a department store display. “Nothing needs to match perfectly, and I actually prefer when it doesn't,” Noceda adds.

What entrepreneur and Real Housewives of New York star Erin Lichy remembers best about Thanksgivings growing up is the smell. “We always spent Thanksgiving out east, and the house was full of pumpkins, local hay, and lanterns,” she explains. The scent of that fresh produce, mixed with her grandmother’s homemade turkey t’bit, has shaped the way she decorates for and hosts Thanksgiving. “That coveted and highly personal (and incredibly delicious) recipe is in my cookbook, She’s a Host, and it’s part of every Thanksgiving I host.”

Decorating starts in the kitchen for her family. Lichy wants the home to smell like fall, so she focuses on that sense, making seasonal recipes with squash and pumpkin and incorporating autumnal flowers in her interiors. The melding of those scents gives her townhouse a warm feeling, especially when mixed with layered fall colors and the crackling of the fireplace.

Ever since Lichy and her family have moved into their townhouse, she’s been obsessed with decorating her stoop too. “The stoop is the first thing everyone sees, so this year I decided to bring in Simon Wallace Design to make it really stand out!” Lichy adds. “They completely transformed it with layers of seasonal color and texture, and it feels so warm and welcoming.” She specifically loves using the faux garlands and pinecones, as well as lanterns, from Balsam Hill and Frontgate because they’re so beautifully made that they look real, and she’s able to reuse them year after year.

No matter what she does to decorate though, she just wants her home to feel warm and lived-in, and this is the season and holiday to really lean into that.

Decorating for the holidays is Gabby Amato’s version of a self-care day. “It's truly one of my favorite days of the year,” she says. “I carve out a weekend afternoon when my husband and son are out of the house, light my favorite fall candles, turn on some music, and have the house entirely to myself.”

Amato, the founder of Table Two Studio, a paper plate brand which specializes in beautiful, artistic designs that you definitely won’t want to throw away, credits her love of Thanksgiving and the fall season in general to her mother. While growing up in the ‘90s, she saw her mom put so much care and effort into the holiday decorations, always making their home feel warm, nostalgic, and full of spirit. “For Thanksgiving, she decorated our mantel with gourds, greenery, and ceramic pilgrim statues that were a gift from my grandmother,” Amato says. “We had oversized cornstalks and pumpkins at the front door. Nothing ever felt overly styled or precious.”

It’s this feeling that has stuck with her throughout her life, and it’s what she hopes she captures in her own home every year. She begins decorating for the fall early, right after Labor Day, pulling out old pieces and unboxing anything new and arranging it all in fresh ways so nothing feels stale. “I love beautiful decor, of course, but I'm much more interested in creating an atmosphere than achieving a particular aesthetic,” she adds. “I want my home to feel warm, lived-in, a little nostalgic, and festive.”

Donna Lennard’s family was never one for holiday dressing—most of the attention at Thanksgiving fell on the delicious food shared with family. As a restauranteur and cofounder of beloved NYC establishment il Buco, it makes sense why the meal remains to be the most important thing to Lennard even today.

“Clearly, the table is always festive, whether it’s the tablecloth, the napkins, or linens that reflect the season in burnt orange and the like,” she explains. “I love to decorate a table with assorted colorful gourds and seasonal florals, beautiful candlelight (our beeswax candles already have an orange glow), orange ranunculus, and the occasional dried floral.” Colorful Pienza glassware dots the table and her plethora of beautiful tableware gives it more character, but she keeps everything else relatively simple to ensure the food remains the center of attention.

Though Lennard doesn’t often host her family and friends for the Thanksgiving meal, she’s already looking forward to next year when everyone will finally gather at her beach house.

Being from Australia, Kendall Knox—the cofounder of Olive Ateliers along with her husband—didn’t have a personal reference of what the holiday “should” be or look like. This gives her the freedom of deciding what it is for her family. “What it has become is an intimate table: my husband Ben, our son Rhodes, and Ben's mum,” she says. “No rotation, no kids' table, no forty-pound bird. Just the four of us and a dinner that runs long because nobody is in a hurry to leave it.”

Since Knox isn’t beholden to the classic Thanksgiving decor, she tends to keep her home and her table rather simple, “shopping the house,” as she puts it to decorate instead of buying something new. She keeps a rather neutral palette of rich browns, creamy whites, terracotta, and silver, then uses antique linens, mismatched silverware, and candlesticks that have collected wax to capture the true lived-in feel.

Her focus tends to be on the table and the meal, letting the food add more color. If a tablecloth has a wine ring on it from the year before, all the better—it complements the stacked plates at the end of the meal.

“The objects that come out every year, that get a little more marked each time, are the ones that end up meaning the most,” Knox adds. “Thanksgiving is just a very good excuse to use them.”

What’s worth shopping right now? The AirPods Pro 3 are 28% off, and the AirPods Max 2 are $130 off. Keep reading for my honest opinions on what you should grab now, and what may be cheaper next week.

Even though there aren’t any record-low AirPod deals yet, these prices are on par with other sales like October Prime Day and Labor Day. Still, it might be worth holding out until the day of Black Friday—though you risk stock selling out. Plus, I predict that we might see a first-ever discount on brand-new models like the AirPods 5.

#6827. Please Enjoy Jenna Johnson’s Perfect Clapback to Critics and Broadway Rumors After Her ‘DWTS’ Elimination

Harry Shum Jr. and Jenna Johnson may have been eliminated ahead of the Dancing With the Stars finale, but the pro dancer isn’t quite finished yet.

After they bid farewell to the ballroom on Tuesday night, Jenna posted a hilarious clapback to critics and, in the process, seemingly debunked all those Broadway rumors. In a video shared to her Instagram account on Wednesday, November 18, Jenna and the rest of the eliminated DWTS pros performed dramatized choreography, poking fun at folks who claim she kept “putting the emphasis on herself” throughout the season.

Her song choice for the post, “I Can’t Make You Love Me” by Bonnie Raitt, was pointed and aspirationally petty, too. Some of the lyrics? Ahem: “Cause I can’t make you love me if you don’t / You can’t make your heart feel something it won’t.”

As the other dancers lifted her and danced circles around her, Jenna also referenced Harry’s polarizing “scream” during their week two Viennese waltz to “Iris” by the Goo Goo Dolls…which the internet made fun of at the time.

Harry himself even chimed in with a comment, writing, “Scream. Solo after solo. It’s like I was never there… 😂😂😂.”

Another pro, Britt Stewart, added, “It’s the scream for me.”

ICYMI, one of The Sun’s sources previously alleged Jenna had been “gunning for a Broadway role, 100 percent” when she performed a mini solo at the end of her and Harry’s performance of “Supercalifragilisticexpialidocious” earlier in the season.

“She has become more and more focused on her getting a Broadway offer after this season and after the DWTS tour ends than she is highlighting Harry and it shows,” a source told the outlet. “She put a solo of herself into the routine for Disney Night—which is basically unheard of for a pro to do. Jenna is doing a disservice to [Harry] by putting the emphasis on herself, and the other pros know it.”

Welp…seems like Jenna got the last laugh. 10/10 comeback!

#6819. Amelia Gray Brings Her Effortless Edge to H&M’s Latest Collection

The model shares her fresh take on holiday dressing and the pieces she’s making her own.

“Chic, playful, holiday”: This is how Amelia Gray sums up H&M Studio Holiday 2026, a collection that makes a case for getting dressed with a little more imagination. The model, who is currently one of the industry’s most recognizable faces, knows a thing or two about making an outfit memorable. These days, though, her approach is taking a more refined turn. “My personal style right now is definitely elevating, becoming more mature, chicer, and more refined,” she says. “I think H&M’s collection reflects that perfectly.”

Like she says, that evolution feels right at home with H&M Studio. The brand’s most directional offering, Studio collections bring a more experimental perspective to the company’s portfolio, with limited-edition designs developed by the Stockholm atelier’s in-house team. For holiday 2026, the inspiration comes from the theatrical world of early-1900s Paris, where grand opera houses and salons set the scene for expressive dressing. Sweeping proportions, tactile fabrics, and unexpected details give the collection a sense of movement, while sharp tailoring keeps things grounded. The result is a wardrobe that feels equally suited to a festive gathering and an ordinary day that calls for something special.

Gray has already picked her favorite combination. “I loved the bomber jacket with the dress,” she says. “That outfit is great for Thanksgiving or Christmas dinner.” The bomber jacket, finished with a fluffy collar, brings a relaxed edge to the dual-fabric dress, which has a full balloon skirt that gives the look plenty of movement. Fluffy-lined ballet flats complete the outfit and add a sense of playfulness. Together, the pieces strike a balance between dressed-up and laid-back.

The bomber also has plenty of mileage beyond the dinner table. Gray calls it her “new favorite,” and for good reason: “The bomber jacket is perfect to dress up and down. With jeans. With a skirt. With shorts.” Throw it over denim for a casual weekend lunch, or pair it with a sleek pencil for a night out. The trick is to let the contrast do the work. A sporty jacket can change the mood of a dress, just as a more polished piece can bring an unexpected finish to something casual.

Some moments call for a dress that handles the styling decisions for you. The satin open-shoulder dress does exactly that, bringing a fluid and elegant neckline to the occasion. Its silhouette has enough presence to stand on its own, while dangle hoop earrings add movement and a touch of shine. It’s an easy combination for a holiday party, a dinner reservation, or any invitation that warrants getting dressed up without spending an hour figuring out what to wear.

The key is to keep the rest of the look uncomplicated. The dress could also take on a different personality with a more casual shoe or a relaxed jacket layered over it. That’s part of the appeal of a versatile occasion piece: It doesn’t have to be saved for one particular night on the calendar.

A peplum top and an A-line skirt make an interesting study in proportion. The puff sleeves and defined waist of the top bring shape up above, while the viscose skirt creates a fluid counterpoint below. It’s a combination that feels polished without being overly formal—a smart option for a lunch that turns into an afternoon of shopping or a dinner with friends. For a slightly more understated take, swap in the scoop-neck viscose top. The silhouette becomes simpler, but the skirt still gives the outfit its sense of movement.

The handbag adds another layer of personality. Its soft shape, velvet rope handle, and oversize tassel bring texture and a hint of old-world theatricality to the mix, tying back to the collection’s Parisian inspiration. It’s also the kind of accessory that can change the feel of classic pieces, whether carried with a coordinated outfit or used to add interest to a simple top-and-jeans combination.

A crisp cotton poplin shirt is one of those wardrobe staples that rarely needs an introduction. Here, its clean lines meet the texture and embellishment of an embroidered crepe skirt, creating a combination that feels easy to wear. Over-the-knee leather boots round out the outfit, giving it a little more attitude. It’s a strong look for a holiday gathering, a night at the theater, or a dinner when your denim go-to simply won’t do. For another interpretation, the skirt can be swapped for barrel-leg trousers, while a raw-edged puff-sleeve blouse offers an alternative to the cotton poplin.

The appeal lies in the mix of familiar and fashion-forward pieces. A poplin shirt is hardly an adventurous purchase, but paired with a more expressive skirt and statement boots, it takes on a different character. This balance also makes the individual pieces easier to wear beyond the occasion. The shirt works with denim, the boots can ground a shorter hemline, and the skirt can be dressed down with a simple knit. It’s a reminder that a statement piece doesn’t need an entirely new wardrobe built around it.

For Gray, the best outfits leave room for a little experimentation. “I love an unexpected pairing,” she says. “That’s why I love the dress and the bomber!” It’s a simple philosophy, one that gets to the heart of personal style. It’s about recognizing what you like, finding interesting ways to put things together, and having the confidence to wear them your way. H&M Studio Holiday 2026 offers plenty of starting points, from dramatic silhouettes to tactile accessories and everyday staples with a twist. What happens next is up to you.

#6818. Dyson’s Black Friday Is Here—Shop Vacuums and Air Purifiers Up to 50 Percent Off

This is your chance to get a new model at its lowest price all year.

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Though Black Friday is a week away, our home editors are spotting early savings on Dyson’s coveted vacuums and tech—making now a rare opportunity to score some of the brand’s newest models on sale. #6813. Lindsey Graham’s famous quote makes sense now

Sen. Lindsey Graham, R-S.C., talks with reporters in the basement of the Capitol on June 21, 2016. Back then, the late senator had a different view of President Donald Trump, Curtis writes. (Tom Williams/CQ Roll Call)

“If we nominate Trump, we will get destroyed ... and we will deserve it.”

Reflecting on Tuesday’s midterm election results, a shellacking by Democrats that served as a rebuke to President Donald Trump, this quote deserves another look.

In 2016, in the midst of a heated Republican primary race, with assorted candidates jockeying to be the party’s presidential nominee, those words were posted by what now seems a surprising source: South Carolina Sen. Lindsey Graham.

Of course, Graham himself had been one of the candidates in that bitter contest. Insults like “lightweight” and “jackass” were tossed back and forth, and Trump publicly shared Graham’s cellphone number. (Trump finally apologized for that stunt — at Graham’s funeral this past July. Better late than never, I suppose.)

By the time of Graham’s death this year, though, his earlier self would not have recognized the senator from South Carolina who had transformed himself into one of the president’s golfing buddies and closest allies.

In May of this year, after Sen. Bill Cassidy lost his Louisiana primary to a Trump-backed opponent, Graham reasoned on NBC News that Cassidy lost because he “tried to destroy” the president, and warned the same fate would befall any Republican who crossed Trump. “This is the party of Donald Trump.”

Why the switch? “He allowed me into his world. That’s great,” he told The New York Times.

Lindsey Graham stayed relevant, with a desired seat at the table — at a cost to his political reputation and legacy.

After he died, Graham’s sister was appointed to complete his Senate term and was then pushed to run for a full term by her brother’s onetime nemesis Donald Trump. The president’s endorsement pushed Darline Graham past a more experienced Republican, Rep. Ralph Norman, in the Senate primary.

That she played a part in the Republicans’ historic midterm loss is a full-circle moment that Shakespeare would relish.

Graham lost to Dr. Annie Andrews, as the state elected a Democratic senator for the first time in decades. It came as a big surprise, especially to those Republicans who, like Lindsey Graham, started out as skeptics of the inexperienced, cruel president before making peace with the man, his policies and his power.

MAGA swallowed the GOP, and voters who elected Trump in 2016 and 2024 followed.

What was the breaking point for the people who ignored millions of dollars in attack ads on their way to the polls?

In Ohio, was it the Vivek Ramaswamy ad that dredged up opponent Amy Acton’s childhood trauma, supported by Trump and Vice President JD Vance?

In the South, was it the seeming glee of Republican state legislatures as they gerrymandered districts with substantial African American constituencies, all with the blessing of a Supreme Court-weakened Voting Rights Act?

In Texas, did Hispanic voters who swung to Trump in 2024 realize that ICE’s definition of “the worst of the worst” included business owners, grandmoms and schoolchildren?

Did tariffs turn off farmers as it affected expenses and bottom lines?

Did Americans rebel when they saw their dollars buy less while the president gushed over gold trinkets, a ballroom and a reflecting pool his hand-picked contractors couldn’t fix, and made billions for himself and his family?

Did the Islamophobia and anti-Semitism, the racism and homophobia in the name of Jesus leave true people of faith or just plain decent people disgusted?

In South Carolina, Darline Graham didn’t explain her plans for the economy or for fighting corruption or foreign entanglements, except to say “national security is not my thing” on a debate stage.

But the candidate with a scant resume did manage at a South Carolina rally to call her opponent “bats — crazy” while standing next to an approving Trump, who coined his own anti-trans slur for Andrews.

Andrews responded by saying: “If name-calling would help lower gas prices and grocery prices, he can call me all the names he wants. But I don’t think that’s going to get the job done.” In a state with 14 counties “without a single OB/Gyn,” as Andrews has said, the emptiness of insults is hard to miss.

Whatever lessons they draw from Tuesday, Democrats should not get comfortable.

Angry, hurting voters expect results — fast.

But for now, it’s hard not to recall the warning issued and then forgotten 10 years ago, with the hope that Republicans realize the old Lindsey might have been right all along.

Mary C. Curtis has worked at The New York Times, The Baltimore Sun, The Charlotte Observer, as national correspondent for Politics Daily, and is a senior facilitator with The OpEd Project. She is host of the CQ Roll Call “Equal Time with Mary C. Curtis” podcast. Follow her on Twitter @mcurtisnc3.

#6808. ‘Off Campus’ Season 2 Might Make a Major Departure From the Books, Thanks to This Casting Shakeup

The absence could change a key storyline from the books in the Prime Video series.

Fans are already counting down the days until we see Allie and Dean’s love story pan out on Off Campus season 2, and I don’t blame them one bit. It’ll be a minute until we reunite with the Briar U gang, but fans are keeping up with every update on the TV adaptation of Elle Kennedy’s book series in the meantime. And the latest news on season 2 is that a certain cast member won’t be returning.

“I’m not gonna be in the next season,” Riley Davis, who plays Allie’s ex-boyfriend Sean on the Prime Video series, told People at the New York City premiere of his movie November 1963 on November 18.

As for the reason behind his exit? Riley says there were some scheduling conflicts—which checks out, since he has a role in another YA series, Netflix’s Crew Girl. It’s unclear whether that particular project played a part in his departure from Off Campus.

“There was a plan for some Sean in season 2, and we just had a bit of a scheduling thing on my side and a bit on their side,” he explained. “So we’ll see what happens down the line.”

What’s more: Riley’s exit could potentially signal that the second season of Off Campus might go a different direction from the book series it’s based on.

As viewers saw in the show’s first season, Riley’s character, Sean, plays a pivotal role in Allie and Dean’s romance. In the books, he and Allie have an on-again, off-again relationship—but we might not see that pan out now that Riley is out.

This isn’t the first time the TV adaptation shook things up with some changes from the source material. In season 1, the show’s writers introduced John Logan’s nonbinary sibling Jules Logan, who noticeably wasn’t in the OG book series.

We’ll have to see how Riley/Sean’s absence is really handled when Off Campus season 2 hits Prime Video on April 28, 2027!

#6807. The Horse Girl Trend Is Here for Your Jewelry Box

Horseshoes and tail motifs are dominating charms and rings.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Saddles, stirrups, and horseshoes, oh my: We’ve been firmly in the year of the Fire Horse since February 2026, and the jewelry scene is officially in its horse girl era. Aside from the look being a clear classic in the world of jewelry (horseshoes were popular in Victorian-era jewels), today’s examples vary from sparkly creations to more minimal, toned-down pieces with everyday appeal. No matter the aesthetic, the jewelry of the Year of the Fire Horse is strong: This era only comes around every 60 years in the Chinese zodiac and is said to represent strength, independence, and fearlessness.

Celebrities are clearly fans: Taylor Swift just wore two double-layered horseshoe pendants to announce her Grammy nomination. Horse-ified jewelry also spanned the runway. For spring/summer 2027, both Gucci and Celine revisited the archival horsebit hardware detail and reworked it in a contemporary way to highlight the beautiful lines and sculptural symmetry of the timeless motif. Elsewhere, Chloé’s statement belt covered in huge running horses has been a fashion girl favorite and is the equivalent of jewelry for your waist.

There is perhaps nothing more classic than a chunky horseshoe ring or a horse-themed charm. Verragio and Oscar Heyman each reworked the symbol in gemstone-studded bracelets and double-motif rings. Elsewhere, Lionheart makes a slew of gloriously stunning oversize horse-head pendants and brooches brimming with diamonds and, sometimes, accessorized with mother-of-pearl saddles. The brand also partners directly with 13 Hands Equine Rescue, a nonprofit sanctuary located in Clinton Corners, New York.

But this year, equestrian jewelry is not horsing around when it comes to experimenting. One such example is Hermès’s latest collection, which has an obvious horse connection: saddles, diamond-decked horse heads, and golden saddles. Titled “Into the Horsescape,” the 90-piece lineup is quite literally the horse girl’s dream: lassos in the form of massive baguette-cut diamonds and glimmering oversize horsebit bracelets replete with pavé brown and white diamonds. Pomellato also showcased a carved horse cameo surrounded by brown diamonds and suspended from a delicate rose-gold Dressage-link chain.

If you want to lean into your inner horse girl in a more subtle, everyday way, Gucci’s iconic horsebit rings, refined drop earrings, and sleek bangles offer a sophisticated and versatile place to start your collection. Going vintage is another option: Equestrian themes have been reinterpreted in the jewelry space for hundreds of years now, so there’s ample opportunity. One thing is clear: Horse girl jewelry is galloping from the archives to the runway and into everyday style.

#6805. Thune to run for minority leader

Senate Majority Leader John Thune, seen here at the Capitol on Sept. 15, is hoping to bounce back from Tuesday’s losses and “not stand idly by.” (Tom Williams/CQ Roll Call)

Senate Republicans are preparing to make the transition from the majority to the minority — and their top leaders will seek to retain their positions in the 120th Congress, even after the political drubbing they endured Tuesday night.

Majority Leader John Thune, R-S.D., will run for minority leader during next week’s GOP leadership elections, a spokesperson for his office said.

After enjoying a 53-47 majority, he will face a very different chamber next year, with Democrats looking to control between 54 to 56 seats, depending on how the dust settles.

In a post on X just after midnight on election night, as the extent of the losses became clear, Thune wrote, “Over the last two years, Senate Republicans have put up a strong record of accomplishment that’s created safer streets, more money in Americans’ pockets, and new opportunities for families and businesses to get ahead.”

“As we head into 2027, Senate Republicans will not stand idly by as the far-left, socialist wing of the Democrat Party attempts to unwind everything we’ve done,” he said. “Thankfully, we still have President Trump in the White House, and we’ll work with him to stop the worst impulses of Democrat extremism.”

Thune, 65, has served as the majority leader for nearly two years since winning a three-way race to succeed Mitch McConnell of Kentucky as the chamber’s top Republican. That was a spot McConnell had held since taking his party’s leadership reins in early 2007, making him the longest-serving Senate party leader in history, counting his time in both the minority and the majority.

Thune at times had a tumultuous relationship with the president as Trump pushed legislation, like the voter-ID related bill known as the SAVE America Act, that never had a chance of passing the chamber.

Senate Majority Whip John Barrasso of Wyoming is expected to seek his current role in the minority as well.

“Republicans had a tough night last night. We lost great Senators in hard races,” he said on X Wednesday afternoon. “There are clear lessons to be learned from the results.”

“We should listen to what voters unmistakably said last night,” Barrasso, 74, said. “In the Senate, we will aggressively oppose terrible ideas that drive our country toward the socialism Democrats embraced. Our commitment is to common sense. And our focus should not waiver from the top priority of driving down prices that have remained stubbornly too high.”

The leadership elections for Senate Republicans will take place on Tuesday, Nov. 10, at 9 a.m. in the Old Senate Chamber.

So far, no one has publicly come forward to challenge Thune, and his fellow Republican colleagues were already coalescing around him ahead of the midterms.

Florida Sen. Rick Scott, who ran against Thune in 2024 and is a friend of the president’s, said in September then he was not planning on challenging him again.

Sen. James Lankford, R-Okla., is expected to seek a repeat as vice chair of the Republican conference, while Sen. Shelley Moore Capito, R-W.V., is set to run again for Republican Policy Committee chair, according to people familiar with their plans.

#6803. Who Is Daddy Franklin From ‘The Body’? For Starters, Nnamdi Asomugha Is Kerry Washington’s Husband

Daddy Franklin is married to Olivia Pope?

If you watched The Body in one sitting and are now fully obsessed with it, you’re not alone!! The horrifying coming-of-age series is scary good, and while you may not be familiar with the actor who plays Father Samuel Franklin (aka Daddy Franklin), you definitely know his very famous wife. As it turns out, Nnamdi Asomugha has been married to Kerry Washington for over 13 years now, having fallen in love after watching Kerry perform in Race on Broadway in 2010. Kerry later confirmed the story during a 2018 interview with Marie Claire, saying, “The last time I did theater, it completely transformed my life. That’s where I met my husband.” Cute!!!

Though things got off to a rocky start (Kerry apparently told Nnamdi she didn’t date athletes, and Nnamdi told Kerry he didn’t date actors), the two got together anyway, and just about three years later in June 2013, they tied the knot in a super low-key ceremony. According to their marriage license, Kerry and Nnamdi said “I do” in Hailey, Idaho, where Nnamdi’s sister officiated and Kerry’s parents served as witnesses. Since then, the couple has welcomed two kids together—Isabelle Amarachi, born in 2014, and Caleb Kelechi, born in 2016—and are seemingly thriving outside of the spotlight. But we think it’s time to learn a lil bit more about Nnamdi, don’t you?

Here’s everything you should know about the man behind Daddy Franklin, including how he rose to fame as a former NFL cornerback and what he’s up to next.

After growing up in Louisiana in a Nigerian Igbo household, Nnamdi attended the University of California, Berkeley, where he played for the California Golden Bears football team. Soon after graduating, Nnamdi was drafted in the first round of the 2003 NFL Draft by the Oakland Raiders, which is very impressive.

Over his 11-year professional football career, Nnamdi also played for the Philadelphia Eagles and the San Francisco 49ers, and in December 2013, the cornerback signed a one-day contract with Oakland so he could retire as a Raider. I love to see a loyal man!

According to Nnamdi, the decision to retire at 32 was difficult, but it still felt like the right time for him. “Yeah, I felt that it was a good time, but it didn’t make it any easier,” he told UPROXX. “It’s a game that 'til this day I miss, and I miss playing. It’s not that I want to go play again, but I miss so many aspects of it; being around the team, being around the coaching, that calm before the storm.”

Nnamdi first caught the acting bug all the way back in 2008, when he landed the role of “Party Guest with Wife” on the CW sitcom The Game. (Remember: There are no small roles, only small actors!!) He then caught the eye of director Peter Berg on the set of a Nike commercial shoot in 2009, who was impressed by the athlete’s ease in front of the camera. From there, Nnamdi went on to play small roles in Friday Night Lights, Leverage, and Cubed before making his on-screen feature film debut in 2012’s Fire with Fire.

Since retiring from the NFL, Nnamdi has fully committed himself to his acting career with starring roles in the films Crown Heights, Sylvie’s Love, The Good Nurse, The Knife, and—most recently—The Body. He’s also served as a producer on several of those projects as well.

Despite all his success, Nnamdi still remains as modest as ever, avoiding social media and press to instead focus on his craft. “I’m really bad at self-promotion. I know that about myself,” he previously explained to Bleacher Report back in 2017. “People told me all of the time, ‘You could be such a big star if you just talked about yourself more,’ but I’m not good at that. It’s always been about team.”

During a 2018 interview with Today, Kerry corrected a host when they alluded to her two children, saying, “I’m a mother of three." The reason: Nnamdi has a now-teenage daughter from another relationship, who Kerry has helped raise alongside her two biological children. And just like with Isabelle and Caleb, the couple keeps their eldest far from the paparazzi, so very little is known about her.

As Kerry joked to People, “I have moments where I take pictures or videos of one of my three amazing kids and I wanna post it online, and I tend to just send it to my parents or my shrink instead.”

Philanthropy is important to Nnamdi, which is why he created the Asomugha Foundation in 2005 at the very beginning of his career.

The foundation has two main programs: Orphans and Widows in Need (which provides food, shelter, medicine, vocational training, literacy efforts, and scholarships to widows and orphans in Nigeria) and Asomugha College Tour for Scholars (which takes selected students from Bay Area and Los Angeles area high schools on college tours across the country).

Though Kerry and Nnamdi are both actors and producers, it isn’t often that they work together on creative projects. Back in 2017, Nnamdi told Vanity Fair that it was possible he and his wife would co-produce a project at some point.

“We would produce,” he said. “We’d probably produce before acting, I would think.” The following year, his production company, iAm21 Entertainment, helped produce American Son, a Broadway play starring Kerry, and hopefully, that won’t be the last time the two are involved in the same project.

As far as sharing the screen goes, however, Nnamdi doesn’t think that will ever happen. As he told The New York Times, “I produced American Son, but as actors, there’s no plan as of now for that collaboration. We’re very supportive of each other’s journeys, but we’ve always been that way. We always want the best for each other in whatever we’re doing.”

Case in point? The couple’s *chef’s kiss* appearance at the 2025 Met Gala. Ahem:

Oh, and the duo’s 2025 Golden Globes looks:

And ofc, he was right by Kerry's side during her December 2024 Hollywood Walk of Fame ceremony, where she finally received her well-deserved star. How cute do they look with Kerry's parents??

TL;DR: Kerry and Nnamdi truly couldn’t be any cuter. Keep on being adorably low-key, you two.

#6799. Progressives and moderates rejoice: 6 takeaways from the midterms

House Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries addresses an election night watch party in Washington on Tuesday after Democrats were projected to win the House. (Tom Williams/CQ Roll Call)

Democrats were savoring their midterm election rout Wednesday after decisively flipping both the House and Senate.

“Last night was beyond even our largest expectations of what could happen,” Arizona Rep. Greg Stanton said on a press call.

Hakeem Jeffries, the top House Democrat in line to become the first Black speaker, predicted Democrats would hold at least 255 seats next year, which would be the largest majority for either party since Democrats won 257 seats in 2008.

In the Senate, the Democratic Caucus was poised to hold at least 54 seats after seismic red-state gains, with two key races too close to call.

“What we have now seen is a generational remake of the Senate map that is going to change America not only now but on into the future,” Charles E. Schumer, the chamber’s top Democrat, said Wednesday.

Here are six takeaways from the midterm elections:

Democratic gains were so sweeping that both ideological wings of the party had plenty to celebrate. The moderate Blue Dogs more than doubled their current membership, with at least 22 Blue Dog PAC-endorsed candidates winning Tuesday. Meanwhile, the Congressional Progressive Caucus anticipated at least 25 new members.

For House progressives, swing-seat wins in Michigan and California were signs that their candidates’ message resonated in battleground races and that Democrats should move to implement those progressive ideas next year.

“Big majorities can get big things done,” Congressional Progressive Caucus Chair Greg Casar said on a Wednesday press call.

Illinois Rep. Brad Schneider, the chair of the center-left New Democrat Coalition, told reporters he had a strong relationship with Casar and envisioned different factions of the party working together.

“It doesn’t matter what you call your idea. If your idea is going to help us move the ball forward, join us at the table,” Schneider said.

Army veteran Cait Conley, who was leading GOP Rep. Mike Lawler in a race that remained uncalled Wednesday afternoon, said she’d been open about having differences with incoming New York Democrats aligned with the Democratic Socialists of America. But she said Democrats across the political spectrum needed to prioritize “real solutions for the American people.”

“If we don’t make that our priority, if we don’t show that that is more important than anything else … then I think the voters are going to show us in 2028 that they’re not going to accept that,” she said on the New Dems press call.

While the president cast a shadow over the entire midterm map, three of his marquee candidates were among the night’s biggest busts.

Trump was behind appointed South Carolina Sen. Darline Graham every step of the way, helping her win a contested primary to replace her late brother, Lindsey Graham, on the ballot. But Graham lost to Democrat Annie Andrews in a stunning upset. In North Carolina, Trump had publicly urged Michael Whatley to run for the state’s open seat. But the former Republican National Committee chairman lost to Democratic former Gov. Roy Cooper by 20 points. And most significantly, in Texas, Trump elevated scandal-plagued state Attorney General Ken Paxton over Republican incumbent Sen. John Cornyn. But the president’s support wasn’t enough to stave off a general election drubbing at the hands of Democrat James Talarico.

Even Rep. Julia Letlow, Trump’s pick for Senate in deep-red Louisiana, won her election by less than 2 points.

Republicans in seven states successfully embarked on a mid-decade redistricting spree aimed at boosting GOP candidates. But the push couldn’t overcome the political headwinds that battered the Republican brand. In California, where Democrats launched a redistricting push in response to the Texas redraw, the new map proved more beneficial: Democrats are poised to flip at least four seats they redrew in their favor.

Republican Alex Mealer was the heavy favorite to win a redrawn Houston-area seat that Trump would have carried by 20 points. She raised $2.8 million to first-time Democratic candidate Leticia Gutierrez’ $215,000. Yet Gutierrez won the contest by double digits. In an interview on MS NOW, Gutierrez said her campaign proved that “you don’t need money to buy a congressional race.” She wasn’t the only under-the-radar House contender to pull off an unexpected victory Tuesday. In North Carolina, several low-profile and under-funded Democrats appear poised to pull off unlikely upsets, though the races remain too close to call.

Democrats have chased the dream of a blue Texas for more than 30 years — and always came up short. Until Tuesday. The party notched a series of stunning wins in the nation’s most consequential red state — from Talarico’s decisive victory in the Senate contest to winning several key House races to capturing the governor’s mansion. Democrats seized every elected statewide position on the ballot and flipped the state House, according to The Associated Press. “They said it could not be done,’’ Democratic Rep. Christian Menefee told supporters Tuesday night. “We did it.”

Democrats made deep gains in red states and districts across the country, flipping Senate seats in Texas, South Carolina and Kansas, while races in Alaska and Mississippi remained too close to call. Florida Gov. Ron DeSantis called it “probably the political worst case scenario for a midterm election.”

But Democrats didn’t appear convinced that their brand had completely rebounded from deep losses two years ago, saying they had work to do over the next two years.

“Many of the folks who voted for us yesterday, of course, are still skeptical,” Schumer said.

Similarly, Casar and Rep. Pramila Jayapal both warned Democrats not to assume that Tuesday’s wins had resolved the party’s branding issues.

“We still are not particularly popular as a Democratic Party across the country,” Jayapal told reporters. “I think we’ve got a shot to show that we can deliver on the kind of bold populist change that people ran on and won on.”

#6793. Wait, Did Hailey Bieber Buy CBK’s Jacket on the RealReal?

Well, no, but there’s a lot to unpack here

Yesterday, Hailey Bieber took her obsession with ’90s minimalism one step further with a reference to the ultimate ‘90s minimalist: Carolyn Bessette-Kennedy. For a talk at the 2026 DealBook Summit here in New York, she and her stylist Dani Michelle pulled a Yohji Yamamoto look from Spring 1998, the one with a black, high-necked jacket with a line of delicate buttons paired with a white linen column skirt so famously worn by CBK in June 1998. It did what it was supposed to do: Stop us in our scrolling. But it jogged something else in the collective consciousness of fashion lovers everywhere. Hadn’t this jacket just made the rounds on our feeds?

A few weeks ago, savvy shoppers spotted the look for sale on the RealReal. Bessette-Kennedy’s reverence for Yohji Yamamoto is one that fashion’s acolytes tend to share, so it didn’t take long for the listing to go viral. The description identified its starry provenance: “From the Spring/Summer 1998 Collection; as worn by Carolyn Bessette-Kennedy in 1998 and worn by Winona Ryder for the season 5 premiere of 'Stranger Things' in 2025.”

TRR listed the jacket for $7,025. It sat on the site long enough to make the Instagram rounds, but someone snatched it up before the site had time to mark it down. The jacket sold for full price on November 6, less than two weeks before the DealBook Summit.

Could it be that Bieber scrolls for vintage Yohji on the RealReal like the rest of us? Does she have saved searches? Is she watching her obsessions with the eyes of a hawk? (Or has she outsourced this job to Michelle or someone on her team?) Bazaar reached out to the RealReal, and a spokesperson denied that Bieber’s look came from them. Well, it was fun to imagine. But just the possibility that this could have happened is enough to make you think.

Michelle’s post of the look does not reveal her source, though some accounts are claiming Bieber’s jacket came from Tab Vintage. The vintage resale platform Shy mentioned in comment sections that it recently sold the same piece. Another one of the jackets, also without a proven link to CBK’s personal wardrobe, sold via the Fashion Auctioneer in March for $12,500. That’s the thing with ready-to-wear: It’s not one-of-a-kind. Bessette-Kennedy bought her jacket off the rack, which means there are multiple versions floating around out there.

If you scroll down the jacket’s sold page on the RealReal to the “Related Items” section, you’ll find multiple listings of the Cruise 2005 Chanel jacket Andy Sachs famously wore in The Devil Wears Prada, priced at roughly ten grand. “Similar style seen on Anne Hathaway in 'The Devil Wears Prada,'” the description reads. “We are seeing more and more archival pieces with incredible cultural provenance come in on consignment,” says Kristen Naiman, chief brand officer at the RealReal. “And we love helping these shards of fashion history resurface in pop culture.”

It’s become common practice for major stylists to mine specialty vintage stores for these megawatt, nostalgia-driven red carpet references. Just look at Law Roach’s Instagram account, where he documents pulling 1950s Balenciaga for Kim Kardashian from Lily et Cie, a source that specializes in museum-quality vintage. But as this thought experiment exemplifies, these transactions are becoming more normalized.

Naiman continues: “Whether you know Carolyn Bessette-Kennedy or not, there is now an entire new generation coveting this Yohji Yamamoto look on Hailey Bieber, and it has a renewed desirability because of that. And that’s the fun of being a fashion hobbyist and TRR enthusiast. You not only love the piece, you know the love story behind the piece. It’s a way to participate in fashion history. You can spend years coveting a jacket that has left an indelible mark on culture and then, one day, find it in your size.”

Which brings us back to that Yohji jacket that sold on November 6. We know Bieber wasn’t the one who purchased it, but some lucky person did, and now they own a piece of fashion history. Wonder how many other people have the same one.

#6791. David Corenswet Wore a Cult-Favorite Seiko on the Red Carpet

Superman actor David Corenswet, who’s set to reprise his Man of Steel role in Man of Tomorrow next year, just wore a way-out-of-left-field watch with a tuxedo during the 17th Governors Awards last night. There was no mistaking the glint of its dial from across the step-and-repeat: a Seiko ref. 6139 "Pogue" automatic chronograph. The bulky sport watch looked totally out of place in the restrained, elegant atmosphere of the awards ceremony…and absolutely perfect.

The “Pogue,” as the yellow-dial variant is known, is a wildly cool and beloved watch, and Corenswet smartly took advantage of the way it pops against the black-and-white of a classic tux. Plus, it doesn’t hurt that among Seiko collectors, the Pogue is a cult classic, often ranking towards the top of best-Seiko-ever lists. Not only is it an aesthetic swerve on the red carpet, but it’s the type of relatively affordable oddball celebrities rarely reach for.

What’s so special about the 6139, and why is it called the “Pogue,” you might ask? The 6139 was among the very first batch of automatic chronographs. A group of brands—Seiko; a consortium of Hamilton-Büren, Heuer-Leonaids, Breitling, and Dubois Dépraz; and Zenith-Movado—raced to be the first to debut the mechanical innovation. Who won the race is still a matter of debate, but one thing is for certain: when the 6139 hit the Japanese market in 1969, it featured an integrated design, a column wheel, and a vertical clutch. Meshing hallmarks of classic chronograph construction with the convenience of automatic winding at a relatively affordable price, it was paired with a wide variety of colorful dials and funky cases and produced for roughly a decade.

One of these designs, the ref. 6139-6002, is particularly eye-catching thanks to its bright-yellow dial with a contrasting blue-and-red tachymeter bezel, a 30-minute chronograph register, and a day-date display. American astronaut Col. William R. famously wore the Pogue during the Skylab 4 mission from November 1973 to February 1974. Subsequently, the 6139-6002 took on Pogue’s name, and is now one of the most collectible vintage Seiko references.

The best part? Though there’s unfortunately no magic bullet to make you 6’4” and handsome as Superman, you can snag a pretty good “Pogue” for under $2,000. It may not take you into space, but as Corenswet has proven, it sure livens up your average black-tie outfit.

Though Ezra Sosa and his celeb partner, Julia Stiles, were eliminated after performing a paso doble to “Satisfied” from Hamilton on Dancing With the Stars’ Disney Night, the pro is still keeping up with the competition—and he seemingly called out some shady behind-the-scenes behavior from former DWTS pros.

#6789. These former members are returning to Congress next year

Former Ohio Sen. Sherrod Brown, here at a Oct. 20 campaign event near Ohio University in Athens, won his comeback bid Tuesday. (Tom Williams/CQ Roll Call)

Even in an election cycle marked by surprises and upsets, Capitol Hill observers will spot some familiar faces (identical twins notwithstanding) among this year’s midterm winners.

At least seven former members won comeback bids Tuesday.

The winners included alumni who dusted themselves off after election losses just two years ago. But other winners haven’t been in office in nearly two decades, their service predating the addition of a women’s restroom in the House (more recent than you may guess).

Here’s how these former members did on election night:

Carl lost a primary to fellow Republican Rep. Barry Moore in 2024 after a new map placed them in the same district. But with Moore running for the Senate in 2026, Carl had an easier path back to his old seat, which was redrawn again this year. He defeated his Democratic opponent Tuesday by 18 points.

It has been nearly two decades since Solis last served in Congress. She left in 2009 to serve as President Barack Obama’s first Labor secretary. Solis was in her sixth term as a Los Angeles county supervisor when she announced a bid for Congress after California voters approved a map that created five Democratic pickup opportunities.

Solis faced limited resistance on her path to victory in the 38th District, especially after Democratic Rep. Linda T. Sánchez opted to run for the redrawn 41st District.

Bean is making her way back to the House after losing her suburban Chicagoland seat in the conservative tea party wave of 2010.

A decade-and-a-half later, she won an eight-way primary for the 8th District seat Democrat Raja Krishnamoorthi vacated for an unsuccessful Senate bid. Winning the nomination all but guaranteed Bean a November victory in the deep-blue seat.

One of the cycle’s biggest comeback stories was in the Buckeye State, where Sherrod Brown was mounting a bid two years after Republican Bernie Moreno had unseated him. Positioning himself as an opponent of data centers, Brown defeated appointed GOP Sen. Jon Husted in a special election for the remainder of now Vice President JD Vance’s term. He could take office as early as December but would have to run again in 2028 if he wants another full term.

First elected in 2018, Allred left Congress two years ago after an unsuccessful Senate campaign against Republican incumbent Ted Cruz. After briefly launching a second Senate bid, Allred switched to a redrawn open House seat and won his primary over Rep. Julie Elizabeth Johnson, his successor in the chamber. Allred had no trouble winning his general election Tuesday for a fourth House term.

McAdams served one term in Congress representing a battleground Utah seat, before being unseated by Republican Burgess Owens in 2020. But after a state judge approved a new congressional map that created a heavily Democratic seat anchored in Salt Lake City, McAdams jumped into the race. He comfortably won his primary over more progressive opponents and stomped his way to a second term on Tuesday.

Once a high-ranking member of the House Armed Services Committee, Luria lost her Hampton Roads seat in 2022 to Republican Jen Kiggans.

But a week after Virginia Democrats posted strong showings in the commonwealth’s 2025 off-year elections, Luria launched her House comeback bid and went on to oust Kiggans on Tuesday.

Former House Intelligence Chairman Mike Rogers left the House after the 2014 elections and made his first Senate bid 10 years later. He lost to Democrat Elissa Slotkin but sought the seat of retiring Democratic Sen. Gary Peters the following year.

Rogers tried to take advantage of Democratic divisions between primary winner Abdul El-Sayed and Rep. Haley Stevens, even winning a late endorsement from the congresswoman. But it was not enough to stop El-Sayed on Tuesday.

Republican John E. Sununu served three terms in the House and one in the Senate before losing to Democrat Jeanne Shaheen in 2008. With Shaheen retiring, Sununu attempted a comeback but ended up losing decisively to Democratic Rep. Chris Pappas.

Perriello won an upset victory in a Charlottesville-anchored district in 2008 before losing reelection in the tea party wave two years later. He launched a comeback bid this cycle for the current version of the seat, but the Republican incumbent, John McGuire, narrowly prevailed.

Two years after losing her at-large House seat to Republican Nick Begich, Peltola chose to challenge Republican Sen. Dan Sullivan. The race was rocked by late revelations by The New York Times that top aides to Peltola’s campaign were closely involved in recruiting a second candidate named Dan Sullivan into the race to peel votes from the incumbent. As of Wednesday afternoon, Peltola was clinging to a narrow lead as votes were counted.
