#6788. Nike Made an Air Jordan 1 for Guys Who Love Japanese Denim

Menswear guys love Japanese denim. Menswear guys also love sneakers. So why not make some kicks for the people who can tell you exactly which artisanal mill produced their selvedge? That seems to be the thinking behind the new Social Status x Air Jordan 1 Low OG ‘Sashiko.’

Social Status is a North Carolina-based sneaker and streetwear boutique owned by the Whitaker Group, the same company behind A Ma Maniére. Over the years, it’s worked with Nike on everything from milk-inspired Dunks to Air Pennys. The shop’s last proper Jordan collab was an extremely hairy Air Jordan 6 from 2019, but now the pair are back with something a little easier to wear.

Social Status x Air Jordan 1 Low OG ‘Sashiko’

Social Status’s take on the AJ1 Low starts with the classic ‘Chicago’ color palette. The toebox is white, the overlays are red, and the Swoosh and collar are black. But rather than keeping everything box-fresh, Social Status has roughed up the leather so it arrives pre-scuffed, as though you’ve owned these shoes for years.

The big switch happens across the sidewalls. The usual white leather panels have been replaced with mismatched patches of indigo fabric covered in white sashiko-style stitching. Sashiko is a traditional Japanese embroidery technique used to reinforce, repair, and extend the life of clothing. It’s particularly associated with vintage workwear and denim, which is why these Jordans look like they’ve been mended using a very expensive pair of jeans.

There’s also an old-school nylon tongue, a slightly aged sole, and a custom shoebox wrapped in the same blue pattern.

Social Status x Air Jordan 1 Low OG ‘Sashiko’

While you might normally expect this sort of big Jordan release to land on the High, it’s nice to see the Low silhouette get some love. Some dudes prefer it, after all—it’s less bulky around the ankle and works better under wider trousers. You still get all the bits that make an Air Jordan 1 instantly recognizable, only without half the collar.

The Social Status x Air Jordan 1 Low OG ‘Sashiko’ is set to drop on December 18 for $155 through Social Status, with a wider release via Nike, the SNKRS app, and select stockists expected to arrive later on. Japanese denim guys, get your credit cards ready.

A version of this story originally appeared on British GQ.

#6785. Joseph Zada of The Hunger Games Is Still Getting Used to Being a Movie Star

Joseph Zada is doing “very, very, very good,” he tells me, with a heavy emphasis on the third very and an audible exclamation mark. And why shouldn’t he be?

His much-anticipated film, The Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping, finally comes out this week. Zada and I spoke the morning of his New York City premiere, while he was being shuttled from one press obligation to the next, already go-go-go before actually hitting the red carpet Monday night in custom Louis Vuitton, punctuated with a pair of gold-toed cowboy boots from Pharrell’s first collection for the brand.

In the film, Zada plays a young Haymitch Abernathy, who we follow through his own Hunger Games, some twenty-five years ahead of the events of the original trilogy. When asked about the movie, the 21-year-old Australian demonstrates palpable care and excitement, even a bit of sadness that it’s over. “I just think that it was the most I’ve had to prepare and the most support that I’ve had and the most that I’ve ever stepped into a character’s shoes,” Zada says. “And it was just such a joy to act in this and to feel what it was like to really be this character.”

Since being cast, he’s barely had a second to stop and reflect on joining one of the biggest franchises in the world—and this comes right on the heels of East of Eden, too, in which Zada played another haunted young man. He laughs when I note the comparison: “I guess I just pride myself on my ability to show my vulnerability.”

Below, read our full conversation with the emerging star as he reflects on his Hunger Games journey, talks red carpet style, and shares his thoughts on Robert Pattinson playing James Bond.

Joseph Zada: Yeah, I wonder if I’ll be nervous.

Maybe towards the start. It doesn’t get old… but it gets familiar.

Well, I did SiriusXM this morning, and then, I mean, it’s basically just been like, "Hey, do press all day and then go to your premiere." So there’s not much time for a ritual, so I guess I go a little bit nonverbal and half-dissociate.

No, it’s so much fun. I have a lot of fun at these premieres. It’s always like a crazy, crazy crowd. The way they’ve set it up has just been otherworldly, and I’m not sure if I’ll ever have it again, but I distinctly remember each stop and the feelings that I’ve had thus far, and it doesn’t get old. It’s always crazy to see this many fans and the passion they have for this project. I mean, it’s not every day that you get to step into something with a built-in fan base where people are already coming out and supporting it before they’ve even seen it. So it’s a really special experience, and I don’t know when I’m going to be able to get that again.

Where did I have the most fun? I had a lot of fun in London because my little sister was there, my little sister and my brother’s girlfriend, so I got to hang out with them and I haven’t seen them in a while. And Mexico. Mexico was the craziest event that we’ve had. There were literally thousands, several thousands of people there. The reception was incredible.

Am I allowed to drop this? Oh, yes, that is what this [interview] is for. I’m wearing a Louis Vuitton suit—custom, custom—that Jason [Bolden] and I have worked together on, and I’m a big fan of the boots. The boots are pretty dope.

I feel a lot more comfortable in these clothes nowadays, and it’s actually become a lot more enjoyable to put them on. And it feels like more of an armor and less of a scary thing, and it feels like I build a lot of confidence being able to do this.

My red carpet style, I think... I don’t know. Just whatever Jason puts me in for the night.

Yeah, I think just doing it so often and finding different looks and just being able to wear all the different brands and find what I’m comfortable in.

I pride myself on my ability to have vulnerability. I think as a young man, that’s what I find interesting in these performances of these young men coming of age, and I think that was really what I was just looking for in my early career. I modeled my career on people like Timothée Chalamet and Lucas Hedges, and I love the way that they kept their heart on their sleeve and how they feel like an open nerve. I love being able to portray that in my performances.

I just think that it was the most I’ve had to prepare and the most support that I’ve had and the most that I’ve ever stepped into a character’s shoes and really, really felt like I was there every single day and just had the most incredible support in terms of my cast mates and my directors and my producers. And it was just such a joy to act in this and to feel what it was like to really be this character.

I guess everything just kind of changed just like that. Everything just changed in one quick second, and it feels like... I guess I haven’t really thought about what it was like before. I’ve just kind of been trying to ride the wave. I’m sure being able to go back home for Christmas, I’ll have some time to reflect, but right now it’s just—what’s the day ahead of me? And just making sure I’m ready for all of that.

I’m just excited to be at home every single day and watch movies with my mom and my siblings and see all my friends. That’s what I’m really excited for. I’m just excited to also just turn off my phone for a little bit, maybe go on a little road trip down to South Australia and get a little beach house and not bring my phone and have six days where I’m just off the grid.

That’s a great question. I’ve got Apocalypse Now on 4K Ultra HD on my projector that I bought at home, and I cannot wait. I feel like people ask me what was the best part of my year, and I just start naming all the movies that I’ve watched. And genuinely the happiest that I ever am is when I’m watching a film, which is kind of sad but also kind of cool.

I think... I don’t know. I loved Wild Horse Nine. That’s a favorite I’ve watched in cinemas. I loved Primetime too, because I love Rob Pattinson. I love Skyler Gisondo.

I did just see the Bond casting news! I am chuffed. That is so exciting. That is beyond exciting. He is just the most phenomenal actor, and he really just makes the most interesting choices. And I think him in that role, the stoicism that he’ll bring along with those really interesting choices, it’s going to make that character so interesting. He’s a really special actor. It feels like every young male actor that I speak to feels like that is a person to look to right now and a person’s career to emulate in terms of the risks and the commitment to his craft and all these roles that he’s churning out.

I don’t know. It’s so ridiculously helpful to have a book with you because it’s just so much information, and I love preparing for roles and having a book because it’s just an internal monologue, which is what you should be doing for every character. You read a scene, and you should be writing what is going through your character’s head. And when you have a book, it’s all done for you, or at least you have a good foundation.

I hope I get to do as many book adaptations as possible, but I think what I’m really looking for is just a great director and a great script. I’m not sure what’s [next]. I’ve got a fresh slate right now, but there’s a couple of things floating around.

#6784. Robert Pattinson, I’m Begging You For a Quirked-Up James Bond

Our long international nightmare is finally over: two-time Meme of the Year nominee Robert Pattinson has been chosen as Daniel Craig’s James Bond successor, bringing an end to an exhausting series of “reports” and “leaks” about Amazon’s search for a new 007. Finally we can stop the debates and fan-casting and let Patts and Denis Villeneuve cook until they’re ready to present their new vision. But that would require a level of restraint that the internet doesn’t typically exercise. Immediately after the protracted process of casting Bond concluded, the internet moved on to naming its dream picks for Bond Girls and Bond Villains (sometimes, to brilliant effect.) I can’t say I have a dog in either fight, at least not right now. My Bond WishList is of a different magnitude altogether: can we make James Bond silly again? Just a little, even, 15 percent goofball?

In the hands of another (read: more limited) actor, I’d never suggest such a risky swerve. But the best thing about Pattinson is his range. Sure, if Villeneuve and co. want to render Bond as the stoic and taciturn gentleman he’s generally been this century, Rob has proven he can do that, as every Tenet truther has been gleefully preaching for the last 24 hours. But more often than not, Pattinson is keen to get weird, whether that’s going Full Freak, as he does in Primetime, or approaching certain archetypal parts with a silly, goofy mood (like his conniving suitor in The Odyssey). He can do a “voice” (Mickey 17), he can do scumbag grime (Good Time), he can do hapless screwball (The Drama). Why not make what’ll be his biggest role to date a little cocktail of all his best elements? (Okay, he can leave the Mickey 17 voice behind.)

20 years ago, Casino Royale was a refreshing reset and a crucial corrective to the overflowing ridiculousness of the Piece Brosnan era. But in the past two decades, the pendulum has swung back—the gritty reboot has become studios' stock-in-trade. I feel the same way about Batman; the actual risky choice would be to make something less like Nolan’s series that, instead, hewed a little closer to, say, Batman Forever, or even the gothic camp of Tim Burton’s two films.

Of course, Robert Pattinson was at the center of a Batman reboot as well, and the result was a movie that felt more like a spiritual cousin to David Fincher’s Se7en; audiences ate it up so much that the hunger for a sequel persists even after endless delays. When it comes to franchises as high-stakes as Batman or Bond, you can’t trust these increasingly sauceless execs to make anything but the basic choice. There’s no reason to expect Jeff Bezos isn’t texting Denis screenshots from Skyfall for reference from his megayacht at this very moment.

But if there’s anybody capable of putting a little English on the handle while still giving the mainstream what it wants, it’s Big Bob Pattinson. He could easily make his James Bond a little off, or he could go full seductive cad who smiles like Neil Tenet, has a ki with Q, and charms women with some playful banter instead of simply brooding them into submission. I’m not looking for invisible cars and sharks with lasers, or a Lothario like Roger Moore who speaks in puns. All I’m asking is that we give camp a chance. Because ultimately, the most exciting thing Villeneuve and Pattinson can do to set their Bond apart would be to loosen him up a little.

#6780. Meet the history-makers of the 120th Congress

Democrat Annie Andrews, here addressing supporters at her election night party in Charleston, S.C., on Tuesday, is the first woman elected to the Senate from South Carolina. (Bill Clark/CQ Roll Call) (Bill Clark CQ Roll Call)

The incoming freshman class of the 120th Congress includes several people who scored historic wins in their states and the nation at large.

Among them were women breaking glass ceilings in the Senate and trailblazers for Native American representation. Beyond Washington, a handful of nominees pulled off milestone victories in gubernatorial elections, including four Black candidates. Congress is also poised to witness history next year with New York Democrat Hakeem Jeffries expected to become the first Black speaker.

Here are some of the candidates who made history on election night, both in Congress and in the governor's mansions:

Alfonso, the son-in-law of Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy, is the first Gen Z Republican elected to Congress with his victory in Wisconsin’s open 7th District, a seat his father-in-law once held.

Andrews, a pediatrician, becomes the first woman elected to the Senate from South Carolina after defeating appointed Republican incumbent Darline Graham, the first female senator from the Palmetto State.

With her victory in New York’s 13th District, Avila Chevalier becomes the first Muslim person elected to Congress from the Empire State. She succeeds Democratic Rep. Adriano Espaillat, whom she defeated in the primary.

Benitez-Thompson, a former majority leader of the Nevada State Assembly, is the first Democrat elected to represent the state’s 2nd District since its creation after the 1980 census.

With his victory over Republican Sen. Cindy Hyde-Smith, Colom, a district attorney, will be Mississippi’s first Black senator since Reconstruction. He will also be the Magnolia State’s first Black senator elected directly by voters.

Conely, a Navy veteran, unseats Republican Rep. Mike Lawler to become the first openly gay woman elected to Congress from New York.

Dandiya, the daughter of Indian immigrants, becomes the first South Asian American elected to Congress from Florida with her victory in the redrawn 22nd District. She is also one of the first adherents of Jainism to be elected to Congress, along with Arizona's Amish Shah (see below).

With his victory over Republican Mike Rogers, El-Sayed, the son of Egyptian immigrants, becomes the first Muslim person elected to the Senate.

A member of the White Earth Band of Ojibwe, Flanagan becomes the first Native American woman elected to the Senate with her victory over Republican Michele Tafoya. The outgoing Minnesota lieutenant governor is also the first Native American to represent the state in Congress.

Ford, a state senator, unseated Republican Rep. Victoria Spartz to become the first openly gay person elected to Congress from Indiana.

Hamawy, a former Army surgeon who emigrated from Egypt to the U.S. as an infant, is the first Muslim person elected to Congress from New Jersey, with his victory in the state’s 12th District.

Killin, an Army veteran and onetime chief of staff to former second gentleman Doug Emhoff, is the first Democrat elected to represent Colorado’s 5th District since its creation after the 1970 census.

Kiros, who upset Rep. Diana DeGette in the Democratic primary for Colorado’s 1st District, easily won the general election to become the first Black woman elected to Congress from the Centennial State. Kiros, who was born in Addis Ababa, Ethiopia, and emigrated to the U.S. as an infant, is also the first Gen Z woman elected to Congress.

Koh, the son of a Korean American father and a Lebanese American mother, is the first Asian American elected to Congress from Massachusetts. The former Biden administration alumnus will succeed Democrat Seth Moulton in the state’s 6th District.

Letlow, the first Republican woman elected to the House from Louisiana, earns the same distinction in the Senate with her victory over Democrat Jamie Davis in the open-seat race to succeed GOP Sen. Bill Cassidy.

Limtiaco will be the first Chamorro woman to represent Guam in Congress after she unseated Republican Del. James C. Moylan. She previously was the first woman to serve as U.S. attorney for the Districts of Guam and the Northern Mariana Islands.

McCann, a state senator, defeated Republican Rep. Bill Huizenga to become the first Native American elected to Congress from Michigan. McCann is a member of the Citizen Potawatomi Nation.

Moss, the president pro tempore of the Michigan Senate, keeps Rep. Haley Stevens’ 11th District in Democratic hands, becoming the first openly gay person elected to Congress from Michigan.

The grandson of a Mexican immigrant, Ojeda, who defeated National Republican Congressional Committee Chairman Richard Hudson, is the first Latino elected to Congress from North Carolina.

By defeating former Sen. John E. Sununu, Pappas, a fourth-term congressman, becomes the first openly gay man elected to the Senate.

The daughter of immigrants from Chile and Cuba, Powell is the first Latino person elected to Congress from Nebraska after flipping the state’s 2nd District. She is also the first Democratic woman elected to Congress from Nebraska.

Unofficial returns show Sarauw winning the open-seat race to succeed outgoing Del. Stacey Plaskett, making her the first openly gay person elected to Congress from the U.S. Virgin Islands.

The son of Indian immigrants, Shah is the first South Asian American elected to Congress from Arizona. The former state legislator is also one of the first adherents of Jainism to be elected to Congress, along with Florida’s Pia Dandiya.

A citizen of the Ysleta del Sur Pueblo Nation, Valdez is the first Native American elected to Congress from New York. The New York State assemblywoman will succeed retiring Democratic Rep. Nydia M. Velázquez.

Wiener, a state senator who succeeds former Speaker Nancy Pelosi in California’s 11th District, is the first openly gay person elected to represent San Francisco in Congress.

With her victory over Republican Vivek Ramaswamy, Acton becomes the first woman elected governor of Ohio. Republican Nancy Hollister briefly served as the state’s top executive in the late 1990s but was never elected governor.

The son of Mexican immigrants, Becerra, a former congressman and Health and Human Services secretary, is the first Latino to be elected governor of California. He is the state’s second Latino governor after Romualdo Pacheco, who was never elected to the role but briefly succeeded an incumbent in 1875.

Blackburn, Tennessee’s first female senator, will soon become the Volunteer State’s first female governor after her victory over Democrat Jerri Green.

Crowley, the Milwaukee County executive, will be the first Black person elected governor of Wisconsin after he defeated Republican Rep. Tom Tiffany.

Republican Rep. Byron Donalds will become Florida's first Black governor after he defeated Democrat David Jolly, a former GOP congressman.

Ford, the current Nevada attorney general, will become his state’s first Black governor after defeating Republican incumbent Joe Lombardo.

Haaland, a member of the Pueblo of Laguna, will become the first Native American woman to be elected governor in the U.S. after defeating Republican Gregg Hull. The former Interior secretary was also one of the first Native American women elected to Congress, along with Kansas Rep. Sharice Davids, in 2018.

Hinojosa, a state representative, will become the first Hispanic person elected Texas governor since statehood after she unseated three-term Republican incumbent Greg Abbott.

Klobuchar, the first woman to be elected senator from Minnesota, makes history again with her election as her state’s first female governor.

With her victory over Republican businessman Rick Jackson, Lance Bottoms becomes the first woman to be elected governor of Georgia. The former Atlanta mayor is also the state’s first Black governor and the first Black woman to be elected governor anywhere in the country, along with Del. Stacey Plaskett in the U.S. Virgin Islands.

Unofficial returns show Plaskett, the nonvoting delegate from the U.S. Virgin Islands, becoming the first woman to be elected governor of the island territory. She is also one of the first Black women to be elected governor in the country, along with Lance Bottoms in Georgia.

#6779. Upgrade Your Basics: Best Undershirts for Men in 2026

Just because you don't see a building's foundation doesn't mean it isn't holding everything up. In much the same way, the best undershirts for men are the humble base layer of countless great outfits. Many GQ staffers, in fact, wear-test theirs daily, and yet they're often overlooked, even though they've long-perfected their role: to be the protective layer between your gross body and your steezy, attention-hungry shirts and polos.

And while they're certainly good for some added warmth in the winter, there's a common misconception that they can make the wearer too hot. In fact, lightweight, moisture-wicking undershirts made from fabrics like modal, or cotton blends can actually transfer the sweat away from your body, speeding up your cooling down. All that's to say: Undershirts are more than just men’s underwear. They’re the cornerstone of a hall-of-fame fit.

An undershirt is an undershirt is an undershirt. If you want the most straightforward version, the kind that you’re picturing in your head is most likely from Calvin Klein. It’s a classic crewneck undershirt that’s made of a lightweight jersey that’s 100% cotton which has a smooth, very slightly brushed hand that feels worn-in in the best way possible. The slim fit won’t cause any weird bunching or bulging and it’s got enough length to stay tucked even when you’re out on the town cutting rugs.

My colleague, GQ senior commerce editor Avidan Grossman, has been wearing CK's genre-defining undershirts since his high school years. (He could do without the logo at the hem, but has made his peace with it, given that you never see it once it's tucked.) Otherwise, he prizes Calvin Klein's riff for its simple, pared-down design and blessed lack of modern-day frills. After all, not every shirt needs to moisture wick or odor block. If you just want an undershirt that gets the job done, don't overthink it.

Sometimes, sleeves are just too much undershirt. That’s where tanks come in. Also known as A-shirts, the humble, sleeveless tank has been rocked by every heartthrob and neighborhood grandpa as far back as anyone can remember. And while the low neck and lack of sleeves means you’ll practically never see them underneath a button-up, the irony is that they look great shown off with an unbuttoned camp shirt or on their own.

For the money, we think Hanes takes the cake with their multipack of undershirts. The stretchy ribbed jersey is made of 100% cotton and fits snug with a low neck that’s slightly squared off compared to most tanks. They may cost just under twenty bucks for a pack of six, but they look like a million bucks. “I’ve had buyer's remorse over many a menswear garment over the years,” explains GQ editor Jordan Bowman, "but the one thing that has never let me down is the humble tank top from Hanes. They nailed the fit and the material, and the great thing is, you can buy a pack just about anywhere."

If every part of your wardrobe has to be fit for a king, down to the most basics of basics, then CDLP is what we’d recommend, my liege. The reality is that most luxury tees don’t offer much more than the regular stuff. It’s diminishing returns after a certain price point, but CDLP is the notable exception. My colleague, our contributing editor Louis Cheslaw, has put his same CDLP cottons through the ringer year after year, and says they still haven't lost their luster.

It’s a pricey undershirt, for sure, but the high-quality fabric has a silky hand and lightweight feel that’s unparalleled. (It really feels like you’re almost wearing nothing.) Thanks to a wider neckline, you’re a lot less likely to see the tee poking out, too. And if you feel like such a nice T-shirt is wasted as underwear, don’t worry—the cut and shorter length means it looks great untucked, and can double as your regular T-shirt too.

Mack Weldon’s silver series of garments is what happens when you get nerdy about chemistry and fashion (in the best possible way). The Ionic+ technology embeds silver charged silver ions which are naturally antimicrobial into the fabric itself. Essentially, that means bacteria and odor is neutralized, which means you can take it easy on the deodorant. Beyond that, the tees are made of super long staple Supima cotton and a smidge of lycra for an extra silky fee, comfortable stretch, and long-lasting wear.

The main bummer is that they’ll cost you more than most tees, so stocking up for the week can get pricey. Still, it’s one of those undershirts that makes you realize you’ve been settling for squalor—plus, you can bundle up and get 10% off to help offset the sticker shock.

Not everything at Uniqlo is a great value. But when it comes to upgraded basics at an affordable price, you’d be hard-pressed to find brand that does it better. When you need an airy, breathable undershirt that's truly lightweight, the Japanese mega-retailer’s AIRism tees are legendary. They’re crafted from a smooth and breathable cotton-polyester-spandex blend that dries quickly, feels cool to the touch, and fights odors—a triple threat that’ll have sweaty days in a headlock. I personally wear these—they're a failsafe way to save my expensive dress shirts from dreaded pit stains, but they’re also perfect for wearing as a gym shirt. And at just $15 per tee, there’s no other undershirt that can do as much for the price.

GQ contributor Evan Malachosky was always a little skeptical of Skims, but the brand's tees won him over. He found that Skims’s proprietary fabrics are silky on the skin, and are neither too thin (and therefore exposing), nor too thick (and therefore too hot). Pleasingly, the brand has applied just as much care to its undershirt, even though—or perhaps because—it's designed to look invisible under your clothes. (The design team even thought about cleaning up the seam lines, so nothing would show through.) Made from 100% cotton, and available in an incredibly wide range of sizes, our favorite detail is that it's a little bit longer than the brand's standard tees, so that there's no risk of any unsightly seams being visible at the hemline, either.

If the purpose of an undershirt is to fly under the radar, then it stands to reason that a V-neck one—which remains invisible even when you unbutton your shirt collar—might be the most efficient of all the styles here. You'll still want to trust the classic makers here, which is why our favorite comes from Calvin Klein. Like the Calvin Klein crewneck option above, the fabric is smooth, slightly brushed, and 100% cotton. It's also affordable, and available in not just white, black, and gray, but also a multipack comprising all three. Lastly, CK's trademark slimmer fit means no bunching or bulging risk.

Are these “undershirts” far heavier than the usual ones? Yes. But they never lose their shape, and if you live in a bitterly cold climate, you'll want even your undershirt to max out its warming properties.

The luxury car of undershirts, Falke's premium undershirts go on just as smoothly (and last just as long) as the beloved German brand's famous socks.

You heard it here first: Ralph Lauren's Pony has become the coolest label in menswear again.

When James Bond—well, Daniel Craig as James Bond—needs an undershirt, he wears Sunspel's.

Kirkland Signature is somewhat of a fashion industry secret. These are made of soft, long-staple Egyptian cotton, and they're tube knit, meaning you don't have to worry about any uncomfortable side seams.

Made from rayon and polyester, Hanes’ moisture-wicking undershirts are fast-drying, cool to the touch, and silky smooth, all for just $5 a pop.

If you wanna let your clavicles shine uninhibited by any undershirt peek-age, a V-neck is the best way (short of a tank) to do it.

Cut from a premium cotton, this lightweight undershirt is going to feel a little thicker and warmer than others on this list, purely because Reigning Champ's premium cotton fabrics tend to lean towards the beefier end of the spectrum.

Though Mack Weldon’s 18-Hour Rib Tank doesn’t come with the microbe-smashing odor technology as their Silver crew neck undershirt, it does have an even silkier fabric and plenty of stretch.

The first thing I noticed when I met Wythe's founder was not his jeans, or amazing western shirt. It was actually this ribbed tank.

Undershirts are meant to be that intermediate layer between your outermost layers and your body, a protective guard that keeps your star garments fresh and free of sweat. Therefore, it's materials, construction, and fit that are the main components that determine how good an undershirt (and, well, every garment) actually is.

Above all else, your undershirt's fabric should be breathable, soft, comfortable, and, ideally, sweat-wicking. We primarily love undershirts and tanks that are made from high-quality 100% cotton, which has many of those characteristics naturally, but we also feel like there’s a time and place for synthetics, which are often used for their ability to wick away moisture and dry easily—especially for those who run particularly hot. And note: Stretchy materials like Spandex are helpful for comfort, but their elasticity will eventually degrade over time.

Undershirts should almost always fit closer to the body than your regular T-shirts. This is because they’ll be layered underneath another shirt or sweater, and can bunch in odd ways if too baggy. They don’t need to be skin-tight, but they should be slim enough to layer comfortably and long enough to tuck into your shirt. In fact, Peter Middleton, founder of Wythe, even told us that they should fit slim to start, since they are cotton and are knit, and will therefore stretch a little over time. If they are loose to start with, they are only going to become looser and saggier, which is really not the point.

Style is subjective, we know—that’s the fun of it. But we’re serious about helping our audience get dressed. Whether it’s the best white sneakers, the flyest affordable suits, or the need-to-know menswear drops of the week, GQ Recommends’ perspective is built on years of hands-on experience, an insider awareness of what’s in and what’s next, and a mission to find the best version of everything out there, at every price point.

Our staffers aren’t able to try on every single piece of clothing you read about on GQ.com (fashion moves fast these days), but we have an intimate knowledge of each brand’s strengths and know the hallmarks of quality clothing—from materials and sourcing, to craftsmanship, to sustainability efforts that aren’t just greenwashing. GQ Recommends heavily emphasizes our own editorial experience with those brands, how they make their clothes, and how those clothes have been reviewed by customers. Bottom line: GQ wouldn’t tell you to wear it if we wouldn’t.

We make every effort to cast as wide of a net as possible, with an eye on identifying the best options across three key categories: quality, fit, and price.

To kick off the process, we enlist the GQ Recommends braintrust to vote on our contenders. Some of the folks involved have worked in retail, slinging clothes to the masses; others have toiled for small-batch menswear labels; all spend way too much time thinking about what hangs in their closets.

We lean on that collective experience to guide our search, culling a mix of household names, indie favorites, and the artisanal imprints on the bleeding-edge of the genre. Then we narrow down the assortment to the picks that scored the highest across quality, fit, and price.

Across the majority of our buying guides, our team boasts firsthand experience with the bulk of our selects, but a handful are totally new to us. So after several months of intense debate, we tally the votes, collate the anecdotal evidence, and emerge with a list of what we believe to be the absolute best of the category right now, from the tried-and-true stalwarts to the modern disruptors, the affordable beaters to the wildly expensive (but wildly worth-it) designer riffs.

Whatever your preferences, whatever your style, there's bound to be a superlative version on this list for you. (Read more about GQ's testing process here.)

#6777. Chocolate Strawberry Nails Are the Moody-Sweet Manicure Trend to Try Next

Think mocha, pinky brown, and deep berry tones with a just-dipped shine.

We’ve had espresso nails, almond milk nails, and now we’re savoring chocolate strawberry nails. So what exactly are they? Think of anything in the family of mocha shades, pinky browns, and deep berry tones. It’s the color you get when a ripe strawberry takes a dip in melted chocolate.

The look works because it balances warm and cool. The rich brown base keeps things grounded and neutral, while the berry undertones add a soft flush of color that feels fresh rather than flat. It’s a shade that flatters nearly every skin tone, and it’s subtle enough for the office yet moody enough for a night out. For an extra touch, try a glossy top coat to mimic that just-dipped chocolate shine, or add a thin chrome or sheer pink accent nail for a little dimension.

Below, we’ve rounded up some fun designs to get you inspired for your next chocolate strawberry manicure.

Like a strawberry dipped fully in chocolate, this dark shade hides a subtle red glow that peeks through almost like a cat-eye effect.

This dark berry shade has a high-gloss, mirror-like shine, just like a strawberry fresh from a dip in silky milk chocolate.

From dreamy cotton-candy pink to a deep, jammy berry, these rosy shades capture every stage of a juicy strawberry, from first blush to fully ripe.

This nail set looks like creamy milk chocolate glazed under a frosty chrome finish.

The silver studs on this rich berry shade mimic a strawberry’s tiny seeds, with a glam twist.

These short nails are neatly filed and glossed to perfection. The rounded shape keeps them practical for everyday wear, while the mirror-bright finish gives them a polished, just-left-the-salon look.

This mani pairs a lustrous chocolate base with a sweet pink French tip. The soft pink line adds a playful pop against the deep brown, giving the classic French a fresh, dessert-inspired twist.

Soft pink polka dots float across a deep burgundy base, a playful nod to retro charm with a ripe, berry-rich finish.

This manicure features DND’s Strawberry Wine, a moody fall shade that’s hard to beat.

Another DND color, Fudgsicle, is a rich chocolate brown with a hint of red, the ideal base for chocolate strawberry nails.

Like real strawberries, the black dots melt into the base so completely they’re nearly invisible, until a closer look reveals the quiet detail hiding in plain sight.

#6775. Grand Theft Auto VI Review: Better Than the Hype

There are moments in GTA 6 that’ll have you levitating off your sofa. Unprecedented in its scope and production, it was created by a team of almost 5,000 developers over nearly 10 years. Its budget is rumored to rival the construction of Dubai’s Burj Khalifa skyscraper; its first trailer set records with 93 million views in a day. Every social media brand account, from the White House to BMW, has jumped on the hype, while economists predict a national sick day as millions play hooky to game. When you do, you see the time, talent and flair on the screen. But, at its heart, Grand Theft Auto VI follows in series tradition: an epic American crime story, set in a stunning parody of the US that riffs on film, music, politics, style and satire.

Fronting the first franchise entry in more than 13 years are Jason and Lucia. Developer Rockstar attempted a multi-character arc in Grand Theft Auto V, but those three leads learned to loathe one another. Instead, VI tackles love, a game design challenge most developers have sworn off with good reason. Not a parent/child relationship like The Last of Us or God of War, but a mature, romantic, adult one. You can call or text each other at pretty much any point—to do missions together, commit robberies, explore, meditate, work out, fly, or just to coexist under the Vice City sunshine.

This isn’t some kind of ChatGPT fantasy to indulge in. Neither Jason or Lucia exists at your beck and call as some kind of hyper-realistic plaything. You have a role in their relationship, both in the way they speak to each other and where the story takes them. You can cruise down the Vice City waterfront together in your souped-up ’70s muscle wagon with one of you driving, the other scrolling through your Snapmatic feed (the in-game social media channel), before grabbing a drink and carving up the sidewalk with an assault rifle five minutes later. You just don’t get these detours in Call of Duty.

More than a car: in GTA VI, your vehicle is a mobile base of operations to stash your guns, fits, money, and drugs.

It’s a different pace from previous Grand Theft Autos too. Rockstar changed its dynamics with 2018’s Red Dead Redemption 2—a more thoughtful take on the outlaw tale that added friction to the action and a conscience to the narrative. For a developer that once experienced the spotlight of US Congress during 2005’s infamous “Hot Coffee" controversy (when a sexually explicit mini-game meant as an inside joke between developers was figured out by savvy users), this kind of nuance was revelatory. With VI, Rockstar takes Red Dead 2’s ethos and applies it to a modern city with 1,000 x the population. The result is staggering, but still an absolute blast to play. That is to say, the greatest hits of GTA—driving cars, shooting guns, and stealing money, drugs and more (all to spend on a better Beretta, G-Wagon replica, and impeccably modeled Air Jordan dupes)—but with added depth to how you play. Weapon slots are finite; you can no longer run amok with an entire arsenal stashed in your jacket pocket. Money is vulnerable; get killed or caught with cash in your possession, and you’ll lose it. And if you jack a car that’s fitted with a tracker, the police will find you quickly.

Should you get into a bust-up with the law after a jewelry store smash-and-grab gone wrong, the game’s action is frenetic. Roads are much narrower, traffic is denser, and you can’t see police on your minimap. That $40,000 in cash sitting on the back seat had better be worth the hassle. As such, the game supports your decision to play chaotically, but truly immerses you in playing methodically. Jason and Lucia each have a “criminal profile”—be unnecessarily violent and you’ll get a “psycho” rating—that adds replayability and roleplay. Still, I had the most fun when planning missions: using a silenced pistol, shooting to maim not kill, and wiping security footage to leave no trace. It’s the closest GTA has honed in on the art of crime. The feds will surround the store you’re robbing. “Come out right now with your hands above your head!” Do things right, and you’ll have prepped your partner with a getaway car out back. You’ll leave the scene anonymously as backup blares past you on the other side of the road.

Every club, pub and dingy dive bar in GTA 6’s Leonida has a distinct vibe—from the music to the decor to the sweaty Floridians inside.

The game kicks off with an electrifying setup accompanied by an original song from composers Justice. It perfectly sets the tone: VI has the ’80s flamboyance of Vice City made new and fresh. It takes its opening hours steadily, sending you on odd jobs to make a little money here and there. Gradually—then suddenly—you’re thrust into the most high-octane campaign in Grand Theft Auto history. Tense hospital escapes, daring bank heists, cross-country chases, and more. Its set pieces are balls-to-the-wall bravado, rivaling what you’d see in Nolan’s The Odyssey or Villeneuve’s Dune, upping in intensity through its chapters. Tonally, it’s the most “of the now” Grand Theft Auto game yet, as your two in-love criminals race to find a better life in an unfair world. No previous GTA has given you a savings account—it’s like playing the millennial curse. The only dings are some pacing in the game’s second chapter as the game wrestles with its story while still introducing mechanics. Think of it as VI’s version of RDR2’s Guarma, albeit with bargain bin stores and grimy strip malls instead of exotic Caribbean fauna.

And then to Leonida, with which Rockstar has created the most technically and artistically impressive game world ever made. The pre-release trailers promised beaches bustling with vacationers and nightclubs packed with dancers. That is the reality. From sea to sand to sky, the world is alive. Vice City alone could be an entire game in its own right, complete with distinct districts, an explorable international airport, and more. Yet, Rockstar spoils us with a massive expanse of varied towns, high-class beach resorts, lush green national parks, murky swamplands and sun-kissed island keys. All packed with people that range from useful, to funny, to completely unsavory (shout-out to the old woman who took one look at my Jason and said “I’mma take my teeth out and suck the skin off that dick”). The density and fidelity of it all often stretches the limits of even Sony’s PlayStation 5 Pro. Frame rate dips don’t spoil gameplay, but the slowdown is noticeable when you’ve punted a couple of grenades into passing cars to set off multiple explosions at once.

NPCs don’t just feel like backdrop mannequins anymore, but real people with an incredible range of faces, body types and outfits.

Every frame a photograph. Stop anywhere in GTA 6, and you’ll likely find something enticing to see, hear or interact with.

As with Red Dead Redemption 2, the most impressive of the game’s many achievements is how everything seems to live on without you. All it took to make this world was Rockstar founding an entire studio to work solely on Leonida’s population. I’ve seen a woman throw herself out of a cop car window as I scooted along Ocean Beach, and car thieves burst through the glass windows of a car dealership while the alarms howl. I saw a furry taken down by armed police, staged perfectly with a helicopter spotlight. During one 3 a.m. wander through the rough neighborhood of Crosstown, a man approached me to warn me of the local gang and tells me they have a nearby base. I accidentally tapped the button that brings up your in-game phone. “Ohhhh! You don’t just believe what I say” the man says, responding to my actions. “You do your own research!” In more than 70 hours of play, I have seen only one moment of repetition.

There is so much more besides. A cast of interesting, funny and believable characters; off-the-beaten-path secrets that can lead to side quests both big and small; perhaps the best radio track list of any GTA spanning from Travis Scott to Lana Del Rey; podcast episodes; golf to play; gators to hunt; dozens of supercars to steal and customize; weapons that feel punchy and satisfying; multiple racing series; a litany of actually interesting shops and bars and clubs to explore and buy things in. I’ve seen brilliant horror vignettes, startling science fiction, and laugh-out-loud comedy just by exploring. Rockstar has created a world that rewards you for spending time in it. Expect to spend 100 hours if you want to see everything.

As you’ll likely have seen and heard—in everything from Miami Heat takeovers, to Netflix launches, to Selfridges pop-ups—that this is the most-wanted entertainment property in the world. In a way, all it had to be was Grand Theft Auto V: The Second. But, on the largest and most scrutinized stage imaginable, Rockstar takes creative risks with its story, protagonists, and mechanics, and makes big leaps in what games can be. It’s unlikely other developers will catch up soon. In 2002, Grand Theft Auto: Vice City reshaped and refashioned pop culture to give us a playable version of a place and a time we already knew. A quarter of a century later, VI shows Rockstar at the peak of entertainment in its very own right.

#6774. Kyle MacLachlan and Michelle Zauner Celebrate the Power of the Written Word at the National Book Awards

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

The evening’s nominees included authors in fiction, nonfiction, and poetry, among others

The 77th National Book Awards opened with a song. Japanese Breakfast’s Michelle Zauner, also the author of the best-selling Crying at H Mart, walked onto the stage to serenade the crowd with her loving ballad “Be Sweet.” She then welcomed the evening’s host, Kyle MacLachlan, who did a theatrical reading of the children’s tale Paddle-to-the-Sea by Holling C. Holling. The actor, who just recently published a memoir, Fictional Selves, shared tales of his first library card, his now-ironic childhood obsession with Frank Herbert’s Dune (he’d go on to star in David Lynch’s ’80s adaptation), and the beauty of reading stories to his children—the last being of the utmost importance.

Two Lifetime Achievement Awards were handed out first. Perri Klass, the medical director of Reach Out and Read, received the Literarian Award for Outstanding Service to the American Literary Community. “[Children] need to grow up with their bodies and their brains cherished and protected,” she said to the crowd after taking the stage with a stethoscope and a copy of Goodnight Moon. “They need to grow up properly nourished. Their minds and their bodies.” Next, Salman Rushdie, the 79-year-old Indian-British writer who has devoted his life to fighting censorship after several of his books were banned in multiple countries, received the National Book Foundation’s Medal for Distinguished Contribution to American Letters.

“This boy is born in Bombay at the end of the British Empire—79 years ago—born just before the freedom that came at midnight, and that same boy improbably ends up halfway around the world on a stage in New York City with this medal dangling from his neck,” Rushdie said in his acceptance speech. “If that's not magic realism, I don't know what is. I have given my life to the art of words, to the creation of books and to their defense, to the exercise of artistic freedom, and to an insistence of the importance of that freedom.”

David Levithan and Gabriel Duckels’s The Fight of Our Lives: AIDS in America, a nonfiction book tracing the history of the AIDS epidemic and the first grassroots movements, was the recipient of the Young People’s Literature Award. “Our aim with this book has been to write a history of HIV and AIDS that didn’t oversimplify or generalize, that centers HIV and AIDS as not just part of the past but part of the present, an epidemic whose legacy young people all deserve to understand,” Duckels said in his acceptance speech. “We will not let the noise of others drown out the music of who we are,” Levithan added, highlighting the need to protest trans and nonbinary youth.

The award for best nonfiction went to Huna: A Memoir of Revolution, Prison, and Becoming by the Egyptian author Abdelrahman ElGendy, which his editor, Helen Thomaides, accepted after ElGendy was denied entry into the country. The memoir details his six years in a political prison after being arrested during the 2013 military coup in Egypt. “I used to fantasize about writing a memoir,” Thomaides read from ElGendy’s note. “I would slip under a bright-red duvet. My mother had bribed the guards to let through and imagine that everything happening to me would one day end up in a book.”

The night concluded with the fiction award, given to Hannah Lillith Assadi for Paradiso 17, which follows a Palestinian man’s lifelong search for home. “I’ve watched cancer ravage my father's body, and genocide ravaged his people and their children,” Assadi said in her acceptance speech. “Perhaps this is the Palestinian story on this earth. So beautiful, so painful. And isn’t that story all of ours? Maybe that is why we long for that land, because it reflects the beauty and pain in us.”

In an evening dominated by the heavy and relentless state of the world, MacLachlan brought levity, sprinkling in jokes about dating Laura Dern (“If you ask Claude what it’s like to date Laura Dern, it’s almost exactly my words”), criticizing censorship and AI (“We need to stick together, especially in what feels like a never-ending fight against censorship in our libraries, in our schools. Every time you think the battle is won, it pops up again. It’s a war out there, and every one of you here tonight is on the front line.”) and noted how new tech problems place intense pressure on the publishing industry, adding, “No wonder you’re drinking so heavily!” to uproarous laughter.

But the spirit of the evening was best captured by The Invisible Mountain author Caro De Robertis, who chaired the fiction category, as she introduced the award. “From this vantage point, U.S. literature is alive and radiant and thriving,” she said. “Do not let anybody tell you different. Books are essential, and they aren’t going anywhere.”

#6773. How Celine’s Michael Rider Quietly Seized Fashion’s Attention

You can tell a lot about a designer by the scene backstage after a show. Usually, it’s sweaty, manic, crammed with hangers-on—the fashion equivalent of an airport lounge during a blizzard. But on the unseasonably warm October day of Michael Rider’s most recent Paris runway show, the space was alive with Champagne toasts and bubbly conversation.

The co-ed summer 2027 runway was a blowout party hosted by Rider with models channeling twitchy new-wave dancers, preppy ’80s croquet players, and the heroic DIY divas from Paris Is Burning. Rider showed new tailoring shapes for men, specifically loose, floppy blazers and slim cigarette-leg pants, cropped confidently above the ankle. A showstopping look of gleaming pink separates somehow avoided silliness. Rider’s signature styling took the edge off. A silver shimmering suit was mildly de-glammed by a jacket tied around the waist. A jaunty black hat topped off an outfit with a white long-sleeve shirt and shorts. Improbably, these wild ideas felt coherent—a hard-won sleight of hand.

In the garden of the National Archives Museum, friends of Rider like Hannah Einbinder, Tracee Ellis Ross, and Dan Levy milled around waiting for the designer to finish telling critics about his “intuitive” approach to fashion. Like something out of Lewis Carroll, a spread was unfurled on a long outdoor table. Waiters poured Champagne into a tower of coupes; editors licked homemade popsicles and bit into crunchy sandwiches of jambon-beurre. No one seemed like they had anywhere to be. This was the place to be.

In less than two years, Rider has cleaned the slate following Hedi Slimane’s shrunken, skinny rock era at Celine. He’s brought more accessible, artfully styled fashion to the stores. He’s delivered at least two hit shoes: the dirty white jazz lace-up, and, this past September, an immediately sold-out collaboration with Reebok that reissued the 1982 Freestyle Lo sneaker in decadent leather, in shades from oxblood to buttery yellow.

In the process, his Celine has become a favorite among fashion-minded men. Ebon Moss-Bachrach, the tall Girls and The Bear actor, told me backstage that Rider’s menswear feels “masculine without any kind of footnote.” Youngjae, from the K-pop band TWS, explained the allure of Rider’s vision. “The Celine man is very free,” he said—just as the wild-haired designer burst outside for a smoke, laughing and relieved.

Wearing a standard-issue, backstage-friendly black crewneck sweater, Rider wasn’t exactly channeling the improvisational look of the brand he’s re-engineered in only a handful of shows. He assumed the role of artistic director in 2025, succeeding Slimane. Before taking Celine’s reins, he put in time under Nicolas Ghesquière at Balenciaga (2004–08) and at Phoebe Philo–era Celine (2008–17). But the secret to the Rider Effect—his unique ability to conjure clarity from chaos—was a lesson he picked up during his tenure at Ralph Lauren.

Rider worked for the all-American designer as creative director of women’s Polo Ralph Lauren from 2018 until he decamped for Celine. Early on, he threw himself into staging his first “rig room.” A very Ralph tradition, the rig room is an installation of a fashion collection designed to be presented to Lauren internally—a fully immersive world complete with music, decor, props, and reference photos. Rider worked on his first one incessantly for two months. When Lauren arrived, he looked around in silence while Rider stood beside him, sweating in a navy blazer.

At last, Lauren asked, “Why do you want to confuse people?”

“I think about that a lot,” confesses Rider when we speak in a circular antechamber at Celine’s Paris headquarters a few days after the show. Disappointing Lauren was a turning point for Rider. He had gotten the job designing Polo after 14 years in the upper echelons of European fashion. “Mixing references and confusing people was part of the game,” he remembers. “You elevated yourself and your brand through adjacencies and pastiche. Ralph was the total opposite.”

“I definitely appreciated going back to the States and hearing from a master: Be clear,” says Rider. The ongoing tension of his current work lies in resolving his tendency toward anarchy with a desire for legibility. His Celine is layered and unfettered and fun. But it is also as clear, as the French say, as water on rocks.

Sitting in the same room where he and Philo “dumped our bags and met people” during his first stint at Celine, Rider looks calm and perfectly at home. Now that he’s at the helm of the 81-year-old LVMH maison founded by Céline and Richard Vipiana, the “rotonde” room has a few framed New Yorker cartoons propped on a wicker bench, some personalized thank-you cards to send, a pack of cigs, Evian, and not much else. Twinkly and alert, with no phone in sight, Rider wears a blue button-up, dark khaki pants, black canvas trainers, a gold Cartier watch, and some chunky rings. His hair sticks straight up like one of his inspirations: Isaac Mizrahi.

Mizrahi’s Unzipped documentary, which recently celebrated its 30th anniversary, was one of the references for the summer show. So, too, was another canonic ’90s documentary, Paris Is Burning, about the queer ballroom scene in late-’80s Manhattan. Both films look at the human side of fabulosity, something that interests Rider. Brands are the creations of curious, restless minds, and the best transcend desire, attracting something close to faith. Rider himself has been one of those believers, lusting after Ghesquière’s Balenciaga during his first real job, when he was just an underpaid teacher. (“I would have killed for something from there,” he says.) In his show notes, he wrote about “our desires and frustrations. Memories, moments that made us love fashion. Moments where you get a glimpse of possibility.”

In practice, that looks like something you want to buy, but that has soul. Rider, like Lauren and Mizrahi, is a designer who makes clothing and accessories that feel just familiar enough to strike a nostalgic chord, but fresh enough that they can’t be replaced by trawling The RealReal. In his first Celine collection, shown in July 2025 under a sudden Parisian downpour, the looks bore a healthy pinch of Polo-honed prep: red blazers, diagonal-stripe ties, pressed jeans, loafers. Now that style is officially everywhere, with every middle-class menswear brand and fast-fashion factory churning out their own versions.

What separates Rider’s Celine is that it gets men excited to dress, says Nordstrom men’s fashion director Jian DeLeon: “It triggers that lizard-brain impulse that inspires them to shop. They didn’t realize they wanted a bright-blue military jacket, and then when they see it in store, Rider’s design nuances—like oversized, ruched sleeves and slightly different blocks of color in the fabric—further satisfy the kinds of bells and whistles that men’s fashion enthusiasts nerd out about.”

In his first solo men’s presentation this past January, Rider brought his Ralph-perfected merchandising flair to Celine for a rig-room-esque installation of styling combinations that shouldn’t have worked—like a paisley tie layered beneath a red-string, hippie pendant necklace—and yet, did. Loafers in every shade played to an audience of ’90s kids who lusted after stacks of Benetton sweaters. Army jackets became accessories, belted around waists. It was American mall culture whipped through a blender with ready-to-wear refinement.

Rider’s Celine made an impact even earlier with his very first outing, the co-ed spring 2026 collection, shown in mid-2025. Soon after that range was available, Paul Anthony Kelly, who played nouveau John F. Kennedy Jr. in Ryan Murphy’s hit FX series Love Story, wore a Kennedy-ish Celine look: a white Oxford shirt, red-and-navy striped tie, navy blazer, jeans, white socks, loafers. The message was clear: Kelly had arrived—and so had Rider. Kelly’s stylist, Warren Alfie Baker, says he got hundreds of messages from people saying, “Oh my God, I need to go and buy that.” Why did he think it hit so hard? “What Michael does really well is it feels relatable,” Baker explained. “Everybody wanted to replicate it or have a version of it.”

Baker, who styles classic leading-men types like Glen Powell, Andrew Scott, and Andrew Garfield, says Rider’s work was heating up in Hollywood ahead of the fall awards season. “When you see a runway with 45 looks, and you’re like, ‘I could actually use 40 of them,’ that’s the dream scenario.”

Slimane had introduced menswear to Celine in 2018 for the first time in the brand’s history with skinny black jeans, skinnier black ties, and shrunken leather jackets—an aesthetic driven more by rock-and-roll than red carpets. When he brought the brand to Los Angeles in late 2022 for an 87-look fashion show-cum-concert at the Wiltern, rockers including Iggy Pop, the Strokes, Interpol, and the Kills wore Slimane’s indie-sleaze uniforms to perform. “Celine’s past has been very skinny. Maybe some guys wouldn’t have known that they could wear it,” conceded Baker.

Rider’s Celine offers more elbow room, literally and otherwise. “It’s not such a rigid idea of how a man should dress,” says fashion commentator and stylist Nicky Campbell, who has styled Mark Ronson in several Rider looks, including a custom leather jacket for this year’s Brit Awards. “Other menswear brands are too rigid: They want you to be this really formal, masculine man, or they want you to be super androgynous. He lands in this in-between space.”

Born in 1980 to two do-gooder attorney parents in Washington DC, Rider was raised in an intellectual, progressive household well outside the fashion bubble. Musing on why he’s still drawn to things that are a little off, and the stalls at the flea market with “the mangiest, strangest assortment of clothing,” he says. “My parents are not materialistic in any way. There are nice things around, and they make things. My mom’s an incredible cook, there’s needlepoint, my dad [told] great stories. But it wasn’t a situation where aesthetic perfection was the goal.”

“I was always in my own strange lane,” says Rider. “Everybody who seemed cool to me was on their own path.” His older cousin introduced him to the Grateful Dead, a lifelong love. Rider was, naturally, first drawn to the T-shirts—“the merch always brings me in”—but then became fascinated by the totality of Deadhead culture. “I was very attracted to the experimentation and freedom and fewer rules,” he says. “I like color, I like people who customize things, and I like weirdos.”

The Dead were a gateway into an adolescence full of experimentation. “I had all the phases,” Rider says, from Birkenstocks worn with Thorlo hiking socks, to hip-hop streetwear, to skate style that he finessed in shops like Green Barrel in Georgetown and Stüssy in the suburb of Potomac. He also dabbled in the all-American look he’d later help shape at Polo. “We went to public school and were proud of it, so preppy was a bad word, but everybody at my school worshipped Polo. They just wore it differently, and we mixed it all.”

Rider’s still mixing. “It’s always been a lot of fun for me to get dressed,” he says. When he lived in New York City while at Ralph Lauren, he had standing movie nights at the Battery Park City cinema with the photographer Tommy Ton and designer Trevor Ballin (who works with him now at Celine), both friends. Ton, a street-style savant, loved seeing the outfits Rider would pull off at the cinema, like ankle-chopped Uggs with an Hermès shawl, or Grateful Dead bear–embroidered shorts. Levy says he could see Rider’s own vacation wardrobe in the women’s looks of the summer collection. “He shows up to dinner in caftans and sandals, or harem pants.”

Which can be hard to square with the fact that Rider went to Brown University with the intention of becoming a teacher. He moved to Oakland after graduating and taught ninth grade at a charter school. “Everyone was bigger than me,” says the petite designer. Living in the Bay Area, going out a lot, and experimenting with clothing, he eventually felt a pull to the creative world that he couldn’t shake, which would lead to him interning with his then hero, Ghesquière.

Rider, the former teacher, remains a perpetual student. He tells me, with a glance at his PR minder, that the kind of fashion profiles he loves—like Judith Thurman’s unvarnished 2005 New Yorker story on Rei Kawakubo—aren’t really possible anymore. In the post-Galliano-meltdown, recession-hardened luxury landscape, publicly traded conglomerates protect their creative directors from criticism like the precious assets that they are.

So he painstakingly protects his distance—but doesn’t sacrifice his sense of humor in the process. You’ll see it in the pop of outrageous purple, a woolly outdoorsy fleece, those shimmering suits. There’s a sense that being too serious is just kind of sad. He likes when people talk about Celine as “good-time clothes” that they wear when they go out. “I would be mortified if anyone thought silliness was on the menu,” Rider says. Still, “Things are very funny to us, and that has to be felt somehow. I think humor is really stylish.”

Eons have passed, seemingly, since Rider interned in the menswear department of Balenciaga in the mid-aughts. “Fashion for men was very fringe then,” he says, remembering that the men’s collection at the label totaled maybe a dozen looks. At the time, Slimane’s strict silhouettes for Dior Homme were in; Rider wore some of that, along with the stiff selvedge APC jeans that became part of the ascent of #menswear. He recalls Ghesquière being obsessed at the time with making the ideal men’s wardrobe, from button-ups to parkas.

“Menswear has completely opened up now,” Rider says. “It’s a different ball game. Because it’s so much broader now, and people have phones; people everywhere have access to so much and are engaged in more conversations. Not always for good, but it moves. The rules can move, and the goalposts can move.”

Rider, after an almost Forrest Gump-y run of consistently being at the center of gravity—at Balenciaga, with Philo, and at Ralph Lauren—without having tried so hard to be there, is now the one doing the goalpost-moving. And he has no interest in hard rules or self-inflicted limitations.

“I think a lot of people who do this thing end up living, or trying to live out, the same story over and over,” he says.

He resists sheeplike notions of what’s considered cool—“this artist, this pottery, this lamp, this show space collaboration,” as he puts it. And he’s careful not to follow the flock too closely. “Paris is amazing, but Paris is insular,” says Rider. “Everyone’s eating at the same canteen, gossiping about the same thing. There’s a gigantic world out there.”

So Rider keeps doing his own thing. He revels in American pop culture—he’s just as passionate about going to the multiplex near his weekend home in Normandy to see the latest Wicked as he is paging through those old World of Interiors magazines. He’s started weight-lifting, in the evenings. Learning Arabic is on his bucket list. And often he finds himself going back to his roots for inspiration.

Every summer, he returns to Martha’s Vineyard, where his family has had a house since the 1970s. He loves its isolation, and its realness: “Against all odds, it has remained what it is. There’s no bridge, the airport’s still tiny, 85 percent of it is protected. There’s no Balenciaga.” On Martha’s Vineyard, he likes being out on the water.

“The most American thing about me is that I like to paddle,” he says, laughing. “I’m my best self in a canoe.” ✦

A version of this story originally appeared in the December 2026 issue of GQ with the title “This Quiet American Has Fashion’s Attention.”

#6772. Your Exclusive First Look at the Official 'Cameron Winter at Carnegie' Hall Merch

“I will keep rolling down until my best shirt rolls off,” warbles Cameron Winter on “The Rolling Stone,” the opening track of his 2024 solo debut Heavy Metal. That album later became the basis of the Geese frontman’s historic December 2025 performance at Carnegie Hall, which was filmed and subsequently turned into a captivating concert film by Oscar-winning director Paul Thomas Anderson. A year later, Winter—and perhaps you—will now have a new best shirt to roll off.

PTA’s Cameron Winter at Carnegie Hall, which was shot on 35mm and 16mm film, will hit select theaters nationwide on Friday. As a reward to the Geeseheads and steadfast cinephiles who attend the 35mm screenings, distributor Janus Films produced a limited-edition run of merch (many such cases!) that can only be purchased IRL at select participating theaters. Which is to say, acquiring this merch…takes miles.

But if you’re looking at the Cameron Winter at Carnegie Hall T-shirts ($40) and baseball hats ($35) and thinking, “Wow, those look a lot like the maximalist, erudite wares from Boot Boyz Biz,” that’s because they were in fact designed by two of the Chicago brand’s current outposts, Now-Time and Mike’s Deli.

In an email, the Now-Time/Mike’s Deli team said that they drew inspiration from Carnegie Hall’s history and architecture. “We looked to the building itself as a visual archive to draw from, seeking forms that resonated with the performance across time,” they wrote. “Familiar architectural details took on a new heightened significance, knowing what was happening inside. In Carnegie Hall’s classic horseshoe layout we saw the camera’s panoramic intent. Outside on the 19th-century facade we found typographic elements that paralleled the energy of the event.” A very Winteresque explanation, in a way. Ideal for any enigmatic T-shirt enthusiast.

If you, too, would like to participate in the seeking forms and panoramic intent of Winter’s moving piano stylings on one winter’s night in Manhattan, you better start a-walking to the nearest 35mm-equipped cinema near you.

GQ staffers Jordan Bowman and Gerald Ortiz testing out the merchandise.
